<meta>This is the title page for one of the most controversial philosophical works of the Renaissance. Written by Pietro Pomponazzi (Latinized as Petrus Pomponatius), the "Treatise on the Immortality of the Soul" first appeared in 1516 and sparked intense debate by suggesting that the immortality of the individual soul could not be proven by Aristotelian reason alone, but must be accepted as an article of faith.</meta>

When one day he saw that I was being troubled less by my illness, with a very humble expression he began thus: "Dearest Teacher <note>Pomponazzi was a famous professor of philosophy; "Præceptor" was a standard term of respect for a master of his craft.</note>, in recent—"

## BY PIETRO POMPONAZZI
# OF MANTUA,
## A TREATISE ON THE IMMORTALITY OF THE SOUL.
### PREFACE CONTAINING THE *intent, or the subject matter and the reason for the book's purpose.*

<image-desc>An ornamental woodcut initial 'F' entwined with vine-like foliage and flowers.</image-desc>

Brother Girolamo Natale of Ragusa <note>original: "Hieronymus Natalis Ragusæus"</note> of the Order of Preachers <term>Order of Preachers: Also known as the Dominicans, a Catholic religious order focused on theological study and preaching.</term>, used to visit me quite frequently while I was suffering from poor health. As he is a most kind man and very fond of us, he came to see me; and when one day he observed that I was being troubled less by my illness, with a very humble expression he began to speak in this way: "Dearest Teacher <note>original: "Præceptor"</note>, during the previous—

<summary>This page introduces the context of the treatise, presented as a conversation between the philosopher Pietro Pomponazzi and a Dominican monk named Girolamo Natale during Pomponazzi's illness.</summary>

--- Page 10 ---
<meta>This page continues the conversation between the Dominican friar Girolamo Natale and the philosopher Pietro Pomponazzi. Girolamo is recalling a specific lecture given by Pomponazzi on Aristotle's "De Caelo" (On the Heavens), which serves as the catalyst for the inquiry into the soul's immortality.</meta>

<page-num>4</page-num>
<header>*On Immortality*</header>

days, when you first lectured to us on *The Heavens* <note>original: "de cœlo"; refers to Aristotle’s treatise *De Caelo*, which discusses the nature of the celestial bodies and the physical universe.</note>, and you reached that passage where Aristotle strives to show through many arguments that the "ungenerated" and the "incorruptible" are interchangeable <note>Aristotle argued that if something has no beginning (ungenerated), it can have no end (incorruptible).</note>, you said that although you in no way doubted that Saint Thomas Aquinas’s position on the immortality of souls was true and most certain in itself, you nevertheless judged it to be not at all in agreement with Aristotle’s words. Therefore, unless it would be a burden to you, I would greatly desire to learn two things from you: first, setting aside revelations and miracles and staying purely within natural limits <term>natural limits: A philosophical approach that uses only human reason and the physical laws of nature, excluding religious doctrine or divine revelation.</term> in this matter, what you think; and second, what you judge Aristotle's own opinion on this same subject to have been? And when I saw that everyone standing there—and there were many present—shared this same great desire, I then replied in this way:

> Dearest son, and you others, although you ask no small thing—for this is a most profound matter, since almost all illustrious philosophers have labored over it—nonetheless, since you only ask me for what I am able to provide, namely what I myself think, it is easy for me to reveal this to you. Indeed, I will gladly comply with your wish. However, as to whether the matter truly stands as I think it does, you should consult those who are more learned. Therefore, with God as my guide, I shall begin the task.

## *In which it is shown that man is of a twofold nature, and a middle ground between mortal and immortal things.*

### C H A P T E R I.

I have decided that the starting point of our investigation must be taken from here: namely, that man is not of a simple but of a multiple nature; he is not of a fixed, but of a twofold <note>original: "accipitis," likely a variant of "ancipitis," meaning two-headed, double, or ambiguous.</note> nature, and is placed as a middle ground between mortal and immortal things. This is clearly seen if we examine his essential operations, for it is through these operations that a thing's essence is made known. Insofar as man performs the functions of the vegetative and sensitive <note>In Aristotelian thought, the "vegetative" faculty governs growth and reproduction, while the "sensitive" faculty governs sensation and movement. Both were thought to require physical organs.</note> faculties—which, as is taught in the second book of *On the Soul* <note>original: "2. de anima"</note> and the second book of *On the Generation of Animals*, chapter 3 <note>original: "2. de gener. anim. cap. 3."</note>, cannot be performed without a physical and perishable instrument—he takes on mortality. However, insofar as he understands and wills—operations which, as is maintained throughout the book *On the Soul*, the first book of *On the Parts of Animals*, chapter 1, and the second book of *On the Generation of Animals*, chapter 3, are performed without a physical instrument and prove separability, immateriality, and immortality—he is to be numbered among the immortals. From these points the entire conclusion can be gathered: namely, that he is not of a simple nature, since three

<meta>catchword: three</meta>

<summary>Pomponazzi argues that human beings occupy a unique middle ground in the universe, possessing both a mortal nature (shared with animals and plants) and an immortal nature (shared with divine beings) based on their different biological and intellectual functions.</summary>
<keywords>human nature, immortality, Aristotle, vegetative soul, sensitive soul, intellect, will, middle ground, essence and operation</keywords>

--- Page 12 ---
<meta>This page continues the argument from Chapter 1, where Pomponazzi is defining the "middle nature" of man. It concludes the sentence begun on page 5 regarding how the human soul encompasses different levels of existence.</meta>

<page-num>6</page-num>
<header>On Immortality</header>

...includes, so to speak, three souls—namely, the vegetative, the sensitive, and the intellectual <note>In Aristotelian philosophy, these are the three "grades" of life: the vegetative (growth and nutrition, shared with plants), the sensitive (sensation and movement, shared with animals), and the intellectual (reason, unique to humans and higher beings).</note>—and thus claims for itself a twofold <note>original: "ancipitem," literally "two-headed" or "ambiguous."</note> nature; since it exists neither simply as mortal nor simply as immortal, but rather embraces both natures. For this reason, the ancients spoke well when they placed man between eternal and temporal things, because he is neither purely eternal nor purely temporal, since he participates in both natures. To man, existing in this middle state, has been given the power to take on whichever nature he wishes.

Because of this, three types of men are found. First, some are numbered among the Gods, though they are very few; these are the ones who, having subdued the vegetative and sensitive faculties, have become almost entirely rational. Others, however, having totally neglected the intellect and devoting themselves only to the vegetative and sensitive faculties, have, as it were, migrated into beasts; and this is perhaps what the Pythagorean fable <note>original: "Apologus Pythagoræus." Pomponazzi interprets Pythagoras's famous doctrine of the "transmigration of souls" (metempsychosis) as a metaphor for human behavior rather than a literal biological change.</note> meant when it said that human souls pass into various beasts. Finally, some are called "pure men"; these are the ones who have lived moderately according to the moral virtues, yet they did not devote themselves entirely to the intellect, nor did they completely abandon bodily virtues <note>original: "virtutibus corporeis," referring here to the functions or capacities belonging to the physical body.</note>. Each of these categories, however, has a great range of variety, as is clearly evident. This also accords with what is said in the Psalm: "You have made him a little lower than the Angels" <note>Psalm 8:5.</note>.

<meta>Digitized by Google watermark at bottom</meta>

<summary>Pomponazzi explains that humanity occupies a middle ground between the divine and the bestial, possessing the free will to ascend toward the rational or descend into the animalistic. He interprets the Pythagorean idea of soul migration as a metaphor for these different moral and intellectual states.</summary>

->*In which are set forth the ways by which the said multiplicity of human nature can be understood.*<-

## CHAPTER II.

Having thus seen the manifold and twofold <note>original: "ancipitique," meaning "two-headed" or "ambiguous."</note> nature of man—not indeed that which results from the composition of matter and form <note>In the Aristotelian "hylomorphic" view, all physical things are a compound of "matter" (the stuff they are made of) and "form" (the organizing principle that makes them what they are). Pomponazzi is focusing specifically on the nature of the form itself—the soul.</note>, but that which pertains to the form itself, or the soul—it remains to be seen how these things can be said. Since "immortal" and "mortal" are opposites that cannot be affirmed of the same thing at the same time, one might rightly wonder how it is possible for both to be attributed to the human soul. Indeed, this seems to be no light matter.

Therefore, either one and the same nature will be established which is at once mortal and immortal, or there must be one nature for the mortal and another for the immortal. If this second option is granted, it could be understood in three ways:

First, there would be a number of mortal and immortal souls corresponding to the number of men. For instance, in Socrates <note>Scholastic philosophers frequently used "Socrates" as a standard placeholder name for any individual human being in their logical examples.</note> there would be one immortal soul and one or two mortal souls, and so on for all others, so that each individual person would possess his own specific mortal and immortal soul.

Or rather, the second possibility is that in all men, only one single immortal soul is established, while for each individual...

<summary>Pomponazzi begins to analyze the logical problem of how the human soul can be both mortal and immortal, proposing that it must either be one nature with both traits or multiple souls within one person.</summary>
<keywords>Aristotelianism, hylomorphism, matter and form, dualism, individual soul, universal soul, logic of opposites</keywords>

--- Page 14 ---
<meta>This page continues the logical division of the six possible ways the human soul can be understood as both mortal and immortal. It transitions from the first set of possibilities (where there are distinct mortal and immortal souls) to the second set (where a single soul possesses both qualities in different respects). It concludes by introducing Chapter III, which focuses on the Averroistic view that there is only one universal immortal intellect for all of humanity.</meta>

<page-num>8</page-num>
<header>On Immortality</header>

...for each individual man the mortal souls are distributed and multiplied, or whether instead we should conversely posit that the immortal soul is multiplied <note>meaning there is a unique immortal soul for every individual</note> and the mortal soul is common to all. If, however, the other path is chosen—namely, that a man is mortal and immortal through one and the same soul—since it seems impossible for opposites to be predicated of the same thing, it simply cannot be that the same soul is mortal and immortal in an absolute sense. Rather, it must either be immortal <term>simpliciter: in an absolute, unqualified sense</term> and mortal <term>secundum quid: in a certain respect or relatively</term>, or conversely, absolutely mortal and immortal in a certain respect. Or perhaps it embraces both in a certain respect: that is, mortal in one way and immortal in another. By these three ways, contradiction can be sufficiently avoided. Therefore, to one gathering the points, this can be imagined in six ways, as appears to one reasoning and collecting the arguments.

->*In which is set forth the position affirming that the immortal soul is one in number, while the mortal is multiplied; a position followed by Themistius and Averroes.*<-

### CHAPTER III.

Of these six enumerated ways, the authorities <note>original: "iudices," literally "judges," referring to the leading philosophical schools or thinkers who have weighed in on the matter.</note> have accepted four, while two have vanished; for no one has posited that the imma- <note>The text cuts off here at the start of the word "immortal."</note>

<page-num>9</page-num>
<header>**On the Soul**</header>
<sig>A v</sig>

[the imma]terial soul is multiplied, but the material soul is one in number. This is held reasonably, as it is inconceivable that a single corporeal thing could exist in so many distinct locations and subjects, especially if it is subject to decay <note>original: "corruptibilis," referring to things that can break down or die</note>. Similarly, no one has proposed that the same thing is equally mortal and immortal, just as nothing can be composed equally of two contrary elements; rather, one must always predominate over the other, as is shown in *On the Heavens* <note>original: "1. de caelo tex.comm. 7."</note>, *On Generation and Corruption* <note>original: "2. de gener. 47."</note>, and the *Metaphysics* <note>original: "10. metaph. 23."</note>. We shall therefore deal with those four remaining ways individually.

Averroes, then—and, as I believe, Themistius before him—agreed in positing that the intellective soul is essentially distinct from the corruptible soul; however, they held that the intellective soul itself is "one in number" <note>meaning a single, universal entity shared by all humans</note> across all human beings, while the mortal soul is indeed multiplied <note>meaning each individual possesses their own unique mortal soul</note>.

The reason for this first claim is that when they saw Aristotle proving simply that the potential intellect <term>intellectus possibilis: the capacity of the human mind to receive and process information, which Aristotle argued must be "unmixed" with the body</term> is unmixed and immaterial, and consequently eternal—and that all his words tend toward this conclusion, as is clear to anyone examining his books *On the Soul*—and because they believed Aristotle's reasoning to be true in itself, they simply affirmed that the intellect is immortal. However, when they further observed that the sensitive and vegetative souls <note>the "lower" functions of the soul responsible for physical senses, growth, and nutrition</note> necessarily require a physical organ for their duties, as appears from the passages cited above, they [concluded...]

<summary>This page argues that a material soul cannot be singular and universal because a physical thing cannot be in many places at once. It introduces the "Averroistic" view that humans share one eternal, universal intellect while possessing individual, perishable souls for sensory and biological functions.</summary>

<keywords>Averroes, Themistius, Aristotle, potential intellect, intellective soul, corruptible soul, sensitive soul, vegetative soul, immortality, singularity of the intellect</keywords>

--- Page 16 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the intellective soul's nature, specifically building on the argument that a single entity cannot be both mortal and immortal. <page-num>10</page-num>
<header>On Immortality</header>

Such a thing is necessarily corporeal and perishable <note>original: "caducum," meaning falling or fleeting</note>, and they concluded that such a soul is simply mortal. But since it is impossible for the same thing to be both simply and absolutely mortal and immortal at once, they were forced to posit that the immortal soul is really distinct from the mortal soul. Themistius <note>a 4th-century Greek philosopher and commentator on Aristotle</note> also tries to pull Plato into this opinion, citing Plato’s words in the *Timaeus* which clearly seem to suggest this. Furthermore, that there is only one unique intellect in all human beings—whether it is posed as the potential intellect <term>intellectus possibilis: the capacity of the mind to receive and be shaped by knowledge</term> or otherwise—can be seen from a famous proposition among the Peripatetics <note>the followers of Aristotle, who famously walked while teaching</note>. This proposition states that the multiplication of individuals within the same species cannot occur except through quantified matter <note>the idea that individuals of the same "type" (like humans) are only distinct from each other because they occupy different "pieces" of matter</note>, as is stated in books 7 and 12 of the *Metaphysics* and book 2 of *On the Soul*. However, as to how the doubts raised against this view might be resolved, one can see by looking at their own books and those of their followers; for here we intend to be brief, reporting only what is necessary.

Although this opinion is very celebrated in our own time, and is held by almost everyone as a certainty that it is

[...attributed to] Aristotle, yet it seems to me that it is not only utterly false in itself, but even unintelligible and monstrous, and completely foreign to Aristotle <note>The author is arguing that Averroes’s interpretation of Aristotle is a gross misunderstanding</note>. Indeed, I believe that such foolishness was never even thought of, let alone believed. First, regarding its falsehood, I do not intend to bring forward anything new, but only to refer the reader to those things which that glory of the Latins, Saint Thomas Aquinas, wrote—both in his own book *Against the Unity of the Intellect* and in the first part of the *Summa*, and in the second book of *Against the Gentiles*, and in the *Disputed Questions on the Soul*, and in many other places. For indeed, he attacks this opinion so clearly and so subtly that, in my view, he leaves nothing untouched, and leaves no response which anyone could bring forward on behalf of Averroes un-attacked. For he attacks, scatters, and annihilates the whole thing, and no refuge is left for the **Averroists** <term>Averroists: followers of the philosopher Averroes who argued that all humans share one single, eternal mind</term> except for insults and curses against that Divine and Most Holy man. But regarding the second point, I have decided to bring forward these few points which give me full confidence: namely, that this is foreign to Aristotle, and that this is a fiction and a monster fabricated by **Averroes**.

First, because such an **intellective soul** <term>intellective soul: the highest part of the soul responsible for reasoning and understanding</term> either has some operation entirely independent of the body as both a subject and an object, or it has none. The second cannot be said, since to...

<meta>This page continues the author's argument from page 11, where he began to dismantle the Averroist position that the intellect is a single entity separate from the body. Here, he focuses on the logical contradictions in Averroes's interpretation of Aristotle's "On the Soul."</meta>

# On Immortality
<page-num>12</page-num>

It contradicts both reason and itself, since in commentary 12 at the end of the first book of *On the Soul* <note>original: *de anima*</note> he says these things, and he does not mean by this what appears on the surface of his words—namely, that understanding does not exist except through the imagination. For if that were the case, then the **material intellect** <term>material intellect: in medieval philosophy, the "potential" part of the mind that receives information; Averroes argued it was an eternal, non-physical substance</term> would be capable of being created and destroyed, just as **Alexander** <note>Alexander of Aphrodisias (c. 200 AD), a commentator who believed the human soul was tied to the body and thus mortal</note> understands it. From these points it is clear that, according to **Averroes**, the intellect has some operation that is entirely independent of the body. This is also confirmed by reason, since the intellect is not a form whose existence is established by a physical subject; therefore, it does not depend on a subject for its existence. Consequently, it does not depend on a subject for its operation either, since **operation follows being** <note>original: *operatio sequatur esse*; a philosophical principle meaning that a thing's way of acting is determined by its nature or way of existing</note>. However, it is clear enough that this does not harmonize with Aristotle and is contrary to his opinion. For Aristotle, at the end of the cited text in book 1 of *On the Soul*, says that "understanding is either imagination or it does not exist without imagination." And although he speaks conditionally there, nevertheless in book 3 of *On the Soul*, text 39, he states most clearly that there is no understanding without a **phantasm** <term>phantasm: a mental image or sensory representation derived from the senses; Aristotle argued the mind needs these "pictures" to think</term>. This is proven by experience. Therefore, according to Aristotle, the human intellect in no way has an operation that is entirely independent of the body—which is the opposite of what has been granted. To this, I see no other response except that the argument demonstrates something about human understanding, and insofar as through it in- <note>original catchword: *ligere* (to understand)</note>

<summary>The author argues that Averroes’s theory of a body-independent intellect contradicts Aristotle’s own claim that the mind cannot function without mental images (phantasms) produced by the body. This page highlights the tension between the "unity of the intellect" theory and Aristotle's empirical observations on human thought.</summary>

<keywords>Aristotle, Averroes, Alexander of Aphrodisias, material intellect, phantasm, imagination, On the Soul, Scholasticism</keywords>

--- Page 19 ---
<meta>This page continues the critique of Averroes’s theory of the mind. The previous page discussed how the intellect might operate independently of the body; here, the author raises a major objection based on Aristotle’s fundamental definition of the soul as the "act" of a physical body.</meta>

# The Soul
<page-num>13</page-num>

...through the intellect, man is said to be "understanding." For in this way it is confirmed that he always requires a **phantasm** <term>phantasm: a mental image or sensory impression that the mind uses to form abstract thoughts</term>; indeed, in a new act of understanding, and before that knowledge is fully grasped, this need is obvious. But even in eternal understanding, or in the very act of grasping knowledge, this principle still holds true. This is because the **possible intellect** <term>possible intellect: the "potential" part of the human mind that receives information and becomes "all things" through learning</term> is prepared to receive the **agent intellect** <term>agent intellect: the "active" part of the mind that processes sensory data into abstract concepts</term> as its form. This happens through **speculative habits** <note>original: *habitus speculatiuos*; the stable mental dispositions or "stored" knowledge a person acquires through study</note> which depend on the sensitive power <note>the physical senses and imagination</note> to be preserved, as Averroes himself says in commentary 39 of the third book of *On the Soul*. Truly, however, if the intellect is considered entirely on its own, it does not depend on a phantasm at all.

Although this is stated quite ingeniously, it seems to achieve nothing. This is because, according to the common definition of the soul, "the soul is the **act of a physical organic body**" <note>original: *actus corporis physici organici*; the classic Aristotelian definition meaning the soul is what makes a biological body "alive" and functional</note>, etc. Therefore, the intellective soul is the act of a physical organic body. Since the intellect, by its very nature, is the act of a physical organic body, it follows that in every one of its works it will depend on a physical organ, either as its **subject** <note>the thing that performs the action</note> or as its **object** <note>the thing the action is directed toward</note>. Therefore, it will never be totally separated from the physical organ.

From this, perhaps, it is said that it is the same for the soul as it is for the other **intelligences** <term>intelligences: in medieval thought, these were purely spiritual beings, like angels, who moved the celestial spheres</term>. For the **Commentator** <note>Averroes (1126–1198), the Islamic philosopher whose commentaries on Aristotle were central to university learning</note> himself, in commentary 19 of the third book of *On the Soul*, posits that the human soul is the lowest of these intelligences. Other intelligences, however, can be considered in two ways:

<summary>The author challenges Averroes’s claim that the intellect is independent by citing Aristotle’s definition of the soul as the "act of a physical body." If the soul is essentially tied to the body, the author argues, its operations—including thought—must always depend on physical organs.</summary>

<keywords>intellect, phantasm, Averroes, Aristotle, soul and body, possible intellect, agent intellect, speculative habits, intelligences</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the definition of the soul. On the previous page, the author explored the intellect’s possible independence from the body. Here, he attempts to reconcile the intellect's nature with Aristotle's definition of the soul by comparing the human intellect to the "intelligences" that move the stars and planets.</meta>

# On Immortality
<page-num>14</page-num>

In one way, these [higher beings] are considered in themselves and not as they actuate a celestial body; in this sense, they are not "souls," and they have operations that in no way depend on a body, such as understanding and desiring. In another way, however, they can be considered as they actuate celestial bodies, and in this sense, it is appropriate to call them souls. Indeed, the given definition of the soul applies to them, for in this way they are the **act of a physical organic body** <term>act of a physical organic body: the standard Aristotelian definition (entelechy), meaning the soul is the organizing principle that makes a body alive and functional</term>, even if that definition is said somewhat equivocally <note>original: *aequivocè*; meaning the term "soul" is being used in a different sense for stars than for humans</note>, as the Commentator <note>original: "Commentator"; referring to Averroes (Ibn Rushd), the influential 12th-century Islamic philosopher</note> himself says in commentary 5 of the third book of *On the Soul*. For celestial bodies are living beings, as Aristotle posits in the 8th book of *Physics*, the 2nd book of *On the Heavens*, and the 12th book of *Metaphysics*—though, as the Commentator says in his book *On the Substance of the Orb*, this is also stated equivocally.

Therefore, the **intellective soul** can also be considered in two ways:

First, as it is the lowest of the **intelligences** <term>intelligences: in medieval philosophy, these were pure spirits or "angels" that moved the celestial spheres</term> and is not considered in relation to its own "sphere"—namely, the human being. In this way, it depends on the body in no way at all, neither in its being nor in its operation; thus, in this sense, it is not the act of a physical organic body.

In the second way, it can be considered in relation to its "sphere" [the human person], and in this way it is indeed the **act of a physical organic body**. Therefore, as such, it is not detached from the body, neither in its being nor in its operation. For this reason, insofar as it is the form of a human being, it always requires a **phantasm** <term>phantasm: a mental image or sensory impression that the mind uses to form abstract thoughts</term> in its operation, though not in an absolute sense as mentioned above. And according to this distinction, the common doubt can be resolved...

<summary>The author argues that the intellective soul has a dual nature: as a pure intelligence (independent of matter) and as the functional "act" of the human body (requiring sensory images or phantasms to think).</summary>

But in truth, this response seems to fail in many ways. First, indeed, because if the judgment regarding the human soul were the same as that regarding the other Intelligences, then when Aristotle conducts his treatment of the Intelligences in the second book of the *Metaphysics*, he should have also treated the human soul; nonetheless, he did not do this at all. Furthermore, if the judgment concerning the human intellect is the same as that of the Intelligences, why then does Aristotle, in the second book of the *Physics*, text 26, set the human soul as the **limit of natural consideration**? For if Aristotle means this regarding **the fact of its existence** <note>original: *quia est*; a logical term for proving *that* something exists rather than *what* it is</note>, it is false; for the naturalist demonstrates that God and the Intelligences exist. Indeed, according to the Commentator <note>the "Commentator" refers to Averroes (Ibn Rushd), the 12th-century Islamic philosopher whose interpretations of Aristotle were standard in universities</note> in the first book of *On the Soul*, commentary 2, and the 12th book of the *Metaphysics*, commentary 36, the Metaphysician <note>original: *Divinus*; literally "the Divine [scholar]," referring to one who studies the highest, divine causes</note> receives these facts from the naturalist. Indeed, even if an **Intelligence** <term>Intelligence: in medieval and Renaissance thought, these were pure spiritual beings or "angels" that moved the celestial spheres</term> is both an intellect and a soul, and does not require a body for its act of understanding, yet requires a body for the act of moving; nevertheless, moving something through space and understanding are very different operations. 

In the human soul, however, they posit acts of understanding where one depends on the body and the other is simply absolute <note>meaning "absolute" or "independent" (original: *absoluta*); a thought process that does not rely on physical senses</note>, which does not seem consistent with reason. For regarding one and the same operation toward a single thing, there should only be one mode of operating.

Moreover, it seems superfluous and unbelievable that a single thing should have almost infinite operations regarding the same object at the same time. Yet this follows from that opinion: because that intellect would understand God with an eternal act of understanding, and yet, through a "new" understanding, it would have as many individual acts of understanding regarding God as there are humans who understand God. This appears to be a pure fiction, as can be shown by many arguments. Truly, if an Intelligence understands without a body, it would not move...

[...moving] spatially without a body, no contradiction follows, since understanding and moving spatially are operations of very different kinds; one is **immanent** <note>original: *immanens*; an action that stays within the performer, like thinking or feeling</note> and the other is **transient** <note>original: *transiens*; an action that passes from the agent into an external object, like pushing a stone</note>. For he says in <term>Text 12, Book 1 of *On the Soul*</term> <note>Aristotle’s *De Anima*</note>, that whether the intellect is **phantasy** <term>phantasia: the faculty of the mind that processes "phantasms" or mental images derived from the senses</term> or is simply not possible without phantasy, it cannot be separated.

He later declares that its "mover" is the phantasm. However, according to what has been said, anything that requires a phantasm is inseparable from matter. Therefore, that line of reasoning proves the intellect is material rather than immaterial.

But perhaps it might still be said that because the intellect does not require a physical organ as its **subject** <note>subject: the physical "seat" or container for a faculty, such as the eye is the subject for the sense of sight.</note>, it is therefore simply immaterial—an argument Aristotle makes immediately after the one mentioned. Truly, this does not seem to be effective, because either that condition alone is sufficient, or another is required: namely, that the intellect is not moved by the body...

<meta>This page continues the logical refutation of an opponent's argument regarding the soul's relationship to the body. The previous page established that if the intellect (the mind) were synonymous with "imagination," it would be bound to a physical organ. # The Soul.

...[the claim that] being organic in terms of its **subject** <note>subject: the underlying matter or physical organ that "holds" a power, like the eye holds sight.</note> and being immaterial are convertible is false. Furthermore, whenever two modes are assigned to a thing under a **disjunction** <note>disjunction: a logical "either/or" statement.</note>, that thing can be separated from either one of them indifferently, or at least remain while one of them is removed. For example, **illiberality** <term>illiberality: a lack of generosity; the vice of being "unfree" with money.</term> happens in two ways: either through **avarice** <note>original: *auaritia*; greed or the desire to hoard.</note> or through **prodigality** <note>original: *prodigalitas*; wasteful extravagance.</note>. Thus, an illiberal person is found who is avaricious without being prodigal, and an illiberal person who is prodigal without being avaricious. If they could not be separated, they would not be two distinct modes of illiberality; instead, they would either coincide with each other, or both would necessarily occur together in a **conjunction** <note>conjunction: a logical "and" statement.</note> to constitute illiberality. Indeed, if no one were illiberal unless they were simultaneously prodigal and avaricious, it would not be correct to say that illiberality requires "prodigality *or* avarice," but rather that "prodigality *and* avarice" joined together constitute illiberality. 

Therefore, if being **imagination** <term>phantasia: the faculty that produces and stores mental images.</term>, or being "not without imagination," is sufficient for the soul to be **inseparable** from the body: then it must be that the soul can be inseparable without one of those conditions being met, either indifferently or at least in a specific case. If the first is true, then it stands that the **intellect** <note>intellect: the higher reasoning power of the soul.</note> is not without a **phantasm** <note>original: *phantasmate*; a mental image derived from the senses.</note>, and yet it is not the faculty of imagination itself. Consequently, it would always require the body as an **object** <note>object: the thing being acted upon or observed (like a book being read), rather than the physical "subject" where the power resides.</note> and not as a **subject**. This is contrary to what has been conceded by the adversary. But if the second is granted, namely that...

# On Immortality

...determinately and not indifferently, since it cannot happen that it needs some body as a **subject** <note>subject: the physical organ or matter that "houses" a faculty, like the eye houses sight.</note> and not as an **object** <note>object: the thing being acted upon or thought about; for the mind, these are the "phantasms" or mental images provided by the senses.</note>. Therefore, it will stand that it will need [the body] as an object, and not as a subject.

Consequently, the same conclusion follows as before: Furthermore, according to this position, the **intellect** <term>intellectus: the higher power of the human mind capable of abstract thought, distinguished from simple sensation.</term> has some operation that is in every way independent of the body. Since this point is in doubt, it must be verified by some sign; and there is no other sign to devise by which it can be verified, except that the intellect itself is **immaterial**. However, since this [immateriality] is no less doubtful than the first point, it requires a **demonstration** <note>demonstration: a formal logical proof that leads to certain knowledge.</note>.

The question is therefore asked concerning that **middle term** <note>middle term: the bridge in a logical syllogism that connects the subject and the predicate of the conclusion.</note>: is it because the intellect simultaneously does not depend on the body as a subject and object, or because it only does not depend on the body as a subject? The first cannot be said, both because it would be a **begging of the question** <term>petitio principii: a logical fallacy where the conclusion one is trying to prove is already assumed as a premise.</term>—since, when intending to prove that the intellect does not depend on the body in some of its work, we would be assuming that it is immaterial; and when proving it is immaterial, we would be taking for granted that in some work it does not depend on the body. 

Furthermore, there is no such middle term in which it is not assumed that the intellect does not depend on the body as an object. For the first demonstration in Book 3 of **On the Soul** <note>original: *3. de anima*; referring to Aristotle's foundational text on psychology and the nature of living things.</note> proves the intellect to be immaterial because it receives all material forms, provided that such "receiving" consists...

<summary>The author explores the logical requirements for proving the mind is immaterial, distinguishing between the body as a "subject" (physical organ) and an "object" (source of information). He warns that one cannot prove the mind's independence by assuming its immateriality, as this would be circular reasoning.</summary>

# On the Soul

### [Page 23]

...to be acted upon, as **Aristotle** says in that same place; therefore, it will be moved by a physical thing, and thus it will need [it] as an **object** <note>object: the "material" the mind works on, such as a sensory image, rather than the physical organ the mind resides in.</note>. The second demonstration is that the **intelligible species** <term>intelligible species: the abstract mental representation or "concept" that the mind forms when it understands something.</term> is not received in a physical organ, but in the intellect itself. Therefore, the same conclusion follows as before, since to "receive" is to be "acted upon."

It is necessary, therefore, to state the alternative, namely that the **middle term** <note>middle term: the connecting link in a logical proof.</note> is that the intellect does not need the body as a **subject** <note>subject: the physical matter or organ—like the brain or eye—that would "contain" the faculty.</note>, even if it does need it as an object. 

But if this is the case, why is it that **Aristotle**, in order to capture the listener's attention in the introduction to *On the Soul* <note>original: "de anima"</note>, said it was necessary to first understand its operations? He added that if thinking is not possible without **phantasy** <term>phantasy: the faculty of "phantasms" or mental images derived from the senses. Medieval philosophers debated if we can think without using these mental pictures.</term>—or if it never occurs without phantasy—then it is not possible for the soul to be separated from the body. 

Therefore, to conclude that the soul is "separable" (the opposite of being inseparable), one must simultaneously assume both that thinking is not the same as phantasy and that in some part of its work, the intellect does not depend on phantasy. Consequently, it must not depend on the body as a subject, nor even as an object—at least in some of its operations. However, this contradicts what we just granted <note>The "concessi": the previous admission that the mind might still need the body for its "objects" or data.</note>.

This point is confirmed again by the opponent’s own logic: needing the body as a subject and being a material power are interchangeable terms. Likewise, their opposites are interchangeable: namely, not needing the body as a subject and being an **immaterial** power. For as stated in the first book of the *Posterior Analytics* <note>original: "1. post." <meta>This page continues the logical dissection of Aristotle’s argument regarding the soul's independence. Page 23 ended mid-sentence, discussing Aristotle's introduction to "On the Soul," where he suggests that if thinking requires "phantasy" (mental images), the soul cannot be separated from the body. # On Immortality

[...to conclude that the soul is material, **Aristotle**] added that, besides **phantasy** <term>phantasy: the faculty of "phantasms" or mental images derived from the senses.</term>, it is not possible for it to exist without phantasy. For he errs through the fallacy of "non-cause as cause" <note>This is a logical error where someone claims a certain factor is the cause of an outcome when it actually isn't. Here, the author argues that needing a mental image is not the same thing as being made of physical matter.</note>, since the precise cause of immateriality is not needing the body as a **subject** <note>subject: the physical "vessel" or organ, like the brain, where a faculty resides.</note>. This is also most evidently confirmed again, because for **immateriality**, besides not needing the body as a subject, it is either necessary beyond this not to need a specific body as an **object** <note>object: the data or "input" the mind works on, such as a sensory image.</note> in any of its specific operations, or that is not necessary. 

The second option cannot be granted based on what has already been conceded, since "not needing the body as a subject" and "not needing the body as an object in any specific operation" are **convertible** <note>convertible: terms that are logically equivalent; if one is true, the other must be true.</note>. Therefore, once one is established, the other is established. It remains, then, that immateriality requires both conditions to be met together. 

Consequently, since materiality is the opposite of immateriality, for **materiality** itself it is sufficient to need the body as a subject, *or* to need the body as an object. Therefore, either these two types of dependence are separable—and thus they would not be "convertible," which contradicts what was just agreed upon—or they are inseparable. If they are inseparable, they should not be presented as an "either/or" choice (**disjunction**), which is nonetheless what **Aristotle** does. 

The final logical consequence is clear: because what is stated as a disjunction <note>disjunction: an "either/or" statement.</note> is verified by one or the other being true, but not necessarily both together. For in the 10th book of the *Metaphysics* <note>original: "metaph."</note>, text 17, and the **Commentator** <term>Commentator: Averroes, the 12th-century Andalusian philosopher famous for his extensive commentaries on Aristotle.</term> in the 3rd book of *On the Heavens* <note>original: "de caelo"</note>, commentary 56, and **Boethius** in his book *On the Hypothetical Syllogism* <note>original: "de syllogismo hypothetico"</note>, say that a ques-

<meta>catchword: tion</meta>

<summary>This page critiques Aristotle's logic, arguing that his criteria for the soul's "materiality" are flawed. The author contends that needing a body to provide images (as an object) is logically linked to needing it as a physical home (as a subject), challenging Aristotle's use of "either/or" reasoning in his definitions.</summary>

# Of the Soul

...[that the] negation and the disjunctive <note>A "disjunctive" proposition is an "either/or" statement (e.g., "The soul is either material or it is independent").</note> proposition are vain and ridiculous if all parts are proven true; and furthermore, an affirmative copulative <note>A "copulative" proposition is an "and" statement (e.g., "The soul is immaterial AND it uses the body").</note> would then be contradicted by a copulative made of opposite parts, rather than a disjunctive—which is most obviously false. Wherefore, unless I am mistaken, the **Commentator** <note>original: "Commentator"; this refers to Averroes (1126–1198), the Islamic philosopher whose commentaries on Aristotle were foundational to medieval university study.</note>, **St. Thomas** <note>original: "D. Thomas"; referring to St. Thomas Aquinas.</note>, and whoever else believes that **Aristotle** thinks the human intellect is truly immortal, is far from the truth. Besides, it is strange that **Aristotle** posited that the intellect sometimes understands without a **phantasm** <term>phantasm: a mental image or sensory impression that the mind "looks at" to form abstract thoughts.</term>, yet in all other places says that there is no understanding without a phantasm; for he should not have spoken so absolutely.

Thirdly, regarding the main point: because, according to the **Commentator**, it is necessary to place **human happiness** in the conjunction of the **agent intellect** with the **possible intellect** <term>agent and possible intellect: Aristotle's theory that the mind has two parts—one that acts (agent) to process sensory data, and one that receives (possible) the resulting knowledge.</term>, as he clearly shows in Book 3 of *On the Soul* <note>original: "lib. 3. de anima comm. 36."</note>, comment 36. But how vain and dissonant this is with **Aristotle** is not difficult to see. It is vain because, as far as histories record, no one up to these times has been found [who has achieved this union]. And thus the "end" or purpose of man is futile, since it is attained by no one; indeed, it cannot be attained by anyone, since the means ordered toward that end cannot be possessed. For it is impossible for any man to know everything, as **Plato** says in Book 10 of the *Republic*, nor even all things that can be contemplated; indeed, no science is perfectly possessed...

# On Immortality

...[has never been ha]bited <note>The text likely reads "habited" or "experienced" in the sense of a state of being.</note> until the present day, as experience clearly shows. Moreover, it is manifest that this theory is against **Aristotle**, since in the book of the *Ethics* <note>Aristotle’s *Nicomachean Ethics*.</note>, where he determines the ultimate goal of man as a human being, he places it in the **habit of wisdom** <term>habit of wisdom: a stable mental state (Latin: *habitus sapientiali*) where one possesses deep understanding through contemplation.</term>. Nor can anyone claim that this book was incomplete, since at the very end of that work he both provides a summary and connects his discussion to the book of *Politics*. 

Therefore, I cannot help but wonder greatly why the **Commentator** <note>Averroes.</note> ascribes this **unity of the intellect** <term>unity of the intellect: the belief that all human beings share one single, eternal mind, rather than having individual souls.</term> to Aristotle, given that Aristotle never proposes this anywhere. On the contrary, in the second book of the *Physics*, text 26, where he mentions the human soul, he says that they are multiplied <note>original: "multiplicatas"; meaning there are many individual souls, not just one.</note>. For he says the following: "to what point [must the natural philosopher know the form]? for whose sake is each thing? and concerning those which are species separated in matter—for man and the sun generate man from matter." <note>Aristotle, *Physics* II.2, 194b. The argument is that since individual humans are born, they must have individual "forms" or souls.</note> 

From this, it is manifest that he posited not just one soul, but many. Furthermore, there is the matter of that **conjunction** <note>The supposed union between the individual mind and the universal agent intellect.</note>, since no such thing has ever been found in another person, nor did Aristotle say anything about it. Therefore, it seems to me that this theory, which is a **fiction** <note>original: "figmentum"</note> in itself, is also contrary to Aristotle’s own words.

### CHAPTER VI.

This approach, in both of its parts, is challenged by **Saint Thomas** <note>Thomas Aquinas (1225–1274), the highly influential Dominican theologian.</note> in the first part of the *Summa*, and in many other places—and in my opinion, he does so abundantly and clearly. # The Soul

...and the wagon, and many other absurdities follow which are brought forward by him <note>Referring to Thomas Aquinas, who argued against the "plurality of forms."</note>. Furthermore, to propose a **plurality of substantial forms** <note>The "substantial form" is the philosophical term for the essential nature that makes a thing what it is. Some argued a human has several forms (e.g., a "body" form and a "soul" form), while others argued for one single unifying form.</note> within the same composite being—as the second argument suggests—seems foreign to the thought of **Aristotle** and many of the **Peripatetics** <note>The "Peripatetics" were the followers of Aristotle, named after the covered walkways (peripatoi) in Athens where they held their discussions.</note>. However, I will bring forward two points which make all the aforementioned views seem distant from both the truth and the mind of Aristotle.

First, this view seems to contradict our own experience. For I, who am writing these words, am currently distressed by many physical pains <note>original: "cruciatibus corporis"</note>, which is a function of the **sensitive** power <note>The "sensitive soul" or power refers to the capacity to feel, perceive, and experience physical sensations, which humans share with animals.</note>. And yet, the very same "I" who suffers also reason through medical causes to find a way to repel these pains—an act which can only be done through the **intellect** <note>The "intellectual soul" or power is the capacity for abstract reasoning and logic, which was believed to be unique to humans.</note>. If, therefore, the essence by which I feel were different from the essence by which I understand, how could it be that I, the one feeling, am the same as I, the one understanding? If that were so, we might as well say that two different men tied together share each other's thoughts, which is ridiculous.

Furthermore, it is not difficult to see that such an opinion is far from Aristotle’s own view. In the second book of *On the Soul* <note>original: "2. de anima"</note>, he explains that the **vegetative** power <note>The "vegetative" power governs basic life functions like growth and nutrition, shared with plants.</note> exists within the sensitive power just as a **triangle** exists within a **square** <note>original: "trigonum in tetragono." Aristotle used this geometric analogy to show that a higher soul (like a human's) includes all the abilities of lower souls (like a plant's or animal's) within it as a single unit, rather than having separate souls stacked together.</note>. It is clear that a triangle is not in a square as something physically separate from it; rather, that which is potentially a triangle is actually a square. Therefore, since according to Aristotle the sensitive power relates to the intellect in mortal beings in the same way...

## CHAPTER VII.

### *In which is presented the position asserting that the mortal and the immortal are the same thing within a human being; and further, that the soul is immortal in an absolute sense, but mortal in a specific respect.*

**S**ince, therefore, we have universally rejected the view that believes the intellectual and sensitive powers are really distinct within a human being, it follows that the intellectual and the sensitive must be one and the same in man. And although this can be understood in three different ways (as was mentioned previously), only two of those ways are actually reasonable.

The first of these was the view that the soul is **absolutely immortal** <note>original: "simpliciter," meaning in its primary or essential nature.</note> in man, but **mortal in a specific respect** <note>original: "secundum quid," meaning in a qualified sense or regarding a particular function.</note>. And although, by the nature of logical division, this view could itself be split into two—namely, that this soul is either a single entity for all of humanity, or that it is multiplied according to the number of individual human beings—nevertheless, because no...

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the "second mode" of understanding the relationship between the sensitive and intellectual aspects of the human soul. ->Soul.<-

[Averroe]s <note>The text likely refers to Averroes, an Islamic philosopher who argued that the intellect was a single substance shared by all humans.</note> presented the first mode, which we will therefore leave aside. Instead, we will speak only of the second mode. Although many distinguished men have followed this view, it seems to me that **St. Thomas** <note>Saint Thomas Aquinas (1225–1274), the influential Dominican friar and theologian.</note> presented it more fully and more clearly. Therefore, I will refer only to his words. To ensure it is understood systematically, I will summarize his opinion in five points.

The first is that the intellectual <note>The faculty of abstract reasoning.</note> and the sensitive <note>The faculty of physical sensation and perception.</note> powers in a human being are one and the same thing.

The second is that such a soul is truly and **absolutely** <note>original: "simpliciter," meaning in its fundamental essence.</note> immortal, but mortal **in a specific respect** <note>original: "secundum quid," referring to the loss of physical sensory functions after death.</note>.

The third is that such a soul is truly the **form of the human being** <note>In this philosophical context, a "form" is the organizing principle or essence that makes a body a living human, rather than just a pile of matter.</note>, and does not act merely as a motor <note>A "motor" refers to an external mover, like a pilot in a ship; Aquinas argued the soul is joined to the body as its nature, not just its driver.</note>.

The fourth is that this same soul is multiplied according to the number of individuals <note>This counters the idea that there is only one "universal" soul for all of humanity; every person has their own distinct soul.</note>.

The fifth is that this kind of soul begins its existence along with the body, but it **comes from outside** <note>original: "venit deforis," implying it is not produced by biological processes.</note> and is produced by God alone—not through biological generation, but through **creation**. However, it does not cease to exist when the body does; from that point on, it is everlasting.

The first of these points is made clear enough by what has already been said: first, because multiple **substantial forms** <note>The primary essence that defines what a thing is.</note> cannot exist within the same subject; second, because it appears to be the same essence that both understands and feels; and third, because in mortal beings, the sensitive power is contained within the intellectual power, just as a **triangle** <note>original: "trigonum"</note> is contained within a **quadrangle** <note>original: "tetragono"; this is a classic Aristotelian analogy suggesting that higher levels of life include all the functions of lower levels.</note>.

<meta>This page continues the summary of St. Thomas Aquinas's views on the human soul, specifically expanding on the second of the five points mentioned on the previous page: that the soul is truly immortal yet mortal in a specific respect.</meta>

# On Immortality

As for the second point, in which the full force of the argument consists—namely, that such a soul is truly immortal, yet mortal **in a specific respect** <note>original: "secundum quid." This refers to the idea that while the soul's essence lives on, the physical sensory functions it performed through the body cease at death.</note>—it is explained in many ways. First, by the reasoning of **Aristotle** in the third book of *On the Soul* <note>original: "3. de anima," referring to Aristotle's foundational text *De Anima*.</note>, because such a soul is capable of receiving all material forms. However, that which is of this kind cannot itself be material, as Aristotle explains in that same place, since the receiver must be **stripped** <note>original: "denudatum," meaning free from or devoid of the nature of the thing it is trying to perceive.</note> of the nature of the thing being received. This is also a proposition conceded by **Plato** in the *Timaeus*, and as **Averroes** <note>A 12th-century Islamic philosopher whose commentaries on Aristotle were central to medieval university study.</note> says in his commentary on the second book of *On the Soul*, this is universally verified in both physical and spiritual actions. For example, the eye, which is receptive to the **images** <note>original: "specierum," referring to the 'species' or sensible forms of things.</note> of color, must itself be colorless; and so it is universally for the other senses.

Second, because if the intellect were a material form, any form received within it would only be understood **potentially** <note>In Scholastic philosophy, "potential" knowledge is like a closed book—it exists but isn't being actively "read" or understood in its essence.</note>; thus, the intellect would either not know anything at all, or it would only know things in a **particular** <note>Knowing a "particular" means seeing one specific dog; knowing "universally" means understanding the concept of "dog" itself.</note> way.

Third, because if it were material, it would be an **organic power** <note>A power tied to a specific physical organ, like sight is tied to the eye.</note>; therefore, it would be limited to a certain category of beings, or at least to a singular way of existing. Consequently, it would either not know all things, or it would not know them in a **universal way**.

This is also evidently proven by a **sign** <note>An "argument from a sign" is an observation of an effect that points back to a cause.</note>: for the way we desire things naturally follows the way we know things. The intellect apprehends the universal, which is eternal; therefore, the **will** shall also desire the eternal. Such a desire is natural, since the wills of all people seek this.

<summary>This page argues for the immortality of the human soul by demonstrating that the intellect must be immaterial to perceive universal truths, supported by Aristotle, Plato, and Averroes.</summary>

# The Soul.

Furthermore, a natural desire cannot be frustrated <note>In Scholastic philosophy, if every human naturally desires to live forever, that desire must correspond to a reality, otherwise nature would be "in vain."</note>, since according to the first book of *On the Heavens* <note>original: "1. de coelo," a work by Aristotle.</note>, God and nature do nothing in vain: therefore it is proved that the intellect is **simply** <term>simply<gloss>In an absolute or unqualified sense (Latin: simpliciter).</gloss></term> immortal. Even the words of **Aristotle** are so clear on this point that they do not need an interpreter, since in the first, second, and third books of *On the Soul* <note>original: "de anima."</note> he posits that the intellect is separate and is not the **act** <term>act<gloss>The realization or functioning of a potential; the soul is the 'active' principle of the body.</gloss></term> of any body. Also, in the part where he deals with the **active intellect** <term>active intellect<gloss>The power of the mind that abstracts universal concepts from sensory data.</gloss></term>, he says that the active intellect is truly immaterial, because the **passive intellect** <note>original: "possibilis," the part of the mind that receives or "undergoes" thought.</note> is of this kind, and the agent is more excellent than the patient <note>The "agent" (the one acting) is considered superior to the "patient" (the one being acted upon).</note>. He affirms the same in Book 1, Chapter 1 of *On the Parts of Animals* and Book 2, Chapter 3 of *On the Generation of Animals*.

However, the fact that this kind of soul is mortal **in a specific respect** <term>in a specific respect<gloss>A translation of the Latin "secundum quid," meaning it is true only in a certain context or sense, rather than absolutely.</gloss></term> can be shown by two points. First, because this soul is intellectual, sensitive, and vegetative, as is clear from the first statement. Yet the sensitive and vegetative powers, if they were separated from the intellectual, are corruptible; therefore, the intellectual part is not mortal in itself, but because it contains a **level** <note>The human soul is unique because it combines high-level reasoning with the lower-level biological functions of plants (vegetative) and animals (sensitive).</note> which, if taken on its own, is mortal. Second, because the human soul does not exercise the works of the sensitive and vegetative powers except through a **perishable instrument** <note>The physical body.</note>; therefore, it is not mortal in itself, but by reason of such work and such an instrument.

The third statement—namely, that the soul is the **form of man**—is evident from the universal definition of the soul, since it is the act of a physical body, etc., and it is the principle by which we understand, as

<meta>This page continues the logical defense of the immortality of the soul, building on the previous discussion of Aristotle's "On the Soul." # On Immortality

Aristotle clearly demonstrates there.

The fourth point follows from the third; for if the intellective soul is the **form** <term>form<gloss>The essential nature or organizing principle of a living thing; that which makes a human being specifically "human."</gloss></term> by which a human is human, then if there were only one soul for all humans, the existence and the actions of all humans would be the same. This has already been shown to be false in the argument against the **Commentator** <note>original: "Commentatorem," referring to the 12th-century philosopher Averroes, who famously argued that all humans share a single, universal intellect.</note>. Aristotle also explicitly places souls in the plural number in the second book of the *Physics* <note>original: "2. phys. tex. 26."</note>, as was mentioned earlier.

The fifth point—namely, that the soul is "made"—is evident, first from the second book of the *Physics*, since "the sun and man generate man" <note>A classic Aristotelian phrase meaning that human reproduction requires both a biological parent and the broader influence of nature/the heavens.</note>. It is also evident from the third point, which stated that the soul is the form by which a human is human; and in the twelfth book of the *Metaphysics* <note>original: "12. metaph. tex. 17."</note>, it is said that a form begins to exist at the same time as the thing it belongs to. Furthermore, in the second book of *On the Generation of Animals* <note>original: "2. de gener. anim. cap. 3."</note>, it is said that only the intellect "comes from without" <note>original: "venire de foris." This suggests the mind is not a product of physical reproduction but is granted by a higher source.</note>. That the soul is not produced through **generation** <term>generation<gloss>The natural biological process of reproduction and physical growth.</gloss></term> is clear from the fact that whatever is produced by generation is material and perishable. Yet it has already been demonstrated in the second point that the soul is immaterial and incorruptible. That it is made by God alone is also evident, because since it is not made through generation, it must be through **creation** <term>creation<gloss>The act of bringing something into existence from nothing, as opposed to reforming existing matter.</gloss></term>; and it has been demonstrated elsewhere that only God can create. This is also clear from that passage in the second book of *On the Generation of Animals*, where it is said, "therefore, only the divine and immortal intellect exists" <note>original: "solus igitur intellectus diuinus & immortalis existit."</note>. ## Concerning Immortality

...namely, that in a human being, the sensitive and intellective capacities are in reality the same; however, the other four points remain highly ambiguous to me.

First, regarding the claim that such an essence is inherently and truly immortal, yet only improperly and **in a certain respect** <term>in a certain respect<gloss>Latin: *secundum quid*. A logical distinction used to describe something that is true only in a limited sense or under specific conditions, rather than absolutely.</gloss></term> mortal. This is doubtful because the opposite can be proven using the same logic he employs. For it was concluded that the soul is immortal based on the facts that such an essence receives all material forms (since things received within it are **actually understood**), that it does not use a bodily organ, and that it strives for eternity and higher things.

However, by the same token, since the soul operates materially as a **vegetative capacity** <note>The "vegetative soul" refers to basic biological functions like growth and nutrition; the "sensitive soul" refers to perception and movement; the "intellective soul" refers to higher reasoning.</note>, does not receive all forms (acting as a sensitive capacity), uses a bodily organ, and strives for temporal and perishable things, it could be proven that it is truly and absolutely mortal—though it would be immortal only in a certain respect insofar as it understands. This is because the intellect is **incorruptible** <gloss>cannot decay or die</gloss> when not joined to matter, but is **corruptible** <gloss>subject to death</gloss> when joined to it. Furthermore, in the act of understanding, it does not employ a bodily instrument—just as he himself says that the soul is only material **by accident** <term>by accident<gloss>Latin: *per accidens*. A philosophical term for a property that is not part of the essential nature of a thing, but happens to be true of it in a particular circumstance.</gloss></term> and in a certain respect. There seems to be no stronger reason for one side of this argument than the other.

# On Immortality

...mostly concerning the other powers, by which it happens that an essence of this kind is truly bodily and corruptible, and is scarcely even a shadow of the intellect. This also seems to be the reason why, out of so many thousands of people, hardly one is found who is studious and devoted to the intellectual life. This is a natural cause; for it has always been so, though it fluctuates to a greater or lesser degree. The cause (I say) is that by nature, a human being exists as more sensual <note>Driven by the senses and physical desires rather than reason.</note> than immortal. This is even apparent since many have included "mortal" as the specific difference <note>In traditional logic, a "difference" is the specific characteristic that distinguishes a species from others in its genus. Here, the author notes that many philosophers define "man" as a "mortal rational animal."</note> in the definition of a human being. If (I say) you consider these things, the opposing side will seem more in harmony with the truth than that of St. Thomas <note>St. Thomas Aquinas (1225–1274), the influential theologian who argued that the human soul is naturally immortal because the intellect operates independently of the body.</note>.

**Thirdly**, it is argued against the same position because the soul being immortal is not something self-evident. Therefore, just as was argued against Averroes <note>Averroes (Ibn Rushd), a 12th-century Islamic philosopher known for his commentaries on Aristotle. The author is likely referring to Averroes' controversial view that there is only one "Active Intellect" for all of humanity.</note>, we must ask by what evidence this immortality is known. Is it known because the soul, in its operation, does not precisely need a physical organ as its subject <note>The "subject" is the physical vessel or tool required for a function to occur.</note>, or is it known through some other added reason? 

The first point cannot be maintained, according to Aristotle himself in Book 1 of *On the Soul*, text 12 <note>Aristotle's *De Anima*. The "text 12" refers to the specific passage numbering used in medieval and Renaissance commentaries.</note>. For to conclude that something is inseparable from the body, it is enough if either of these conditions is met: either that its operation takes place within a physical subject, or that in every one of its operations it requires the body as an object. As he says: "If, therefore, thinking is either imagination or is not possible without imagination, it cannot be separated from the body" <note>original: "si igitur intelligere aut est phantasia, aut non est sine phantasia, non contingit ipsum separari." Here, "imagination" or *phantasia* refers to the mental images derived from the senses which Aristotle believed were the "fuel" for thought.</note>. Therefore, for the soul to be separable [and thus immortal], both conditions would be required.

### The Soul.

...because a conjunctive affirmative proposition is opposed to a disjunctive one made of opposite parts. Therefore, to know that the soul is separable <note>Meaning able to exist independently of the body, and thus immortal.</note>, it is necessary that it needs the body neither as a subject <note>The physical matter in which it resides.</note> nor as an object—at least in some of its operations. 

But how can this be known, when Aristotle also says that it is necessary for one who understands to gaze upon some phantasm <note>original: "phantasma." In Aristotelian philosophy, a phantasm is a mental image or representation derived from sensory experience. Aristotle argued that the mind cannot think without these images.</note>? And we experience that we always require phantasms, as each person experiences in themselves; and damage to the [sensory] organs demonstrates this. Furthermore, all those arguments that have been brought against the Commentator <note>Averroes (Ibn Rushd), the 12th-century Islamic philosopher known for his influential commentaries on Aristotle.</note> regarding the immortality of the soul are also against this opinion, since they agree on this point, although they differ in others.

**Fourthly**, it is argued thus: if the human soul depends on a physical organ in every one of its works, it is inseparable and material; but it does depend on an organ in every one of its works, therefore it is material. 

The major premise <note>In a formal argument (syllogism), the major premise is the general rule or broad statement.</note> is clear from Book 1 of *On the Soul* <note>Aristotle's *De Anima*.</note>, where Aristotle says: "if understanding is a form of imagination, or at least not without imagination, it cannot happen that the soul is separated." The minor premise <note>The specific case or observation applied to the general rule.</note>, however, is clear from the universal definition of the soul: namely, that it is the <term>act of a physical organic body</term> <note>original: "actus corporis physici organici." This is Aristotle’s classic definition, suggesting the soul is the "animation" or functional reality of the physical body.</note>.

But perhaps it is said from this side that the human soul, as far as the intellect is concerned, is not the "act" of an organic body, since the intellect is the act of no body, but only...

# On Immortality

...as far as the operations of the sensitive and vegetative <note>The "vegetative" faculty governs basic life functions like growth, while the "sensitive" faculty governs senses and movement.</note> [faculties are concerned].

Truly, this position does not seem able to stand; first, because in this way the intellectual soul <note>original: "anima intellectiua"; the human soul, which possesses the unique power of reason.</note> would not be a soul, since it would not be the act <note>In Aristotelian philosophy, the "act" (actus) or "form" is the principle that gives life, purpose, and structure to matter.</note> of a physical organic body. This contradicts Aristotle, who posits that this is the definition common to every soul; indeed, according to Thomas [Aquinas] himself, this is said univocally <note>original: "vniuocè"; meaning the term "soul" applies in the exact same sense to all types of souls (plants, animals, and humans) regarding their relationship to the body.</note> of all souls.

**Second**, because if the intellectual soul required an organ for the very act of sensing, then as long as the soul existed, it would sense—which clearly appears to be false. The consequence <note>A logical term for the necessary result of a premise.</note> is evident, because when the thing defined exists, those things which are included in its definition must also exist.

**Third**, according to Thomas himself in the first part <note>Referring to the first part of the *Summa Theologiae*.</note> and in the second book of *Against the Gentiles* <note>original: "contra Gentiles"; Thomas Aquinas’s *Summa contra Gentiles*, a work written to defend Christian doctrines against non-believers.</note>, the "intelligences" <note>Purely spiritual beings or angels who possess intellect without having physical bodies.</note> are not the forms of celestial bodies for this very reason: if they were, they would greatly require a body for their intellection <note>The mental process of understanding or thinking.</note>, just like the human soul. Therefore, if the soul is the act of an organic body with respect to sensation—that is, for the sake of its own intellection—it follows that in all its understanding it requires the phantasy <note>original: "phantasia"; the faculty of the mind that retains and works with mental images (phantasms) derived from the senses.</note>. But if this is so, the soul is material; therefore, the intellectual soul is material.

But from this, perhaps it is said that it is not necessary for the intellectual soul to always depend in act <note>Meaning "actually" or "currently" performing a function.</note> upon a physical organ, even though the organ is included in its definition; rather, it suffices that it depends by aptitude <note>A natural fitness or potentiality; an inherent relationship that exists even when not being actively exercised.</note>, just as "being moved upward" is...

For in the 8th book of the *Physics*, text 15, Aristotle attacks Anaxagoras <note>A pre-Socratic philosopher.</note> for positing that the world did not exist for an infinite time and then afterwards began. For nature proceeds either in one way or in several; but if in several, it still proceeds in an orderly fashion. However, there is no order or proportion between the finite and the infinite. Therefore, if the soul is joined to the body for a finite time, but separated for an infinite time, the order of nature will not stand.

### On Immortality

It is said that [the previous statement] contains truth concerning intelligence, because its body is eternal, but the human intellect has a perishable body <note>original: "corpus habet caducum"</note>. Therefore, if the body were destroyed, the intellect itself would either cease to exist—which contradicts our premise—or, if it did exist, it would exist without any activity, since by our premise it could not understand without a **phantasm** <term>phantasm: a mental image or sensory impression that the mind uses as the raw material for thought; in Aristotelian philosophy, the soul cannot think without these images.</term>, and thus it would be idle. 

But this statement does not seem to be reasonably argued. First, because that conclusion does not seem to follow; for although the separated intellect would not be moved by a mental image that is already destroyed <note>i.e., the images stored in its own former body</note>, what would prevent it from being moved by *existing* mental images? For they are all of the same nature <note>original: "eiusdem rationis"</note>, and the intellect itself does not seem to be hindered by distance of place, since it is not located in a specific place. This is especially true since many and most weighty **theologians** affirm that both **angels** and **separated souls** <term>separated souls: souls that have been detached from their physical bodies after death.</term> are moved by these lower things. 

Secondly, that which is deduced does not seem unsuitable—namely, being idle during separation. For when two opposites naturally belong to something, it is not necessary that both belong to it at all times; on the contrary, that is impossible. Just as **sleep** and **wakefulness** naturally belong to an animal, and wakefulness, for example, is appropriate by day, while sleep is by night; therefore, the soul joined [to a body] will understand, but the separated soul will be idle. 

But if it is said to be unsuitable for the soul to work for a very short time and to be idle for an infinite time, truly this response destroys itself, for
