# Of Souls

...if it understands for an infinite time while separated, and does so without a **phantasm** <term>phantasm: a mental image or sensory impression that the mind uses as the raw material for thought; Aristotelian theory usually suggests the mind cannot think without them.</term>, yet understands with a phantasm for only a very brief time <note>i.e., the duration of a human life compared to eternity</note>, it would certainly be more natural for it to understand without a phantasm than with one. Therefore, it is not fitting that the "act of a physical organic body" <note>This refers to Aristotle's famous definition of the soul as the "entelechy" or first act of a physical body having life potentially.</note> is placed in the definition of the soul, etc.

Fifth, because such an essence <note>the soul</note> is truly sensitive and vegetative <note>referring to the lower functions of the soul, such as sensing and growing, which are tied to the body</note>; therefore, either after separation from the body it possesses the powers with which it can perform its tasks, or it does not. If the second is true, it seems against nature that for an eternal time it should be maimed and totally deprived of its functions—unless one resorts to the "resurrection of **Democritus**" <note>Democritus was an atomist; this may refer to the idea of a purely material return to life</note> or to **Pythagorean** fables <note>A reference to metempsychosis, or the transmigration of souls into new bodies</note>. If, however, the soul does retain these powers, yet lacks the physical organs with which to perform its proper work, it follows again that those powers would be useless, a conclusion which **Aristotle** would by no means concede.

Sixth, that such a soul is more truly called immaterial and immortal "in a certain respect" <note>original: "secundùm quid"</note> in the manner explained above, is evident from the **Philosopher** <note>Aristotle</note> himself in Book 3 of *On the Soul* <note>original: "3. de anima"</note> in the chapter on the **agent intellect**. For there, although he had said that both intellects <note>the agent and the potential intellects</note> are separate and immortal, he stated afterward: but *this* intellect—namely, the **agent intellect** <term>agent intellect: the active part of the mind that "lights up" sensory data to create abstract thoughts.</term>—is truly that which is immortal and always thinking; but that one—namely, the **potential intellect** <term>potential intellect: the capacity of the mind to receive or become all things; the "blank slate" of the human mind.</term>—is not, since sometimes it understands and sometimes it does not understand. For it is destroyed when something internal is corrupted, which is because it is joined...

# On Immortality

...of matter, whereby through the destruction <note>original: *corruptionem*</note> of the sensitive part, the soul is destroyed; it will therefore be, in reality, a thing that can perish, yet in a certain respect <note>original: *secundum quid*</note> imperishable, since the intellect which is not joined to matter is imperishable. These things, therefore, create an ambiguity for me regarding the second statement.

The third statement is that such a soul is truly the **form of man**, and not merely his **mover** <note>A classic philosophical distinction: is the soul the "blueprint" (form) of the human being, or just a separate pilot (mover) steering the body?</note>. I certainly agree with this statement if the soul is assumed to be material; however, if it is assumed to be immaterial, as he says, the point does not seem to be established. For it is necessary that such an essence be "this specific thing" <note>original: *hoc aliquid*, a technical term for an individual substance that exists in its own right</note> and self-subsisting. How, then, could it be the **act** and **perfection** of matter <note>In Aristotelian terms, "act" or "perfection" refers to the realization of a body's potential to be alive.</note>, since such a thing—namely, the act of matter—is not "that which exists," but rather "that by which something exists," as is clear in the 7th book of the *Metaphysics* <note>Aristotle's *Metaphysics*, Book VII, examines the nature of substance and form.</note>? 

If it is claimed that this is a unique property of the intellectual soul, this is highly suspect and an arbitrary assertion. In that case, the **Averroists** <term>Averroists: followers of the Islamic philosopher Averroes, who proposed that while the individual body dies, a single "Active Intellect" is shared by all humanity and remains immortal.</term> could likewise say that the intellectual soul is a form that grants **being** <note>existence itself</note> and not just **operation** <note>the capacity for thought</note>—the opposite of what he believes their position to be. There would also be a difficulty concerning the "being" of the composite <note>The "composite" is the whole human being made of both body and soul.</note>, which is posited as distinct from the "being" of the soul: what exactly is that being, and which part is destroyed? Regarding this, although they say many things, I confess that I grasp their words, but not their meaning. Therefore, **Plato** seemed to me to speak wisely when, in positing the soul as immortal, he said that man is more truly...

<summary>The author argues that if the soul is an immaterial, self-subsisting substance, it cannot logically be defined as the "act" or "form" of a physical body. He critiques the inconsistency of Aristotelian thinkers who try to maintain both the soul's immortality and its role as the body's essential form.</summary>

# On Souls

...the soul using a body, rather than a composite of soul and body, and more truly its **mover** <note>original: *motor*</note>—that is, the mover of the body—than its **form**. This is because the soul is that which truly is and truly exists, and it can clothe itself in a body or be stripped of it. I do not see, in fact, how even Saint Thomas <note>Thomas Aquinas (1225–1274), the most influential medieval theologian who attempted to reconcile Aristotelian philosophy with Christian doctrine.</note> could avoid saying this.

The fourth point concerned the **multiplication of souls** <note>original: *multiplicatione animarum*. This refers to the philosophical problem of "individuation": if all humans share one "human nature," what makes my soul distinct from yours? Traditionally, Aristotle argued that matter (the body) is what divides a species into individuals.</note>, which is no less doubtful to me. In the 12th book of the *Metaphysics*, text 49, Aristotle says that such multiplication occurs through "much matter." As for what is said by some—that souls are distinguished by their "relationships to different matters," or that Aristotle is speaking about **individuating principles** <note>The specific factors that make one thing different from another of the same kind; here the author argues these are "new inventions" not found in the original Greek texts.</note> which can be called "matter"—these seem to me to be mere entanglements and new inventions created to support a specific position. They are in no way according to the mind of Aristotle. If we followed that logic, we could multiply **Intelligences** <note>Immaterial, celestial beings in medieval philosophy, often equated with angels, which were thought to be unique species unto themselves.</note> within the same species, or even God Himself! I leave it to others to consider how well that harmonizes with the **Peripatetics** <note>The followers of Aristotle, so named after the "covered walkways" (*peripatoi*) in Athens where they held their discussions.</note>. 

And although some might grant this regarding the Intelligences, yet deny it regarding God by bringing forward new arguments for why God cannot be multiplied, it is certain that Aristotle neither made nor saw those arguments. God knows how much they are worth within the Aristotelian system; rather, he simply leans on the higher principle. Furthermore, because according to the principles of Aristo—

->**Soul**<-

...to say; first, because everything **incorruptible** <term>incorruptibile: that which cannot decay or be destroyed</term> is **unbegotten** <term>ingenitum: having no beginning or birth</term>. In the first book of *On the Heavens*, text 125, Aristotle proves their **convertibility** <note>The logical principle that if one trait is true, the other must also be true: if something has no end, it must have no beginning.</note>. However, by way of concessions <note>referring to premises granted for the sake of argument</note>, if the **intellective soul** is incorruptible, then it is unbegotten; therefore it never began, which is the opposite of what is conceded <note>Christian doctrine holds that the soul is created by God at a specific moment, thus having a beginning but no end. The author is highlighting a contradiction in using Aristotle to support this.</note>.

But to this, some respond by denying the final consequence—that is, "therefore it never began." They claim it only follows that it never began through **true generation** <note>meaning the soul isn't "born" through natural biological reproduction like the body is</note>.

But this clearly contradicts the text, as I also noted when I was explaining that passage. For Aristotle says: "I call **unbegotten** that which is, and of which it was never true to say that it 'was' [previously not existing]" <note>original: *ingenitum voco quod est, & nunquam fuit verum dicere quod fuit*. Aristotle’s argument in *De Caelo* is that if something exists now but once did not, it is "begotten."</note>. If, therefore, the intellective soul is "unbegotten" in the sense the Philosopher <note>A common medieval and Renaissance title for Aristotle.</note> uses there, then it was never true to say that it "was not." 

For as is expressly clear, he intends "begotten" to mean not only that which has true causes of physical birth, but whatever **begins to be**, in whatever way it begins. He wants this term to be convertible with "corruptible" <note>perishable</note> when taken proportionally, and in this way he uses the terms "unbegotten" and "incorruptible."

Furthermore, it is a wonder that Aristotle would bring forward so many and such great reasons to prove the convertibility of those terms and yet never made an exception for the intellective soul. He would have given a very great cause for error. Furthermore, regarding the claim made further on—that souls remain after death—the fact that Aristotle makes no mention of them seems very improbable, since Aristotle was such a diligent investigator of nature...

# On Immortality

...[the author] was so diligent in Poetry, in Rhetoric, and in many other subjects, and yet so negligent in such an excellent matter <note>The author is critiquing the idea that Aristotle, usually so thorough, would have ignored the soul's immortality if he actually believed in it.</note>. Furthermore, because in the first book of the *Ethics* <note>Aristotle’s *Nicomachean Ethics*</note>, he seems to posit no happiness after death; indeed, what is more, Saint Thomas <note>original: *D. Thomas*, referring to Thomas Aquinas</note> in the first lecture on the third book of the *Ethics*, concerning the section where one ought to choose death rather than commit a great crime <term>scelus: a wicked act or grave sin</term>, speaks as if he is doubtful of how Aristotle could justify this. Thomas says that Aristotle said this because glory remains after death, or because he considers persisting in that virtuous act for a brief time better than living for a long time in a state of vice. 

Now, if Saint Thomas believed that Aristotle held to the immortality of the soul, neither of these reasons would be appropriate; for the answer would be readily apparent: [one chooses death] because of the other state <note>the afterlife</note>. Hence, Saint Thomas speaks as if admitting that since Aristotle posits that nothing exists after death, one wonders how he can wish for someone to prefer death over living wickedly. For Thomas himself, in his exposition of the *Apostles' Creed* <term>Symbolum Apostolorum: the foundational statement of Christian belief</term> regarding the resurrection of the flesh, says that if we did not expect to rise again, a person ought to commit any crime whatsoever rather than die. 

Furthermore, as was said above, it would be remarkable if Aristotle had made no mention of such a state after death, nor promised to explain it, which is nevertheless contrary to his usual habit <note>Aristotle typically promises to revisit difficult topics in later books; the author notes he never does this for the afterlife.</note>. Besides, it is necessary either to posit a resurrection, or to invent Pythagorean fables <note>referring to the "fables" of Pythagoras regarding the transmigration of souls, or reincarnation</note>; or else for such noble beings <note>human souls</note> to remain idle—all of which...

### *In which the fifth mode is set forth, namely that the same essence of the soul is mortal and immortal; yet simply mortal, and immortal only in a certain respect.*

### CHAPTER IX

Since, therefore, the first mode—which posits that the intellective part <term>intellectivum: the capacity for rational thought</term> is really distinct from the sensitive part <term>sensitivum: the capacity for sensory perception</term> in mortal beings—has now been attacked by everyone; and since the second mode—which posits that the intellective and sensitive are the same in reality, and that such a thing is simply <term>simpliciter: absolutely or essentially</term> immortal and mortal only in a certain respect <term>secundum quid: in a limited or qualified sense</term>—is very ambiguous and does not seem to agree with Aristotle; it remains for us to set forth the final mode. This mode, positing that the sensitive in man is identified with the intellective, says that this is essentially and truly mortal, but immortal in a certain respect. And so that we may proceed in order, we shall speak according to those five points stated in the previous chapter.

<header>On Immortality</header>

...[the soul is] such a thing [that is] in a qualified sense and improperly immortal. 

For the clarity of this, it must be known—and very much committed to memory in this matter—that since all knowledge <term>cognitio: the process of knowing or perceiving</term> abstracts from matter in some way, matter itself hinders knowledge, as the Commentator <note>original: "Commentator" — this refers to Averroes, the 12th-century Islamic philosopher whose commentaries on Aristotle were foundational to Western university study</note> says in his 5th commentary on the third book of *On the Soul*. And this can be seen in the senses, which do not know things according to their real [physical] qualities, but according to their "intentions" <term>intentio: the mental representation or "image" of an object rather than its physical bulk</term>. This is why it is said in the second book of *On the Soul* that it is characteristic of every sense to be receptive of "species" <term>species: the "forms" or mental patterns of things</term> without matter. 

Therefore, according to the three modes of separation from matter, three modes of knowledge are found throughout the universe. For there are some things which are totally separated from matter; thus, in their act of knowing, these require a body neither as a subject <note>The "subject" is the thing in which the knowledge exists, like a brain or a soul</note> nor as an object <note>The "object" is the thing being thought about</note>. For their knowledge is not received in a body, since they themselves are not in a body, nor are they moved by a body, since they are "movers that are not moved" <note>An Aristotelian concept describing beings that cause change or motion in the world without being changed or moved themselves</note>. These are the "separate substances" <term>substantiae separatae: purely spiritual beings, such as angels or celestial intelligences</term>, which we call "intellects" or "intelligences." In them, neither discourse <note>Step-by-step logical reasoning</note>, nor composition <note>Combining different ideas</note>, nor any movement is found. 

However, there are other things which, while they do not know through sensible qualities but rather through their "species" (which take on a certain mode of immateriality, and are indeed called "without matter" and are spiritual), nevertheless, because they are the lowest in the category of knowing beings and are very material... <note>The text cuts off here at the end of the page</note>

<summary>The author explains that knowledge requires "abstracting" from matter and categorizes different types of beings based on how far removed their knowledge is from physical bodies. He begins with "separate substances" (pure intelligences) and moves toward lower forms of perception.</summary>

These are all the sensitive powers, although some of them are more spiritual and some less, as the Commentator <note>Averroes, the influential 12th-century Islamic philosopher</note> says in his 6th commentary on the third book of *On the Soul*, and in his book *On the Sense and the Sensible*. 

Since nature proceeds in an orderly fashion—as is said in the 8th book of the *Physics* <note>Aristotle’s work on the laws of nature</note>—between these two extremes (namely, not needing a body as either subject or object, and needing a body as both subject and object), there is a middle ground that is neither totally abstract <term>abstractum: separated from matter</term> nor totally immersed in matter. 

Therefore, since it cannot happen that something needs a body as a subject but not as an object (as is obvious), it follows that such a middle ground does not need the body as a subject, but rather as an object. This, however, is the **human intellect**, which according to all the ancient and modern [philosophers] is placed in the middle between things that are abstract and things that are not—namely, between the [celestial] Intelligences and the sensitive level, existing below the Intelligences and above the sensitive things. Which is why it is also said in the Psalm: 

> You have made him a little lower than the Angels; <note>Psalm 8:5</note>

and a little later: 

> You have set him over the works of your hands: all sheep and oxen, etc. <note>Psalm 8:6-7</note>

# On Immortality.

...[imagination], or at least not without imagination, this cannot happen without a body. And since in the third book of *On the Soul* <note>Aristotle's *De Anima*</note> he declared that thinking is not the same as imagination, since it is not organic <note>meaning it does not use a physical organ to perform its specific function of reasoning</note>, and yet it cannot exist without imagination (as seen in texts 29 and 39 of the same work, the soul never thinks without a mental image <term>phantasmate: a sensory representation or "picture" in the mind</term>): therefore, the human soul does not need a physical organ as its **subject** <note>the physical base where the power of the soul is "seated"</note>, but it does need it as an **object** <note>the source of information the soul thinks about</note>.

According to Aristotle and Plato, all these levels of knowledge belong to the nature of souls; therefore, at least according to Aristotle, every knowing thing is the **act** <term>actus: the formal or life-giving principle that makes a body functional</term> of an organic physical body, but in different ways. For to the Intelligences <note>the high spirits or movers of the celestial spheres</note>, they <unclear>are</unclear> not acts of a body insofar as they are intellects, because in their thinking and desiring they in no way need a body. But insofar as they actuate and move the heavenly bodies, in that sense they are souls, and are the acts of an organic physical body. For a star is the organ of the heaven (as stated in Book 2 of *On the Heavens* and Book 12 of the *Metaphysics*, text 48: "every sphere exists for the sake of the star"). Therefore, they actuate an organic physical body, and in this way they need a body as an object; however, in so actuating and moving, they receive nothing from the body, but only give to it.

The sensitive soul <note>the type of soul possessed by animals, governed by the five senses</note>, however, is simply the act of an organic physical body, because it needs the body both as a **subject**—since it cannot perform its duty except within an organ—and it needs the body as an **object**. 

The middle ground, however, which is the human intellect, is in none of its works totally freed from the body, nor is it totally immer... <note>original: "immer-", likely "immersum" (immersed)</note>

<summary>The author explains that while animal souls are tied to physical organs and celestial spirits only move bodies, the human intellect sits in the middle: it does not use a physical organ to think, but it still requires sensory images to provide the material for its thoughts.</summary>

<header>Souls.</header>

Therefore, the soul will not need the body as a **subject** <note>original: "subiecto"; the physical matter or organ where a power is "seated"</note>, but as an **object** <note>the source of sensory images for the mind to process</note>, and thus in a middle way between abstract and non-abstract things, it will be the **act** <term>actus: the formal or life-giving principle that makes a body functional</term> of an organic body. For the Intelligences <note>spiritual beings or "movers" responsible for the motion of the stars</note>, insofar as they are Intelligences, are not souls, because in that capacity they do not depend on the body in any way; rather, they are souls only insofar as they move the celestial bodies. 

But the human intellect, in all its work, is the **act** of an organic body, since it always depends on the body as an **object**. There is also a difference between an Intelligence and the human intellect in how they depend on an organ: for the human intellect receives information and is perfected through a physical object, since it is moved by it. However, an Intelligence receives nothing from a celestial body, but only gives to it. 

Furthermore, the human intellect differs from the sensitive power <note>the senses of animals and humans</note> in its dependence on the body, because the sensitive power depends both **subjectively** and **objectively** <note>meaning the senses are physically located in an organ and require a physical thing to perceive</note>, while the human intellect depends only **objectively**. And thus, in a middle way between material and immaterial things, the human intellect is the **act** of an organic body. 

For this reason, celestial bodies, humans, and beasts are not "animals" in the same way, since their souls are not the **acts** of an organic physical body in one and the same way, as has been shown. Therefore, Alexander <note>Alexander of Aphrodisias, an influential commentator on Aristotle</note>, in his paraphrase of *On the Soul*, said that the term "soul of the heaven" is used quite **equivocally** <note>original: "aequivoce"; meaning the word "soul" is being used with a different meaning than usual</note> when applied to an Intelligence, and that the heaven is called an "animal" in that same sense. Averroes <note>a famous 12th-century philosopher</note> also seems to agree with this in his book *On the Substance of the Orb*. In truth, however, beasts are properly called "animals," just as is the common usage of speak—

<header>On Immortality</header>

...divine <note>original: "di-", completing a word from the previous page, likely "divini"</note>, while in a middle way, humans are called animals. Nor should we imagine, according to Aristotle, that this way of understanding used by the human intellect is accidental <note>non-essential or a temporary trait</note> to it—namely, being moved by an object while not requiring a physical subject <note>a physical organ where the thought is "seated"</note>. This is partly because the way a thing operates is essential to its nature, and partly because a "mode" <note>a specific way of being or acting</note> is fixed. Just as the sensitive mode <note>the way animals perceive through senses</note> is never transformed into the mode of pure Intelligence <note>the way angels or celestial movers think</note> or into the human intellect, nor is the mode of pure Intelligence transformed into the human or sensitive mode, so likewise the human mode of understanding does not seem capable of being transformed into the mode of pure Intelligence. This transformation would only happen if the intellect could understand without needing the body as both a subject and an object. This is further confirmed because, if essential operations were changed, one nature would be transformed into an entirely different nature.

Furthermore, no natural sign allows us to know that the human intellect has any other way of understanding, as we grasp through experience, since we always require a **phantasm** <term>phantasmate: a mental image or "picture" derived from the senses which the mind uses to think</term>. Therefore, it is concluded that this mode of understanding through a phantasm is essential to man. From these points, the main intended conclusion must now be framed as a **syllogism** <note>a formal logical argument consisting of a major premise, a minor premise, and a conclusion</note>: namely, that the human soul is **absolutely** <term>simpliciter: in an unqualified or essential sense</term> material, and **in a certain respect** <term>secundum quid: in a limited or qualified sense</term> immaterial.

First, through a **divisive syllogism** <note>an "either/or" logical structure</note>, it is argued thus: the human intellect is both immaterial and material, as is clear from what has been established; but it does not participate in these two qualities equally, nor is it more...

<summary>The author argues that the human intellect's reliance on sensory images (phantasms) is an essential, unchangeable trait that defines human nature as a middle ground. He begins a formal logical proof to conclude that the soul is essentially material, though it possesses some immaterial qualities.</summary>
<keywords>Aristotle, human intellect, phantasm, materiality of the soul, syllogism, essential operations, cognitive modes</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the logical progression of the argument regarding the human soul’s nature. On the previous page, the author explored the soul's "middle" position between purely material things and purely immaterial Intelligences. The text here concludes a syllogism regarding the soul's materiality before moving into a series of arguments based on Aristotle’s *De Anima* and *Metaphysics* to suggest that, from a strictly philosophical (Aristotelian) perspective, the soul must be considered mortal.</meta>

<page-num>57</page-num>
<header>The Soul</header>

...[more] immaterial than material, as was proven in the preceding chapter; therefore, it is more material than immaterial, and thus in an absolute sense <note>original: "simpliciter"</note> it will be material, and in a qualified sense <note>original: "secundum quid"</note> immaterial.

Secondly, it is essential for the intellect to understand through **phantasms** <term>phantasmata: mental images or "pictures" derived from sensory experience</term> as has been shown, and this is evident from the definition of the soul, since it is the "act of a physical organic body"; therefore, in every operation, it requires an organ. But that which understands in this way is by necessity inseparable from the body; therefore, the human intellect is mortal.

The minor premise is evident, both from Aristotle saying that if understanding is a form of imagination <note>original: "phantasia"</note>, or at least not possible without imagination, then it is impossible for the soul to be separated from the body. It is also evident because if the soul were separable, it would either have no operation (and thus be idle and useless) or it would have one (and thus still operate through a phantasm, which contradicts the major premise already demonstrated).

And again, this is further confirmed because Aristotle did not posit any Intelligence <term>Intelligentiam: in this context, a celestial being or "mover" that controls the heavens</term> without a body, since in Book 12 of the *Metaphysics*, he sets the number of Intelligences according to the number of the celestial spheres. Therefore, much less can he posit the human intellect existing without a body, since it is far less abstract <note>further removed from pure spirit and more tied to matter</note> than a celestial Intelligence. Indeed, if the world is eternal, as Aristotle believed, then an infinite number of infinite forms <note>the souls of the infinite number of people who would have lived in an eternal past</note> would currently exist in actuality without bodies, which Aristotle considers to be a ridiculous conclusion. Therefore, according to Aristotle, the human soul must be pronounced absolutely mortal; but since it is...

<summary>This page argues that if the intellect requires sensory images (phantasms) to function, it cannot exist apart from the physical body. Citing Aristotle's Metaphysics, the author concludes that a strictly Aristotelian view requires the soul to be mortal, as the alternative leads to the "ridiculous" idea of an infinite number of disembodied souls.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the argument regarding the human soul's unique position in the hierarchy of being. Having established that the soul is a "middle" nature—neither purely material nor purely immaterial—the author now explains how this dual nature allows it to participate in both immortality and mortality. He introduces the critical Scholastic distinction between needing the body as a "subject" (physical residence) and as an "object" (source of data).</meta>

<page-num>58</page-num>
# On Immortality

...standing in the middle between things that are purely abstract <note>original: "abstracta simpliciter," referring to purely spiritual beings like angels</note> and those that are immersed in matter, the soul also participates in immortality in a certain way. This is shown by its essential operation; for the soul does not depend on the body as its **subject** <term>subiectum: the underlying physical matter that sustains a quality or power, like the way a wall "supports" the color white</term>, in which respect it agrees with the Intelligences <note>original: "intelligentiis," referring to the celestial movers or angels</note>. However, it differs from the beasts because it requires the body as an **object** <term>obiectum: the thing toward which an operation is directed, such as a mental image used for thinking</term>, in which respect it agrees with the beasts; wherefore it is also mortal. To have a complete understanding of what has been said, one must know what it means to need an organ as a subject, to need it as an object, and to not need it at all.

To need an organ as a **subject** is to be received within the body in a quantitative and corporal manner, such that it is received with **extension** <term>extensio: the physical property of occupying space</term>. This is how we say all organic powers are received and perform their functions—just as the eye functions by seeing and the ear by hearing. For vision exists within the eye in an extended way. Therefore, not to be in an organ, or to not require it "subjectively," means either to not be in the body at all, or to not be in it in a quantitative way.

Hence, we say that the intellect does not need the body as a subject in its act of understanding. This is not because the act of understanding is in no way within the body—which would be impossible if the intellect is in the body, as its own immanent operation must also be in the body in some way; for where the subject is, the accidents of that subject must also be. Rather, the act of understanding is said "not to be in the organ and in the body" specifically because it does not exist within it in a quantitative and corporal manner.

<meta>catchword: therefore</meta>

<summary>This page defines the soul's "middle" nature by distinguishing between 'subjective' and 'objective' dependence on the body. It argues that while the intellect resides in the body, its operation is not 'extended' or physical like sight or hearing, allowing it to transcend pure materiality.</summary>

<keywords>intellect, soul, immortality, subject vs object, extension, Scholasticism, corporal organs, Intelligences</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the unique nature of the human intellect. Following the distinction made on the previous page regarding the body as a "subject" versus an "object," the author now explains how the intellect's ability to reflect upon itself proves its independence from matter, while its reliance on mental images (phantasms) demonstrates its ongoing connection to the physical body.</meta>

<page-num>59</page-num>
<header>The Soul.</header>

The intellect is able to reflect upon itself, to reason discursively <term>discurrere: "running to and fro," or the process of moving from one premise to a conclusion rather than knowing everything at once</term>, and to comprehend universally, which organic and extended powers <note>Refers to the physical senses, like sight or touch, which are "extended" because they occupy physical space in a specific organ.</note> are by no means able to do. All of this arises from the essence of the intellect; for insofar as it is intellect, it does not depend on matter, nor on quantity. If the human intellect does depend on matter, it is only because it is joined to the senses; therefore, it happens to the intellect only incidentally—and not by its own nature—to depend on matter and quantity.

Hence, its operation is no more abstract than its essence. For unless the intellect possessed the capacity to exist by itself without matter, the act of understanding itself could only be performed in a quantitative and corporal manner. But although the human intellect, as has been maintained, does not make use of quantity while it is in the act of understanding, nevertheless, because it is joined to the senses, it cannot be entirely freed from matter and quantity. This is because it never knows anything without a **phantasm** <term>phantasma: a mental image or "picture" derived from the senses that the mind uses as a basis for thought</term>.

As Aristotle says in the third book of *On the Soul* <note>original: "3. de anima"</note>, the soul never understands without a phantasm. Because it needs the body as an **object** in this way, it cannot know the **universal** <term>universale: a general concept, like "Humanity," as opposed to a specific individual like "Socrates"</term> simply and purely. Instead, it always contemplates the universal within the singular <note>The idea that even when we think of "Triangle" in the abstract, our minds still "see" a specific, individual triangle.</note>, as everyone can experience within themselves. For in every cognition, no matter how abstract, the mind forms for itself some corporal image <note>original: "idolum," here meaning a mental representation</note>. Because of this, the human intellect does not understand itself first or directly, and it composes and reasons. Therefore, its way of understanding is with

This "middle" status means humans understand abstract concepts by viewing them through the lens of specific, physical examples.</summary>

continuity and time, the exact opposite of which occurs in the **Intelligences** <term>intelligentiae: in medieval philosophy, these are purely spiritual beings, like angels, who possess intellect without any physical body</term> which are entirely freed from matter. Therefore, the intellect itself, existing as a medium between the immaterial and the material, is neither entirely "here and now," nor is it entirely released from the "here and now." For this reason, its operation is neither entirely universal nor entirely particular; it is neither entirely subject to time, nor is it entirely removed from time.

Nature has proceeded rightly and orderly in this way, so that it might arrive from the first things to the extremes by way of the middle. For since the Intelligences are simply abstract <note>original: "abstractae," meaning pulled away or separated from physical reality.</note>, they in no way need a body as a **subject** <note>As a physical location or organ where the thinking happens.</note>, or as an **object** <note>As a source of raw data or images to think about.</note> for the purpose of understanding. Therefore, they know nature simply, first by understanding themselves and through a simple intuition; for this reason, they are released from both time and continuity.

The **sensitive powers** <note>The physical senses, like sight or hearing, which belong to animals and the "lower" part of the human soul.</note>, however, since they are immersed in matter, know only in a singular way <note>They can only perceive specific, individual things (like "this red flower") rather than general concepts.</note>, without reflecting upon themselves or reasoning discursively. But the human intellect, just as it exists as a medium in its being, so it also exists in its operation, as was said before; hence, the things received within it are neither entirely **potentially understood**, nor **actually understood** <note>The author is describing the middle state of human thought: we aren't born knowing everything, but we aren't purely ignorant either; we are in a constant state of moving from the possibility of knowledge to the act of knowing.</note>. 

It is clear, then, what it means to need a body as a subject and as an object, and which things need them and which do not, and in what way. Furthermore, it should be known that whoever maintains that the soul is immortal and **multiplied** <note>The "multiplication" of souls refers to the idea that every individual human has their own distinct, eternal soul, rather than all humans sharing one single "Universal Mind."</note> says that because it is of an imma- <note>The text breaks here; the word is completed as "immaterial" on the following page.</note>

<meta>This page continues the discussion from page 60 regarding the human soul's status as a "middle" being. <page-num>61</page-num>
<header>->The Soul.<-</header>

[imma]terial, it is itself a **this-something** <term>hoc aliquid: a technical term in Aristotelian philosophy referring to an individual, independently existing thing or substance</term> subsisting through itself. For this reason, it can both exist and operate without a body. When it exists in such a separated state, it possesses no powers of the soul except for **intellect** and **will**, just like the **Intelligences** <note>original: "intelligentiae," referring to purely spiritual beings like angels</note>. Consequently, it possesses none of the **sensitive** <note>the Five Senses</note> or **vegetative** <note>biological functions like growth and nutrition</note> powers, except perhaps as a very remote potential principle.

But because the soul is the lowest among the **abstract substances** <note>spiritual beings that exist apart from matter</note>, it possesses another mode of being beyond that one: it can also be that **by which something is made**. For this reason, it can truly **inform** <term>informare: in medieval philosophy, this means to give "form" or essential nature to matter, turning a body into a living human</term> the body itself. Due to its own imperfection <note>relative to higher spirits</note>, it is multiplied according to the number of bodies. It takes on all the powers of sensation and growth so that it may exercise them through a physical organ, and in this way, it becomes **subject to mortality** <note>original: "morticina," literally meaning "of a carcass" or "dying"; here implying the soul shares in the body’s corruptible nature</note>.

However, although the soul possesses **intellect** and **will** while joined to the body in this way, it is not able to exercise them freely, because it cannot perform its duty without a bodily instrument—at least not without a physical image as its **object** <note>The "mental images" or phantasms derived from the senses</note>. The opposite occurs during separation, because then it can move into action entirely without a bodily organ.

But there is another opinion which considers these ideas to be mere **delusions** <note>original: "deliramenta"</note> and contrary to the principles of Philosophy. This view holds it impossible for the same thing to be both a "this-something" subsisting through itself and also the "form" by which another thing is made. It argues that these involve incompatible modes of operating, and that the "separated" mode of existence is proven by no reason or experience, but is asserted by **will** <note>i.e., by mere preference or faith rather than logic</note> alone. Furthermore, it questions how the soul can possess sensitive and vegetative powers at one time, while at another...

...carrying them along, being understood in one way as **joined** to the body and in another as **separate**. It would be joined for the briefest moment of time, but separate for an infinite duration—unless we were to imagine the **transmigration of souls** <term>transmigratio: the belief that a soul moves from one body to another after death</term> into the body, and that this process had a beginning but would never end, now putting on a body, now stripping it off, as the common folk tell of **witches** <note>original: "lamiis," referring to mythical female spirits or witches said to haunt or possess bodies</note>. 

Furthermore, when the soul is separated from the body, it ceases to actually be the **act of the body** <term>actus corporis: the formal principle that gives life and "human-ness" to matter</term>. Therefore, it is either nowhere, or if it is somewhere, how does it arrive there? For it must move either by **alteration** <note>a change in quality or state</note> or by **local motion** <note>physical movement from one place to another</note>. It is not by alteration, as is obvious. Nor is it by local motion, since according to the **sixth book of the Physics** <note>Aristotle's *Physics*, Book VI, where he argues that an indivisible thing—something without parts—cannot move in the same way physical objects do</note>, that which is **indivisible** cannot be moved locally. 

If it is placed nowhere, what then prevents us, according to **Aristotle**, from also positing certain **Intelligences** <note>purely spiritual beings, such as those thought to move the celestial spheres</note> that do not move the heavenly spheres? That multitude would then be posited as infinite an infinite number of times. One could not even know if such a soul were idle or at work, unless some **purpose** or **will** were posited. 

Since **material things** cannot be actually infinite—though in them, multiplication is manifest and necessary—and since multiplication is also posited in **immaterial things** where it is neither necessary nor even possible (because distinction within the same species is impossible), an **actual infinite multitude** would be the result. 

Wherefore, since all these conclusions seem irrational and foreign to the mind of **Aristotle**, it seems more reasonable to conclude that the **human soul**, since it is the highest and most perfect of **material forms**, is truly that by which some...

what this "particular thing" <term>this particular thing: translating the Latin "hoc aliquid," a philosophical term for a distinct, individual substance</term> is, and in no way is the soul truly such a "particular thing" on its own; wherefore it is truly a **form** that begins and ends its existence simultaneously with the **body**. Nor can it in any way operate or exist without the body, and it possesses only one way of being or operating. For this reason, it can also be **multiplied**, since matter is truly the principle of multiplication within the same species. Nor are souls infinite in **act**, but only in **potential**, just like all other material things. 

Furthermore, the soul possesses organic and simply material powers—namely the **sensitive** <note>the ability to perceive through the five senses</note> and the **vegetative** <note>the basic biological functions of growth and nutrition</note>. However, since the soul is the most noble of material things and sits on the **boundary** of the immaterial, it "catches a scent" <note>original: "odorat"; a metaphorical way of saying the soul has a faint perception or hint of a higher nature</note> of something of **immateriality**, though not in a simple or pure sense. From this, it possesses **intellect** and **will**, in which it agrees with the **Gods** <note>likely referring to the "Intelligences" or celestial beings in the Aristotelian tradition</note>, though quite imperfectly and equivocally. 

This is because the Gods themselves are totally abstracted from **matter**, whereas the human soul is always joined with matter; for it perceives through **mental images** <term>phantasms: the images or "pictures" the mind forms based on sensory input</term>, through continuity, through time, through discourse <note>step-by-step reasoning rather than instant intuition</note>, and through obscurity. Therefore, in us, the intellect and will are not purely immaterial, but only in a certain respect and in a diminished way. Because of this, our capacity is more truly called **reason** than **intellect**. For it is not, so to speak, the intellect itself, but a **trace and a shadow** of the intellect. To this, that which is said in the second book of the **Metaphysics** <note>Aristotle's *Metaphysics*, Book II (Alpha Minor)</note> bears witness: just as the eye of the **night-owl** <note>original: "nycticoracis"; a famous Aristotelian metaphor for the human mind's struggle to grasp divine truths</note> relates to the light of the sun, so does our intellect relate to the most manifest things in nature, although per—

<page-num>64</page-num>

...explained that in a different way. Just as the moon is of the nature of the earth, as Aristotle says in his book *On the Soul* <note>original: "de anima"</note>, so the human soul is of the nature of **intelligence**. However, in the Moon, "earth" exists only according to its properties, and not according to its essence; therefore, the act of understanding exists in the human soul through a participation in that property, and not by its essence. All of this agrees with nature, which proceeds in gradual steps.

For **plants** <term>vegetabilia: the "vegetative" level of life, encompassing all plant life</term> possess something of a soul, since they operate within themselves. However, they do so in a very material way, since they do not perform their functions except through the **primary qualities** <note>the four basic physical qualities of Aristotelian physics: heat, cold, moisture, and dryness</note>, and their operations are limited to their physical existence. 

Next follow the **animals** that possess only the senses of touch and taste, and an indeterminate imagination. After these are the animals that reach such a high degree of perfection that we might think they possess **intellect**. For many work "mechanically," such as by building shelters; many "civilly," like bees; and many possess almost all the moral virtues, as is clear to anyone looking at the books of the *History of Animals* <note>Aristotle’s *Historia Animalium*, a foundational text on animal biology and behavior</note>. In those books, such wonders are recorded that it would be too long to repeat them here; indeed, almost countless men seem to possess less intellect than many beasts. 

The **cogitative power** <term>cogitativa: in medieval psychology, this is the highest "internal sense" that allows an individual to judge and compare specific perceptions</term> is also placed among the sensitive powers. What more need be said of its excellence, when according to the **Commentator** <note>Averroes (Ibn Rushd), the 12th-century philosopher known simply as "The Commentator" for his influential work on Aristotle</note> in his second book on *On the Soul* (commentary 60), this power can recognize individuals across all ten categories <term>the ten categories: Aristotle’s classification of all possible kinds of being, such as substance, quantity, and quality</term>, and is identified by everyone as "particular reason"?

<page-num>65</page-num>
<header>The Soul.</header>

...indeed, **Galen**, **Homer**, and many other excellent men have believed that the soul is the intellect itself. If we ascend slightly higher, we shall place the **human intellect** <note>original: "intellectus"</note> immediately above the **cogitative power** <term>cogitativa: the highest physical power of the brain in Aristotelian thought, which handles specific images and "intentions" but lacks the abstract power of the intellect</term> and below purely immaterial things. 

It participates in both realms: it does not require the body as a *subject* <note>original: "subiecto"</note> (that is, it does not exist in a physical organ like the sense of sight exists in the eye) in the manner previously explained, yet it does require the body as an *object* (since it needs the images provided by the senses to think). This relationship is essential and inseparable for the soul. Therefore, it must be placed entirely among the material forms. This is proven by the fact that it only interacts with mortals—unless we imagine, like the **Ancients**, that men become gods and are carried up to heaven. Aristotle, in the 3rd and 12th books of his *Metaphysics*, considered all such stories to be fables, and believed they were invented by lawmakers for the benefit of human society.

No inconsistency seems to follow from this position; everything harmonizes with reason and experience, and nothing is proposed that is mythical or based solely on faith. If there are objections to this view—such as how the soul, if it exists in hot and cold matter, can avoid being physically altered by those qualities, or why it does not use a physical organ for its own specific work, or how it can receive universal truths—certainly these and similar arguments are quite weak. They create no less difficulty for the previous position, since that other view also admits that the soul is a form existing within matter.

If anyone should say that neither opinion is true, but rather that of **Averroes** <note>Ibn Rushd, the influential commentator who argued that there is only one "Potential Intellect" shared by all humanity</note>, truly, in my view, whoever imagines that opinion must possess a very powerful imagi[nation]...

<meta>This page continues the investigation into the nature of the human intellect, specifically addressing how to reconcile Aristotle’s seemingly contradictory definitions of the soul as both "separate" from and "connected" to the physical body.</meta>

<page-num>66</page-num>
->### On Immortality<-

...imagination. I believe that painters have never fashioned a more beautiful monster <note>original: "monstrum"</note> than this particular idea, since this view also goes against **Aristotle**, as was demonstrated above. Therefore, the position I have just recounted seems to me more probable than all others, and more in line with the mind of **Aristotle**.

From all this, it can be clearly seen that many things said by **Aristotle** regarding the **intellect** <note>original: "intellectu"</note> seem to oppose one another, though in truth they do not conflict at all. For sometimes he says that it is material and mixed—that is, inseparable—while at other times he says it is immaterial and separable. In his formal definition, he says it is the **actualization** <term>actus: the "form" or "functioning" of a thing that makes it what it is; here, the soul is the "act" that makes a body alive and thinking</term> of an organic body, yet elsewhere he says it is the act of no body. These points certainly seem to clash.

Because of this, different thinkers have wandered down different paths, and some even believe that **Aristotle** did not understand himself. However, everything is now made clear by what has been stated previously, and there is no contradiction. For the intellect, considered purely and in its essence as an intellect, is entirely unmixed and separate. Yet the **human intellect** retains both natures: it is separated from the body as a **subject** <note>original: "subiecto"; meaning the intellect does not physically inhabit a specific organ the way sight inhabits the eye</note>, but it is not separated from the body as an **object** <note>original: "obiecto"; meaning it requires the physical images provided by the senses to have something to think about</note>.

Furthermore, the intellect as such is in no way the actualization of an organic body, because the **Intelligences** <note>original: "intelligentiae"; referring to higher celestial or divine beings in the Aristotelian hierarchy</note> do not require a physical organ for the purpose of understanding, but only for the purpose of movement. But the human intellect, precisely as *human*, is the actualization of an organic body as its object—and in this sense, it is not separate. However, it is not the act of the body as its subject—and in this sense, it is separate. Wherefore...

<summary>This page resolves the "Aristotelian contradiction" by arguing that the human intellect is unique: it does not live in an organ (separate as a subject) but cannot function without sensory data (inseparable as an object).</summary>

# The Soul.

...nothing contradictory <note>original: "nullum repugnans"</note>.

In the third statement, namely that the **soul** is truly the **form** <term>forma: in Aristotelian philosophy, the "form" is the organizing principle or essence that makes a thing what it is; the soul is the "form" of the body</term> of man, it appears that this is preserved far better according to this method than the previous one. For, as was stated, it is very difficult to imagine that something existing entirely on its own could truly be a "form." For this reason, **Saint Gregory of Nyssa**, as **Saint Thomas Aquinas** reports of him, when he saw **Aristotle** calling the soul the **actualization** <term>actus: the completion or functioning of a potentiality; here, the soul is what makes a physical body "act" as a living human</term> of the body, claimed that **Aristotle** believed the human soul was mortal. He argued this because a form that exists independently cannot truly be the actualization of a physical, organic body. Indeed, some say that **Gregory of Nazianzus** held this same opinion regarding **Aristotle**.

Regarding the fourth point—namely, that human souls are **numbered** <note>meaning that there are many individual souls rather than one single "world soul"</note>—we affirm this. However, the problems that troubled the previous line of reasoning do not trouble us. For since these souls are material, they are distinguished from one another by **matter** <note>the author is arguing that physical matter is what allows one soul to be distinct from another</note>, and the potential for an infinite number of souls does not disturb our logic.

Regarding the fifth point, we also say that the human soul is "made," but not through **creation** <note>meaning a direct, supernatural act by God from nothing</note>; rather, it is made through **generation** <note>biological reproduction</note>. This follows the principle that "the sun and man generate man" <note>original: "sol & homo generent hominem"; a famous quote from Aristotle's Physics 2, implying that human life is produced by both biological parents and the natural energy/cycles of the heavens</note>. 

The soul itself is the final consideration in the study of nature. When **Aristotle** says in the first book of *On the Parts of Animals* (Chapter 1) that the **intellect** does not fall under the study of the "natural philosopher" <note>a scientist of the physical world</note>, this is true of the "true intellect," for that is a **mover** that is not itself moved. However, the **human intellect** is a "moved mover" <note>it initiates action but is also affected by sensory input and physical reality</note>. Therefore, the human intellect falls within our natural consideration, while the "true" or divine intellect does not, since according to the second book of the *Physics*, things that move without being moved are not—

<summary>The author argues that the human soul is the natural "form" of the body and is produced through biological generation rather than direct divine creation, positioning it as a subject for natural science rather than just theology.</summary>

And when it was further said that the soul "comes from without," <note>original: "venire deforis"; a classic Aristotelian phrase suggesting the intellect has a divine origin external to the biological body</note> it must be understood as "mind" in a simple sense, not specifically human; or if it is understood as human, it is not in an absolute sense, but rather that in relation to the **sensitive** and **vegetative** faculties <note>the lower powers of the soul responsible for sensation and basic life/growth</note>, it participates more in **Divinity**. 

For in Book 4 of *On the Parts of Animals*, chapter 9, man alone is of an upright nature, because man alone is a participant in much Divinity. Nevertheless, we do not posit that man remains after death with respect to his soul, since it begins to exist; and in Book 1 of *On the Heavens*, whatever begins to exist also ends, and **Plato** says in Book 8 of the *Laws* that whatever begins to exist in any way also ceases to exist.

...this will [last]. <note>This fragment completes a word or sentence from the previous page, likely regarding the duration of existence.</note> But such pleasure as is best for us for a short time, is always so for them; for us, indeed, it is impossible. From these words, it first appears that the Gods are simply immortal; for if they are always delighted because they are always understanding <note>original: "intelligunt"; in this context, it refers to the act of pure intellectual contemplation</note>—as follows in the same text: waking, sensation, and understanding are the most delightful things—if they are therefore always delighted, they always exist. Therefore, they are immortal. 

But humans are mortal because they are delighted for a very brief time, for "to act follows to be." <note>original: "operari enim sequitur esse"; a fundamental philosophical principle meaning that the way a thing functions reveals its true nature</note> If man is ever called "immortal," it is understood in a certain respect <note>original: "secundum quid"; meaning relatively or in a specific sense, rather than absolutely</note>, since in Book 2 of *On the Parts of Animals*, chapter 10, it is said: man alone among mortal beings is a participant in the greatest **Divinity**. 

Compared to other mortals, man can be called immortal; for as it has been said, man is the middle point between the Gods and the beasts. Therefore, just as a pale object compared to a black one is called "white," so man, compared to the beasts, can be called "God" and "immortal," but not truly and simply. 

As for the fact that our ancestors sometimes posited that men were turned into Gods, **Aristotle** says in Book 12 of the *Metaphysics*, text 50, that they posited this mythically <note>original: "fabulose"</note> to persuade the masses, for the benefit of the many, and as a contribution to the laws. But only God himself is properly called immortal; at the end of Book 11 <note>Though the text says Book 11, this refers to Book 12 (Lambda) of the Metaphysics in modern editions</note>, text 39, he says: "we say that God is a **living being** <term>animal: in the Latin sense of 'a being with a soul/life,' not specifically a beast</term>, eternal and best." Wherefore life and continuous and eternal duration exist in God, for this [is God]...

# On Immortality

God. From those words, another point also becomes clear: namely, that the human <term>intellect</term> <note>original: "intellectum"; the faculty of the mind that grasps abstract truths</note> does not understand without a <term>phantasm</term> <note>original: "phantasmate"; a mental image or sensory representation that the mind uses to think</note>. For eternal beings are always delighted because they are always understanding; they themselves, in their act of understanding, have no need for a phantasm. Indeed, if they did require one, they would not be eternal, since according to Book 2 of *On the Soul*, <term>imagination</term> <note>original: "phantasia"</note> is a movement produced by the senses in a state of activity. 

Furthermore, eternal beings are not moved by anything while they understand, as the Philosopher <note>Aristotle</note> says in the same work, text 50: that which is most divine and most honorable does not undergo change. For change would be a degradation, and movement is already a kind of change. On the contrary, in these eternal beings, the one who understands, the thing that is understood, and the act of understanding are all the same, as is stated in those cited texts.

But the human intellect, since it understands for only a very brief time, cannot be freed from the phantasm. It cannot understand anything except through movement, for the act of understanding consists in a certain kind of "being acted upon" <note>original: "pati"; the philosophical idea that the mind is passive, like a wax tablet, receiving impressions from the world</note>. The thing that moves the intellect is the phantasm, as is clear from Book 3 of *On the Soul*. Therefore, it does not understand without a phantasm, although it does not "know" in the same way that the imagination does. 

Because the human intellect exists as a middle point between eternal beings and the beasts, it knows the **universal** <note>the general concept, like "treeness"</note>. In this way, it agrees with eternal beings and differs from the beasts. However, it contemplates the universal within the **particular** <note>original: "singulari"; the specific, individual object, like a single oak tree</note>, which is where it differs from eternal beings and agrees in some way with the beasts. As for the beasts themselves, situated at the lowest limit of knowing beings, they know neither the universal simply, nor the universal within the particular...

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the modes of cognition and how they relate to physical existence. While the previous page established that human understanding requires mental images (phantasms), this page categorizes all living beings into three distinct groups based on their relationship to the body and their way of knowing.</meta>

## Soul

...but they only grasp the <term>singular</term> <note>original: "singulare"; an individual, specific thing rather than a general concept</note> in a singular way. There are, therefore, three general modes of living beings, and since every living being possesses knowledge, there are also three modes of knowing. For there are beings that are entirely eternal, those that are entirely mortal, and those that are intermediate between these two.

The first are the **celestial bodies** <note>In the Aristotelian worldview, planets and stars were considered living, eternal beings moved by divine intellects</note>, and these in no way depend on a body for their act of knowing. The others are the **beasts**, which depend on a body as both a <term>subject</term> <note>original: "subiecto"; here meaning the physical organ (like an eye or brain) where the process takes place</note> and an <term>object</term> <note>original: "obiecto"; the specific physical thing being perceived</note>; for this reason, they know only the singular.

The **intermediate beings**, however, are **humans**. They do not depend on the body as a subject, but only as an object. Therefore, they contemplate neither the <term>universal</term> <note>original: "vniuersale"; general truths or abstract essences</note> in a simple way as the eternal beings do, nor the singular alone as the beasts do; instead, they contemplate the universal within the singular.

Aristotle suggested these three modes of knowing in Book 1 of *On the Soul* when he said: *if understanding is imagination, or does not exist without imagination* <note>original: "si intellegere est phantasia, aut non est sine phantasia"; a reference to De Anima 403a</note>. For by "imagination," he understands the senses, which require the body in both ways—namely, as a subject and as an object. By the phrase "not being imagination" and yet "not being without imagination," he understands the **human intellect**, which requires the body as an object but not as a subject. Finally, that which is neither imagination nor exists with imagination at all is truly the **intellect**, and it belongs to divine beings. 

No other modes of knowing have been found by Aristotle in any place, nor do any others agree with reason. For to say, as those do who claim that the human intellect...

# On Immortality

...to say the intellect itself is absolutely immortal and possesses two ways of knowing—one entirely without a <term>mental image</term> <note>original: "phantasmate"; the sensory data or "pictures" the mind works with to reach abstract thoughts</note>, and another with a mental image—is to transform human nature into divine nature. This is little different from the fables of **Ovid** in his book *Metamorphoses*. Indeed, it is granted that the ancient philosophers proposed such things in the form of fables for the benefit of the law <note>The author suggests that myths about the afterlife were invented by philosophers to encourage people to follow laws through fear or hope</note>, as has been stated. 

But if someone should falsely argue that the saying—namely, that we enjoy delight for only a very short time—ought to be understood as referring to the human person and not to their soul; and that therefore the human person does not understand without a mental image (for the human person is mortal), but the soul is immortal, and so in its state of <term>separation</term> <note>original: "separatione"; the state of the soul after the death of the body</note> it understands without a mental image; then I beg such a slanderer—or rather, a caviller—to see that while he strives to interpret Aristotle, he is in fact corrupting him. 

First of all, this is because Aristotle here makes no distinction between the human person and the soul, just as he makes none between God and the **celestial body**; for he says that God is an **eternal living being** <note>original: "animal aeternum"; Aristotle defines "animal" as a being with life and perception, which included the divine movers of the stars</note>. Secondly, if he is only speaking of the human person and not the soul, then where does he speak of the knowledge of the separated soul? For that would have been the proper place, since he is writing in the *Metaphysics*, which treats **separated things** <note>original: "separatis"; immaterial substances like the soul or God that exist without physical matter</note>. Thirdly, because in this way the human soul would simply be made divine, since it would take on the mode of acting used by Divine beings. Thus we would be positing Ovid’s fables: namely, the turning of one nature into another...

...while) **Saint Thomas** <note>Saint Thomas Aquinas (1225–1274), the influential theologian who attempted to reconcile Aristotelian philosophy with Christian doctrine</note> in the exposition of the final text of the 9th book of the **Metaphysics** says that, in that text, **Aristotle** settles the question which he left unresolved in the 3rd book of *On the Soul* <note>original: "3. de anima"</note>. For this reason, **Saint Thomas** in book 3 of *Against the Gentiles* <note>original: "contra gentiles"</note>, chapter 48, says that **Aristotle's** opinion regarding the understanding of separated things is that we only understand them according to the course of <term>speculative science</term> <note>Theoretical knowledge reached through reason and deduction, rather than direct vision or intuition</note>; therefore, it is not without a **phantasm**, as is well known. 

And take careful note that here he seems to hint at this opinion of ours: namely, that **Aristotle** felt the human soul is not truly "understanding" in itself, but only possesses a certain participation in the <term>intellect</term> <note>The author suggests the human soul "borrows" its power from a higher, universal Mind rather than owning it individually</note>, and for that reason it is "improperly" immortal <note>meaning immortal in name only, or in a metaphorical sense, rather than having a personal, eternal existence</note>. 

However, I think that the phrase "later to be investigated" can also be understood as referring to the books of the **Ethics** <note>Aristotle's "Nicomachean Ethics"</note>. For there he declares that the ultimate happiness of the soul consists in the contemplation of abstract things through the study of **Metaphysics**; therefore, the conclusion remains the same as before.

## CHAPTER 10.

To ensure our position is more robust, it is worthwhile to respond to the arguments raised against it by opposing views. Since we maintain that within a human being the <term>vegetative</term> <note>The faculty governing growth and nutrition</note>, <term>sensitive</term> <note>The faculty governing sensation and movement</note>, and <term>intellectual</term> <note>The faculty governing reason and higher thought</note> are essentially the same thing, we must address the logic of contradictions. It is argued that contradictory qualities cannot be attributed to the same thing in an absolute sense. However, as noted in the **Sophistical Refutations** <note>original: "Elenchis"; a work by Aristotle on logical fallacies</note>, it is not inconsistent for one quality to be true **absolutely** <note>original: "absolutè"; without qualification</note> while the other is true only **in a certain respect** <note>original: "secundum quid"; a logical term meaning something is true only in a limited or qualified context</note>, or for both to be true in a certain respect. This is the case here: the human intellect is absolutely mortal, yet immortal in a certain respect.

In response to the first objection against our second claim: the human soul, which is capable of receiving all the varieties of material forms, possesses two conditions. First, it is immaterial in its own nature and does not require a physical organ as its **subject** <note>The underlying material or "seat" of a function; where the function resides</note> when it receives and understands these forms—a point we concede. Second, however, it cannot receive those forms unless it is prompted by **phantasms** <note>original: "phantasmatibus"; mental images or sensory representations that the mind uses to think</note>, as **Aristotle** clearly teaches there. Consequently, it requires a physical organ as its **object** <note>The source or focus of its activity, rather than the place where the activity lives</note>. But through what has been demonstrated that

<summary>This page defends the author’s view that the human soul is a single entity performing vegetative, sensitive, and intellectual functions. It employs Aristotelian logic to argue that the soul can be considered mortal in an absolute sense while remaining immortal in a qualified sense.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the defense of the author's hybrid view of the soul. This is how it stands: the soul is **absolutely** material and **in a certain respect** immaterial. This is our intended conclusion. If it is asked whether the soul is a material form, we say that in one part it is, and in another part it is not. Insofar as it participates in immateriality—even if it does not know itself through its own **species** <note>In scholastic philosophy, a "species" is the mental representation or "image" through which the mind understands an object.</note> but through the species of other things, as is stated in Book 3 of **On the Soul** <note>original: "de anima"; Aristotle's treatise on the nature of living things</note>—nevertheless, according to that mode of being, it can in some way reflect upon itself and know its own acts. It does this, however, not primarily or as perfectly as the **Intelligences** <note>original: "intelligentiæ"; higher spiritual beings or angels who possess direct, non-discursive knowledge</note> do. Nor is it surprising, since the soul does not use a physical organ or the **appendages of matter** <note>original: "appenditiis materiæ"; the specific conditions like size, weight, or location that define physical objects</note> in the act of understanding.

But if it is further insisted: the soul itself has no being except in matter, and exists through primary qualities <note>Heat, cold, moisture, and dryness</note> together with secondary qualities; and since **action follows being** <note>original: "operari sequitur esse"; a fundamental philosophical principle meaning that a thing acts according to its nature</note>, it follows that the soul cannot act without these qualities. Therefore, it cannot act without the "appendages of matter," which is the opposite of what you claim.

Furthermore, according to **the Philosopher** <note>Aristotle</note> in Books 2 and 3 of **On the Soul**, in order for the pupil of the eye to receive all colors, its own essence must not only be colorless, but it must not even be joined to a color. Therefore, if the intellectual soul is to receive all material forms, it must not only be immaterial, but it must not be joined to any material form at all—and consequently, not even to heat or cold. Yet we know this to be false <note>Because the soul is clearly joined to a body that possesses temperature</note>. Truly, an opponent who argues this fails to see that these same points could be used against all other opinions, for even according to those views, the soul is not...

[exists] in matter except through quality and quantity, therefore without those it cannot act: because if there is any response for those [faculties], there will also be one for this. However, to the first objection it is said that truly the human intellect cannot understand unless sensible quality and quantity are present in the matter, since it cannot act unless it exists, and it cannot exist without a suitable **disposition** <note>A specific physical state or arrangement of matter required for a soul to inhabit it.</note>. Yet, it does not follow that it understands *through* such dispositions. Indeed, as is clear enough, it does not follow even in the case of the senses; for the **visual faculty** <note>original: "virtus visiua"; the power of sight</note> does not see unless the eye is warm <note>referring to "vital heat," the biological warmth necessary for life</note>, yet it does not see through heat or any other real quality, but through the **visible species** <note>original: "speciem visibilem"; in medieval philosophy, the "species" is the mental or sensory representation of an object that allows us to perceive it.</note>.

To the second objection it is said that a material thing is not universally hindered from cognition by the coexistence of another material thing; for if it were, sight would not know colors, since the **primary qualities** <note>The basic physical properties: hot, cold, wet, and dry.</note> are joined to the power of sight. Rather, it is hindered by the coexistence of one of those things of which it is itself a perceiver. For instance, by **redness** <note>original: "rubedinem"; the author uses the example of an eye physically tinted with a color being unable to perceive other colors correctly.</note> it is hindered from the cognition of other colors, because it is a perceiver of both those colors and the redness. Therefore, if the intellect were a **pure material form** <note>original: "pura forma materialis"; a form that is entirely dependent on and limited by physical matter.</note>, since it is a perceiver of all material forms, it would be hindered from the cognition of them. But it has been proven that the intellect is immaterial, although it is not **absolutely immaterial** <note>original: "simpliciter immaterialis"; meaning it is not a completely detached spirit like an angel, but remains the form of a body.</note>. Therefore, by the coexistence of material forms, it is not hi-

<summary>This page argues that while the intellect requires a physical body and certain "dispositions" (like heat or size) to exist, it does not use these physical properties as the tools for thinking. Using the analogy of sight, the author explains that the mind must be immaterial to perceive material things without being "clogged" by them.</summary>
