<page-num>79</page-num>
<header>The Soul</header>

It is clear that [the intellect] is not entirely set free from quantity and its conditions, since it always contemplates the **universal** <note>The general concept of a thing, like "humanity" in general.</note> within the **particular** <note>A specific individual, like "Socrates."</note>. For the human intellect possesses two aspects: that it is an *intellect*, and that it is *human*. Insofar as it is an intellect, it knows the universal; but insofar as it is human, it is unable to perceive the universal except within the singular.

As for the argument regarding the **organ** <note>The physical body part, such as the brain or eyes, used for sensing.</note>, it is said (according to what was previously stated) that the intellect requires the organ as an **object**, not as a **subject**. <note>The author distinguishes between the brain providing data (the "object" of thought) and the brain being the place where thought actually happens (the "subject"). He argues thought happens in the immaterial mind, not the physical brain tissue.</note> The definition of each of these was given in the previous chapter. 

Furthermore, if it is argued: "Does the intellect not join with matter through the **primary qualities** <note>In ancient physics, these are heat, cold, moisture, and dryness.</note>? If so, what sort of thing would it then become? It would have to become either hot or cold, and therefore it would not be able to know all things." <note>Aristotle argued that if the mind had a physical quality like "heat," it couldn't perceive "cold" objectively.</note> To this, it is replied that the intellect is not joined to matter insofar as it is *intellect*, but only insofar as it is joined to the *senses*. Therefore, even if it takes on a physical quality during the act of sensing, it does not do so during the act of understanding. Because of this, insofar as it is an intellect, it has no physical quality, nor is it **organic** <note>original: "organicus"; meaning tied to or functioning through a physical organ.</note>.

If it is further asked whether the human act of understanding is an <term>accident</term> <note>original: "accidens"; in logic, a property that cannot exist on its own but must exist within something else, like the color of an apple.</note> which cannot exist without a subject, where then shall the act of understanding be placed? To this it is replied that, truly, according to its essence, the act of understanding exists within the intellect itself, following what is written in the third book of *On the Soul*: "the soul is the **place of forms** <note>original: "locus specierum"; the mental "space" where concepts and mental representations are stored.</note>, not the whole soul, but the intellect." However, since the human intellect exists in matter as if through a certain <term>concomitance</term> <note>original: "cōcomitantiam"; the state of existing alongside or accompanying the body without being made of the same stuff.</note>, the act of understanding itself [continues...]

<meta>This page continues the technical argument from page 79 regarding how the human mind relates to the physical body. It addresses whether the "act of understanding" is located in a specific organ and discusses the theories of Alexander of Aphrodisias and Thomas Aquinas.</meta>

<page-num>80</page-num>
<header>On Immortality</header>

...a certain accompaniment <note>original: "quandam concomitantiam"</note>, and the act of understanding itself is, in a way, within matter, but only in an accidental sense. This is because, while it is accidental for the intellect (insofar as it is an intellect) to be within matter, the act of understanding is not located in any specific part of the body. Rather, it is located in the body taken **collectively** <note>original: "categorematicè." In this context, it means the body is considered as a single, unified whole rather than a collection of separate parts.</note>. It cannot be in one specific part, because then the intellect would be **organic** <note>Meaning it would function like a physical tool or organ, such as the eye.</note>. If it were organic, it would either fail to know all things, or if it did know all things, it would only know them as the **cogitative power** <note>The faculty that perceives specific, individual objects (like "this specific apple") rather than general concepts.</note> does—perceiving them only as individual instances and not as **universal** truths. Therefore, just as the intellect is present in the whole, so too is the act of understanding. 

It was not inappropriate, therefore, for **Alexander** <note>Alexander of Aphrodisias (active c. 200 AD), a famous commentator on Aristotle who argued the soul was a product of the body's physical makeup.</note> to claim that the entire body serves as the instrument of the intellect. This is because the intellect encompasses all the soul's powers rather than being tied to one specific, determined part. If it were restricted, it could not know all things, just as none of the physical senses can perceive anything outside their own domain. 

Although the entire body is described as the instrument of the intellect—almost as if it were the **subject** <note>In philosophy, the "subject" is the underlying thing in which a quality exists, like wax is the subject for a shape.</note>—it is not truly a subject in the physical sense. This is because the act of understanding is not received into the body in a physical way, as was said before. And if it is further asked whether the human intellect receives information in an **indivisible** manner, the answer is: insofar as it *understands*, it receives indivisibly; but insofar as it *senses* or *grows* <note>original: "vegetat." This refers to the "vegetative soul," the biological functions like nutrition and growth.</note>, it acts divisibly. There is nothing contradictory in such a complex nature having so many different ways of receiving and operating. 

Regarding the argument from experience, one might first wonder how **Saint Thomas** <note>Thomas Aquinas (1225–1274), the influential theologian who sought to prove the soul's immortality through reason.</note> introduced it, considering that **Aristotle** in Book 3 of the *Ethics*...

<summary>This page argues that the human intellect is not tied to a specific physical organ like the brain or eyes, but instead uses the entire body as its instrument while remaining essentially immaterial. It explains that the mind can understand universal concepts because it is not restricted by the physical limitations of any single body part.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the nature of the human soul and its desire for immortality. It addresses a specific objection: if the human will can desire impossible things (like being a god), does the desire for immortality actually prove the soul is immortal?</meta>

<page-num>81</page-num>
<header>On the Soul</header>

...[Aristotle] says that the will is directed toward impossible things, such as when we desire immortality. From this, it is argued that if our will exists only within the **intellectual soul** <note>The highest part of the human soul, responsible for reasoning and abstract thought.</note>, and if by desiring immortality (according to **Aristotle** <note>The text refers to him as "the Philosopher," the standard title for Aristotle in the Middle Ages and Renaissance.</note>) it desires the impossible, then it would follow that the human soul cannot be immortal.

Therefore, it is said in response to this argument that this desire is not a clear sign [of the soul's nature]. As the Philosopher says in that passage, the will is naturally drawn toward impossible things, because the **essence of the good** <note>original: "ratio boni." The idea that something is perceived as desirable or "good" regardless of whether it is attainable.</note> can be found even in what is impossible. Furthermore, there was an argument that a **natural appetite is not in vain** <note>original: "appetitum naturalem non frustrari." A fundamental philosophical rule of the time: if nature gives a creature a natural desire, a way to satisfy that desire must exist.</note>.

This rule is true if we define "natural" as something distinct from "intellectual." For a natural appetite is the work of an intelligence that does not err <note>This refers to the "intelligence" of Nature or God, which directs animals by instinct.</note>. Thus, when the will is drawn toward something without the need for conscious thought, that desire cannot be in vain. However, when the will is guided by **cognition** <note>The process of conscious thought and understanding.</note>, it *can* be in vain unless the reasoning is correct. For when the supreme good—a state of being that belongs even to the Gods—is presented to the mind, the will is drawn toward that state, even if it is impossible for a human. To keep the will from being in vain, it must therefore be regulated by **right reason** <note>original: "rationem rectam." The capacity to judge correctly what is truly possible and good for a human being.</note>.

We might also say that just as a mule is a middle point between a donkey and a horse and shares qualities of both, yet does not truly possess the full properties of either the horse or the donkey, so too the human soul is a middle point between material and immaterial things. It strives for eternity, even though it cannot perfectly attain it. For even a mule, though it has all the biological tools for reproduction, cannot successfully achieve it, despite perhaps "desiring" it; indeed...

<meta>This page continues the discussion on whether the human desire for immortality is "in vain." It addresses the specific argument that if humans cannot attain immortality, the desire for it—which is natural—would be pointless, violating the principle that nature does nothing in vain.</meta>

<page-num>82</page-num>
<header># On Immortality</header>

It is not unfitting for something to be naturally **frustrated** <note>original: "frustrari." In this context, it means a natural capacity or desire that fails to reach its intended goal.</note> in an entire **species**, provided that it is not frustrated within the **genus** <note>The author distinguishes between a specific type of creature (species) and the broader category it belongs to (genus). For example, humans are a species within the genus of animals.</note> as a whole. This is seen in the case of **mules**, in which the organs of generation are frustrated <note>Mules are naturally sterile and cannot reproduce.</note>, yet reproduction is preserved within their broader genus [of animals]. Similarly, the **mole** has eyes but does not see, yet sight is not frustrated in the wider genus of animals, as is noted in the book *On the History of Animals*.

For this reason, although the human soul desires an **immortality** that it cannot attain absolutely <note>Likely meaning immortality as a combined unit of body and soul.</note>, it is sufficient that it attains it simply as a **separated substance** <note>original: "separata." This refers to the soul existing on its own after death, apart from the body.</note>. This is why **Aristotle**, in the second book of the *Metaphysics*, compared the human intellect to an **owl** and not to a **mole**. For the owl sees in some small way, whereas the mole sees nothing at all. Hence, in the final text of the ninth book of the *Metaphysics*, he said that the human intellect, when perceiving **abstract things** <note>Concepts that are separated from physical matter, such as mathematical truths or the nature of God.</note>, is not entirely blind, but rather **dim-sighted** <note>original: "caecutientem." Literally "blinking" or "struggling to see," like someone squinting in bright light.</note>. Therefore, the soul has an affection for eternity, but it does not desire it with a "perfect appetite" <note>A "perfect appetite" would imply a necessary natural drive that must be fulfilled for the species to be complete.</note>.

Regarding the words of **Aristotle**, the response is clear from what has already been said: universally, the **celestial bodies**, humans, beasts, and plants are all **animated** <note>Possessing an "anima" or soul/life-force.</note>, and their souls are contained under the universal definition of "soul." However, they are not all animated in the same way. The **intelligences** <note>In medieval philosophy, these were the spiritual beings that moved the stars and planets.</note> that actuate the celestial bodies are the **act** <note>term: "act" (actus). The formal principle that makes a body alive and functional.</note> of a physical organic body, but they are not so insofar as they are intelligences. Furthermore, in their role of actuating [the heavens], they receive nothing, but only give.

But the **human soul** is simply the act of a physical organic body, since it possesses no operation in which...

He explains that while all living things have souls, the human soul is uniquely tied to the physical body as its "act."</summary>

...in some way it does not depend on the body, if not as a **subject** <note>original: "subiecto." In Aristotelian philosophy, a "subject" is the underlying material or thing in which a quality exists. To not depend on the body as a subject means the intellect is not "stuck" inside the physical matter like a color is stuck in a wall.</note>, at least as an **object** <note>original: "obiecto." For this reason, they are entirely the **actualities** <note>original: "actus." Nature instituted this rightly and in an orderly fashion. 

For just as we descend from the entirely immobile beings, which are the **Intelligences**, to the celestial bodies which are moved only according to **place** <note>Moving from point A to point B without changing their internal nature.</note>—and even then, not as a whole, but according to their parts—and after these, we descend to things that can be **generated and corrupted** <note>Things that are born and die, like plants and animals.</note>, which are changed both as wholes and through every mode of motion; so also the Intelligences are the least involved in being the "acts" of an organic body among all types of souls. Next is the **human intellect**, thirdly the **senses**, and fourthly the **vegetative power**. For needing a body in any way is not free from imperfection; hence, that order of hierarchy is most appropriate.

# On Immortality
<note>original: "De Immortalitate"</note>

...the **active intellect** <note>original: "intellectus agens." In Aristotelian thought, this is the aspect of the mind that actively produces thoughts, often compared to a light that makes objects visible to the eye.</note> is truly immortal and is always in **act** <note>original: "in actu." This means it is eternally and continuously functioning, never "turned off."</note>, whereas the **passive** <note>original: "passivus." The part of the mind that receives or stores information.</note> is not, since it sometimes understands and sometimes does not. For this reason, since it does not have a perpetual operation, it does not have a perpetual essence. From this reasoning, it is said that the **possible intellect** <note>original: "intellectus possibilis." The mind's capacity to learn or become all things; a "blank slate."</note> is immortal only in a certain respect, but the active intellect itself is truly immortal, as it is one of the **Intelligences** <note>Higher, purely spiritual beings or celestial movers.</note>. Nor is it some part of the human soul, as **Themistius** <note>A 4th-century Greek philosopher and commentator on Aristotle.</note> and **Averroës** <note>The 12th-century Islamic philosopher (Ibn Rushd) famous for his "Long Commentary" on Aristotle.</note> believed, but only a **mover** <note>original: "motor." An external force that sets the human mind in motion.</note>. 

However, when **Aristotle** said that it is a part of the soul, this is true in the sense that "soul" is a term shared by both the [higher] Intelligence and the human intellect. From this, it does not follow that it is a part of the human soul; for that would be a **fallacy of the consequent** <note>A logical error where one assumes that because a general statement is true, a specific sub-case must be true in the same way.</note>. This is similar to what is said of **prime matter** <note>original: "materia prima." The most basic, formless substance of the physical world that has the potential to take on any shape or form.</note>; for matter is receptive to the **actual being** of all material forms. The intellect, however, is receptive only according to **intentional being** <note>original: "esse intentionale." This means the mind holds the *concept* or *image* of a thing rather than its physical reality.</note>, since a stone is not physically inside the soul, but rather the **species** <note>The mental representation or "look" of the object.</note> of the stone is. 

Truly, that which leads prime matter from **potentiality to act** <note>Changing something from "what it could be" to "what it is."</note> regarding the existence of forms is not something belonging to prime matter itself, nor is it joined to it in its essence; rather, it is a **universal mover** which can be called **acting nature**. In the same way, the human intellect holds the same proportion in the category of things that can be known as prime matter holds in the category of things that can be sensed—as even Themistius and Averroës...

<meta>This page continues the "three doubts" introduced at the end of Chapter XI. The previous page ended mid-sentence while discussing whether the human soul should be categorized as mortal or immortal based on the hierarchy of nature.</meta>

<page-num>86</page-num>
<header>On Immortality</header>
<meta>catchword: l (This refers to "licet," the first word of the next page)</meta>

...[soul] <note>The text continues from the previous page: "First, since it has been said that the human soul..."</note> and the lower do not contain the higher. For we say that the **celestial bodies** <note>In the Aristotelian worldview, the heavens are made of an incorruptible "fifth element" (aether) and are superior to the Earthly realm.</note> contain these things that are **generable and corruptible** <term>generabilia & corruptibilia</term> <note>Things that are born, decay, and die.</note>, and not the reverse. Therefore, since the immortal is above the mortal, it should rather be said that the human soul is **simply** <term>simpliciter</term> <note>Meaning "absolutely" or "in its primary nature."</note> immortal and **relatively** <term>secundum quid</term> <note>Meaning "in a certain respect" or "only partially."</note> mortal—since the immortal contains the mortal—rather than saying it is simply mortal and relatively immortal, since the mortal does not contain the immortal.

**The second doubt** is this: if the soul is immortal only in a certain respect, then it is either truly and properly immortal, or it is not truly and is improperly called so. It is not the first, as has been said; nor is it the second, because if it is called "immortal" improperly, it could just as well be called anything else that it is not. For it could also be improperly called a "dog" or a "hare." Why, then, is it more often said to be "immortal" than a "dog" or a "hare," since the term "immortal" is applied to it only improperly?

**The third doubt** is that the entire root of this position rests on the foundation that the human intellect has only one mode of understanding, just as the senses and the **intelligences** <note>original: "intelligentiae." In medieval philosophy, these are pure spirits or celestial minds, like angels, that understand things instantly without needing physical senses.</note> have only one mode of knowing. That mode, however, is the middle ground between the mode of knowing of purely abstract things and that of the **sensitive soul** <note>The part of the soul shared with animals, responsible for sensation and movement.</note>, since it participates in both. For insofar as it knows universally, it agrees with...

Wherefore it was concluded that it stands as a middle point among material things. However, this raises a doubt: for if such a dependence of the **intellect's** <term>intellectus</term> cognition upon the phantasm means contemplating the universal within the singular, then the intellect would know the singular itself—which is denied by many. 

For some say that a material singular can only be known through the senses; or, even if we grant that the intellect knows the singular, it seems to many that it can only be known **reflexively** <term>reflexe</term> <note>By the mind "turning back" to look at the sensory images from which it drew its general ideas.</note>, as **Aristotle** seems to say in Book 3 of *On the Soul* <note>Original: "3. de anima tex. 10."</note>. 

Yet even if this is granted, since that reflection cannot be imagined except as a certain **discourse** <term>discursus</term> <note>A step-by-step process of reasoning, rather than an immediate grasp of truth.</note>—as nearly everyone interprets it—and since this discourse occurs only in time and after the cognition of simple things, it follows that: prior to "composition and division" <note>The mental act of joining or separating concepts to form a statement, such as "the man is tall."</note> and, consequently, prior to discourse, the intellect will contemplate the universal and *not* within the singular. Therefore, the [opposing] position rests on a false foundation.

## CHAPTER XII.

To the first doubt, therefore, it is said that there is a great distance between **containing** <term>continere</term> and **participating** <term>participare</term>. For "to contain" acts in the manner of a **form** <term>formae</term> <note>In Aristotelian philosophy, "form" is the active principle that gives a thing its essence and structure.</note>, and "to be contained" acts in the manner of **matter** <term>materiae</term> <note>The passive potential or "stuff" that receives a form.</note>; wherefore the container is perfect and surpassing, while the thing contained is imperfect and surpassed. 

But the opposite is true regarding the **participant** <term>participante</term> and the **participated** <term>participato</term>; for that which is participated in acts in the manner of a **cause** <term>causae</term> and that which surpasses, whereas the participant acts in the manner of an **effect** <term>effectus</term> and that which is surpassed. 

Therefore, we do not properly say that the human **intellect** <term>intellectum</term> contains divine things and immortality, but rather the opposite; instead, we correctly say that the human intellect **participates** in divinity and immortality, and not vice versa. 

Wherefore **Aristotle**, in Book 1 of *On the Parts of Animals*, chapter 10 <note>Original: "1. de part. cap 10."</note>, did not say, "man alone contains divinity and immortality," but he said, "man alone is a **participant** in divinity and immortality, or most especially so." This phrase ["or most especially"] was added because other mortal beings also participate in divinity; for there is a divine presence in all of nature, as the same Aristotle says, following the opinion of **Heraclitus** <note>A pre-Socratic Greek philosopher known for the idea that change is fundamental to the universe.</note>, in the final chapter of Book 1 of *On the Parts of Animals* <note>Original: "1. de part. cap. vlt."</note>. But the others...

This allows for man to be uniquely connected to the divine while remaining a part of the natural, mortal world.</summary>

...mortal things, and not only as man does. He <note>Aristotle.</note> repeats the same point in Book 4 of *On the Parts of Animals*, chapter 9. For this reason, logic compelled the conclusion that the human **intellect** <term>intellectus</term> does not **contain** <term>contineret</term> immortality, but rather that it **participates** <term>participaret</term> in immortality—which is the position we hold. 

You should also know that just as something truly mortal participates in immortality—indeed, every thing capable of producing its like <note>A reference to biological reproduction, which Aristotle viewed as a way for mortal individuals to achieve a kind of "eternal" existence for their species.</note> is thus a participant in immortality, as stated in Book 2 of *On the Soul*, texts 34 and 35—so too does something immortal seem to participate in mortality and that which is fleeting <term>caduci</term>. 

For **the Commentator** <note>This refers to Averroes (1126–1198), the Andalusian philosopher whose interpretations of Aristotle were standard in university curricula.</note> in Book 2 of *On the Heavens*, commentary 49, when providing the cause for the **moon’s spots** <term>macula lunae</term>, says the cause is that the moon is of the nature of the earth. He cites Aristotle’s book *On the Soul* for this, but he says it more clearly in the book *On the Properties of the Elements*. There, he posits that the other elements are shared by the other **planets** <term>erraticis</term> <note>Original: "erraticis," literally "wandering stars."</note>. Therefore, it is not inconsistent for the mortal to participate in the immortal, and for the immortal to participate in the mortal.

To the second doubt, it is said that although there is no "middle ground" between "properly" and "improperly," a middle ground is nonetheless granted through the **participation of properties**. This is similar to how, although no middle ground exists between **substance** <term>substantia</term> <note>Something that exists in itself (e.g., a tree).</note> and **accident** <term>accidens</term> <note>A quality that exists only in a substance (e.g., the greenness of the tree).</note>—for there is nothing that is not either a substance or an accident—something may nevertheless be posited to participate in the properties of both. 

Similarly, "movement according to a part" is posited as a middle ground between "movement in itself" <note>Original: "moueri per se."</note> and "movement by accident," as stated in Book 5 of the *Physics*. Therefore, the human mind...

# On Immortality

"Man" is prior to "some man" <note>original: *homo est prior aliquo homine*. This refers to the logical priority of a general species over a specific instance.</note>, since the consequence of their existence is interchangeable; for if "Man" exists, then "some man" exists, and if "some man" exists, "Man" exists. This, however, cannot be; otherwise, "Man" would be prior to "some man." To me, these statements are quite ambiguous. First, the claim that "man" is known within "some man," but not in **Socrates** <note>Used here as a standard philosophical example to represent any specific, individual person.</note>, nor in any other singular individual, seems primarily to go against experience. For no matter how immaterial or universal a thing we may recognize, we always form for ourselves some **mental image** <term>idolum</term> within the **cogitative power** <term>cogitativa</term> <note>In medieval psychology, the *vis cogitativa* is the faculty of the soul that bridges the gap between raw sensory data and abstract thought, allowing us to perceive "individual intentions."</note> in which we contemplate that thing, as **St. Thomas** <note>Thomas Aquinas.</note> also says in that same passage. Yet, this mental image is a specific thing that represents something specifically, and reason is directed toward it. For he [Aquinas] wishes to prove that we understand by **turning toward phantasms** <term>conversio ad phantasmata</term> <note>The Aristotelian and Thomistic theory that the human intellect cannot think without "turning" to look at sensory-derived images stored in the memory or imagination.</note>, which cannot be imagined except as something specific.

Because "man" is in Socrates, and "horse" is in this horse—indeed, these are his very words—it follows that we know "man" from the fact that he is in *this* phantasm; otherwise, the proof would not be fitting. For one does not know "man" from the fact that he exists indifferently in just "some man," but rather in this specific phantasm. Certainly, the statement of this interpreter contradicts the literal text, for the text stands thus: he knows in "some individual," which he demonstrates by the words that follow. For it continues: "human nature is in this man, and equine nature in this horse." Furthermore, it is most difficult

For **Porphyry** <note>A 3rd-century scholar whose introduction to Aristotle’s logic became a standard textbook for over a millennium.</note> in his discussion of commonalities proves that the **genus** <note>A general category, such as "Animal."</note> is prior to **differences** <note>Specific traits that divide a genus, such as "rational" or "four-legged."</note> and **species** <note>The specific group, such as "Human."</note>, because if the genus is removed, neither species nor differences remain; yet if they are removed, the genus is not taken away. Therefore, it seems to me that it must be said that one understands "man" in the individual, yet indeterminately; for even if I now understand "man" in **Socrates**, I can nonetheless also understand it in **Plato**, or in any other, provided it is in some individual; just as every body is in one specific place, yet indeterminately. <note>original: "Anima"</note>

...we say that such understanding is truly and properly a **reflection** and a **turning back** toward the **mental images**, <note>original: "phantasmata"—the sensory images stored in the mind from which the intellect abstracts general concepts.</note> to use the words of **St. Thomas** <note>Thomas Aquinas (1225–1274), the most influential scholastic philosopher and theologian.</note> as is clearly seen in number 8 of his commentary on *The Physics* <note>original: "de physico auditu"—referring to Aristotle's work on nature.</note> in that part where he shows that reflected motions are not continuous; he defines a **reflected motion** as one that ends in the same place where it began. However, when the human soul first grasps a **singular** <note>A specific, individual thing, like a particular person or tree.</note> through the **cogitative power**, <note>A specific faculty of the mind that connects the senses to the intellect, allowing us to recognize individual objects.</note> and then through the intellect grasps the **universal** <note>The general concept, like "Humanity" or "Leoneity."</note> which it nonetheless contemplates within that same singular thing known through the imagination, it truly makes a return. Consequently, it performs a "turning back," since the same soul returns to the same object through the intellect that it first knew as a singular through the imagination. 

Nor do I see clearly how a **syllogism** <note>A logical argument where a conclusion is drawn from two premises (e.g., All men are mortal; Socrates is a man; therefore Socrates is mortal).</note> or an argumentation can be appropriately called a "reflection" or "turning back," since they do not proceed from the same point back to the same point, but rather proceed from one distinct thing to another. Both [the universal and the singular] are grasped by the same **mental representation**, <note>original: "specie"—the "species" or form by which the mind knows an object.</note> although not both in the same primary way. Nor is it inconsistent for many things to be understood at once, provided they are understood through a single mental representation. However, the mind grasps *this* individual more than *that* individual because the mental image belongs to this one and not the other. Indeed, from the inspection of **this lion**, I understand "lion" [the concept] and "this lion" [the individual]; yet I do not understand the concept "lion" any more from this one than from that lion which dwells in the woods. For if I were to look upon that other lion, I would no less understand the concept of "lion"...

## CHAPTER XIII

### *In which many difficult objections are raised against what has been said.*

But against this position, greater difficulties arise, which, so far as I can see, are not easily resolved. First, because if the human soul is **mortal** <note>original: "mortalis"</note>, as has been concluded, then there will be no **ultimate end** <note>original: "vltimus finis"—the final purpose or supreme goal toward which human life is directed.</note> for man insofar as he is a man, and thus he would not be **capable of happiness** <note>original: "felicitabilis"—possessing the potential to achieve "felicity" or true flourishing.</note>; but the opposite of this is posited by **Aristotle** <note>The Greek philosopher (384–322 BC) whose works formed the basis of medieval and Renaissance science and ethics.</note> in the first book of the *Ethics*, as is quite clear. It is also contrary to the common saying, where it is stated that "man is an animal capable of happiness," since he is endowed with reason. This is also evident from its opposite, since in the second book of the *Physics*, **misfortune** or **unhappiness** <note>original: "disfortunium seu infelicitas"</note> does not apply to beasts, but only to **rational beings** <note>original: "rationalia"—creatures capable of logic and thought.</note>. Therefore, the soul is not mortal. All these points are clear except for the conditional statement which is the **major premise** <note>In a logical argument, the general rule or first statement upon which the conclusion rests.</note>—namely, that if the soul were mortal, there would be no ultimate end for man *as* man. This is proven as follows: for if there were some ultimate end...

But since the virtues of the mind are divided into **moral** <note>Virtues concerning character and behavior, like temperance or courage.</note> and **intellectual** <note>Virtues concerning the mind’s grasp of truth, like science or art.</note> ones, and happiness cannot be placed in the moral virtues, as the cited men clearly show, it remains that it must be in the intellectual ones. However, when the intellectual virtues are divided into their parts, as is clear from the sixth book of the *Ethics*, happiness does not seem capable of being rationally placed in any except in the habit of **wisdom** <note>original: "sapientia"—the highest intellectual virtue according to Aristotle.</note>, which primarily contemplates God; this appears to be the clear opinion of Aristotle in the tenth book of the *Ethics*. 

But even this is refuted for many reasons: First, because such knowledge requires a man of exceedingly excellent **intellect** <note>original: "ingenij"</note>, and besides this, one who is totally renounced from worldly affairs, of a good nature—namely, healthy—and not lacking in life's necessities. However, such people are necessarily very rare; even over long ages, hardly one is found, as daily experience teaches and histories declare. This, however, is contrary to the nature of happiness, for it is a good suitable for any man who is not deprived <note>original: "orbato"—meaning a person who possesses their natural faculties and is not "blind" or "diminished."</note> of his faculties, since every man naturally desires it. Furthermore, because such knowledge is very weak and much uncertain, since it is more so...

# CHAPTER XIII.

->*In which many arduous difficulties are raised against what has been said.*<-

Truly, greater difficulties arise against this position, which, as far as I can see, are not easy to satisfy.

First, because if the **human soul** <term>anima humana; the vital principle of a human being</term> is mortal, as has been concluded, then no **ultimate end** <note>original: "vltimus finis"; the final purpose or supreme good toward which human life is directed.</note> will be given for man as man, and thus he will not be **capable of happiness** <note>original: "felicitabilis"</note>; but the opposite of this is posited by **Aristotle** in the first book of the *Ethics*, as is clear enough. It is also against the common saying, where it is said that man is an "animal capable of happiness," since he is **capable of reason** <note>original: "rationis capax"</note>. This is also evident from its opposite, since in the second book of the *Physics*, misfortune or unhappiness does not apply to beasts, but only to **rational beings** <note>original: "rationalia"</note>: Therefore, the soul is not mortal. All these points are clear except for the conditional statement which is the **major premise** <note>The first part of a logical argument (syllogism).</note>—namely, that if the soul were mortal, there would be no ultimate end for man as man—which is proved thus: since if there were any ultimate end...

Since such [happiness] cannot be placed in the **vegetative and sensitive** <term>vegetativa et sensitiva; the "lower" functions of the soul responsible for growth and basic senses, shared with plants and animals</term> parts of the soul, nor in the goods of the body or of fortune <note>In philosophy, "goods of fortune" refer to external things like wealth, power, or reputation that are subject to luck.</note>, as **Aristotle** demonstrates strictly enough in the first book of the *Ethics*, and **Boethius** extensively in the second and third books of *The Consolation of Philosophy*, and **St. Thomas** <note>Thomas Aquinas (1225–1274), the influential Catholic theologian and philosopher.</note> in the third book of *Against the Gentiles*: Therefore, his happiness should be placed in the **goods of the soul** or its virtues. 

But since the virtues of the soul are divided into the **moral** and the **intellectual**, and since happiness cannot be placed in the moral virtues (as the men cited above clearly show), it remains that it must reside in the intellectual ones. However, having divided the intellectual virtues into their specific parts—as is evident in the sixth book of the *Ethics*—it seems that happiness cannot reasonably be placed in any of them, except in the habit of **wisdom** <term>sapientia; for Scholastic thinkers, this meant the highest intellectual virtue, grasping the first causes of things</term>, which focuses primarily on the contemplation of God. This indeed seems to be Aristotle's clear opinion in the tenth book of the *Ethics*. 

But even this is refuted by many reasons: 

First, because such knowledge requires a person of very excellent intellect, and besides this, one totally detached from worldly things, of a good nature—that is, healthy—and not lacking in the necessities of life. Truly, such people are very rare; even in the span of many centuries, barely one is found, as daily experience teaches and histories declare. This, however, contradicts the very nature of happiness, because it is a good suitable for **every man** who is not impaired, since every man naturally desires it <note>The author is arguing that if happiness were only for the elite "philosopher-king" types, it wouldn't fit the human nature shared by everyone.</note>. 

Furthermore, because such knowledge is very weak and very uncertain, since it is more [of...]

It is based on opinion rather than scientific certainty, as shown by the diversity of views; for in these matters, one can hardly find two people who agree. Our own mode of understanding proves this too, as it operates through the **senses**, yet these [highest] things are most remote from sensory perception. Thus, it should be called **ignorance** rather than **knowledge**, and suspicion rather than certainty. Who, then, would claim this is happiness? For in true happiness we find rest <note>quiescimus; the philosophical idea that the ultimate "good" satisfies all desire, leaving the soul in a state of peace.</note>, but in this state of intellectual inquiry, we stumble and find no rest.

Furthermore, happiness has the character of an **end** <term>termini; a final destination or goal that completes a process</term>, whereas this intellectual pursuit is entirely "on the road" <term>in via; a common Scholastic metaphor for earthly life as a journey toward a destination</term>, since no one knows as much as they are capable of knowing. Indeed, the more one knows, the more one desires to know.

Additionally, how many arts, how many sciences, how many labors, and how many sleepless nights are required to grasp it! Since a single lifetime is scarcely enough to master one art, how could one reach such a final goal? And when one considers how uncertain he is of his life, of his own strength, and of the events that might block him or strike him down <note>tollere eum de medio; literally "to take him from the midst [of life]," referring to sudden death.</note>, how can he embark on such a vast and difficult journey?

Furthermore, since the time needed to produce such happiness is so long, and the path to reaching it so difficult—for it requires a man to almost entirely renounce his body <note>abdicate à corpore; the idea that high intellectual contemplation requires ignoring physical needs and sensations.</note> while he is still in the body—and since the outcome is doubtful, and even after reaching it, he might lose it in a moment, either through death or some other accident, how then could this [be considered happiness?]...

# On the Soul
<note>original: "Animæ"</note>

...should it not more truly be called unhappiness than happiness? For this reason, many have argued—and not without reason—that if the human soul is mortal, the condition of a human being is far worse than that of any beast. This is evident when one considers the **frailty** <term>imbecillitate; here meaning physical vulnerability or weakness</term> of the human body, which is subject to so many illnesses, and the restlessness of the mind, which is constantly turned this way and that.

Secondly, if we assume the mortality of the human soul, a person should never, under any circumstances—no matter how urgent—choose death. This would effectively do away with **fortitude** <term>fortitudo; the classical virtue of courage or "bravery in the face of death"</term>, which commands us to look down upon death. It would mean we should not choose death for the sake of our country or the **public good** <note>bono publico; the welfare of the community</note>, nor should we risk our life <note>original: "animam nostram"; the Latin word *anima* refers to both the "soul" and the "life-breath" or physical life</note> for a friend. Indeed, one would be driven to commit any crime or wicked deed rather than face death. 

Such a conclusion contradicts **Aristotle** in the third and ninth books of his *Ethics*. It is also against nature itself; we see proof of this in the fact that we naturally despise those who commit such crimes even to save their own lives, and we condemn them. Conversely, we naturally love and praise those who do the opposite. It is clear enough why this logic follows: all choice is based on the pursuit of one's own good, but if death destroys the "whole good," then there is nothing in death that could ever be desirable. This is also clear from **Plato** in his *Phaedo* <note>The *Phaedo* is Plato's famous dialogue depicting the final hours of Socrates, focusing on the immortality of the soul.</note>, where he testifies that death could not be endured with a calm mind unless there were hope for a better life. He also states in the fifth book of his *Laws* that he who...

-># On Immortality<-
<note>original: "De Immortalitate"</note>

...whoever considers life the highest good disgraces it. Divine Saint **Thomas** <note>Thomas Aquinas, the 13th-century theologian</note> also in his *Ethics* <note>referring to his commentary on Aristotle's Nicomachean Ethics</note> almost doubts how those who claim the soul is mortal can ever choose death; and in his *Exposition of the Apostles' Creed* <note>original: "expositione symboli Apostolorum"</note>, he says regarding that article on **the resurrection of the flesh** <note>original: "carnis resurrectionem"</note> that without a doubt, if there were no hope of resurrection, any crime whatsoever <note>original: "flagitium"</note> should be committed rather than facing death.

Thirdly, because it follows that either God is not the governor of the Universe, or He is unjust—both of which conclusions are a wickedness to suggest. For if He did not govern all things, He would not be God; and if He is God, He is the **highest good** <note>original: "summum bonum"</note>, since He possesses no **potentiality** <term>potentialitatis; in Scholastic philosophy, this refers to the capacity to change or be "less than" perfect; God is considered "pure act," meaning He is already fully realized and perfect</term>; and if He is the highest good, how could there be injustice in Him? This injustice is clearly seen [if the soul is mortal], because so many evils occur in this world that are unknown to men, and if they are known, they are left unpunished; indeed, often men receive the greatest rewards for their evil deeds. Conversely, regarding the good, they are either not recognized or, if they are recognized, they remain without reward, and many times suffer death and loss. Now, if God either does not know these things, or if He knows them and yet leaves them unpunished or without reward, then—as **Jerome** <note>St. Jerome, the 4th-century Church Father</note> says—He is not God.

Fourthly, because all laws, both those that have been and those that currently are, posit that the soul remains after the body; and thus this is a most famous truth, celebrated throughout the whole world...

**Pliny the Younger** also narrates that there was an infamous house in Athens in which the image of a terrible old man was seen and loud noises were heard. He tells how the philosopher **Athenodorus of Tarsus** <note>A Stoic philosopher and friend of Augustus</note> saw that image after renting the house. With the ghost as his guide, he discovered in the courtyard of the house, buried under the earth, bones bound in chains. Once they were buried properly according to custom, the house was thereafter freed from the noise.

**Posidonius the Stoic** also relates that when two friends from Arcadia came to **Megara** <note>A coastal city in Greece</note>, one went to an inn. When they had rested after dinner, in the night he appeared in a dream...

To the one who was in the lodging, the other man <note>The author is recounting a famous story of two friends traveling together, recorded by Cicero in *De Divinatione*</note> seemed to beg that he come to his aid because a plot was being prepared for his destruction by the innkeeper <term>caupone; a keeper of a tavern or inn, often stereotyped in antiquity as dishonest</term>. At first, the man woke from his sleep terrified; then, once he had composed himself and decided that the vision should be considered meaningless, he lay back down. Then, while he slept, that same figure appeared to ask that, since he had not come to his aid while he was alive, he should at least not allow his death to go unavenged. He explained that he had been killed by the innkeeper, thrown into a wagon, and covered with manure. He asked that the friend be present at the gate early in the morning before the wagon left the town. Moved by this dream, he was indeed present at the gate in the morning for the ox-driver <term>bubulco; a teamster or driver of oxen</term>. He asked what was in the wagon; the driver fled in terror, the dead body was unearthed, and once the matter was revealed, the innkeeper paid the penalty.

**Simonides** <note>Simonides of Ceos, a Greek lyric poet famous for his memory and wisdom</note> also, when he had seen the corpse of an unknown man cast out and had buried him, and was intending to board a ship, seemed to be warned by the man he had provided with a burial not to do it; for if he sailed, he would perish in a shipwreck. Simonides stayed behind, and the others were shipwrecked. Countless examples of this kind could be brought forward. I myself also bear witness that I have had many things through dreams which are like counsel; therefore, these things seem to clearly declare that the souls of the dead exist.

### CHAPTER XIV.

Indeed, it seems to me an arduous and burdensome task to satisfy these arguments, especially because it is a famous belief that the soul remains after death; and, as is written in the second book of the *Metaphysics* <note>The author refers to Aristotle’s *Metaphysics*, Book II (Alpha Minor), where Aristotle discusses the difficulty of seeking truth and the influence of "custom" or "habit" on how we receive information</note>, it is difficult to speak against custom. Nevertheless, as far as our ability in this matter allows, we will at least try to speak with probability.

Therefore, as a response to the first point, it must be known that every thing—at least every perfect thing—has some end <note>original: "finem"; in this context, "end" means the ultimate purpose, goal, or "final cause" of a thing's existence</note>, and that this end possesses the nature of the good <note>original: "boni rationem"</note>, as is said in the second book of the *Metaphysics*. However, it is not the case that whatever is a "greater good" should be assigned as the end for every single thing. Rather, the end is assigned only according to what suits that specific nature and is proportionate to it. 

For although to possess sensation <note>original: "sentire"; the ability to feel or perceive via the senses</note> is better than not to possess sensation, sensation nevertheless does not suit a stone, nor would it be a "good" for the stone. If it were, it would no longer be a stone. Wherefore, in assigning a purpose to man, if we were to assign one such as we assign to God and the intelligences <term>Intelligences; in medieval and Renaissance philosophy, these were non-physical, purely intellectual beings (often associated with angels) that were thought to move the celestial spheres</term>, it would not be a suitable assign- <note>The text cuts off here at the end of the page</note>

<vocab>Immortality, soul, Metaphysics, Aristotle, God, intelligence, the purpose of the good</vocab>

<summary>The author begins Chapter 14 by acknowledging the difficulty of arguing against the common belief in the soul's immortality. He introduces the Aristotelian concept that every being has a specific "end" or purpose that must be proportionate to its unique nature, rather than simply being the "highest possible" good.</summary>
<keywords>Aristotle, Metaphysics, final cause, human nature, purpose, celestial intelligences, philosophical method</keywords>

--- Page 112 ---
<meta>This page continues the author's response to objections regarding the soul. The previous page ended with a discussion on the "end" or purpose of things, specifically addressing whether sensation or some other perfection constitutes the end of man. <page-num>105</page-num>
<header>The Soul.</header>

perfection, since in that way he would not be a man.

Secondly, it must be accepted and especially committed to memory that the whole human race can be compared to one individual man. In a single human individual, there are multiple and diverse members <note>original: "membra"; the author uses this term to refer to both limbs and internal organs</note>, which are ordered toward diverse duties or diverse immediate ends. Nevertheless, all are assigned to a single ultimate end; for this reason, all those parts must share in certain common traits. If this order were violated, either the man would not exist, or he would exist in a state of dysfunction <note>original: "incommodè", implying a state of being poorly suited or ill-disposed for life</note>. Indeed, all members are ordered for the common utility of the man himself. One part is either necessary for another (and vice versa) or at least useful, though sometimes one is more so and another less.

Thus, the heart is necessary for the brain, and the brain for the heart; the heart is necessary for the hand, while the hand is useful to the heart. The right hand is useful to the left, and the left to the right. All members share in life and natural heat, and they require spirit <note>original: "spiritu"; in historical physiology, "spirit" refers to the vital substances or refined air that carries life and sensation through the body</note> and blood, as is clearly seen in the book *On Animals* <note>This refers to Aristotle's biological works, specifically *On the Parts of Animals* or *History of Animals*</note>. 

Beyond these things which they share, each individual member has its own specific duty: for the heart has one, the brain another, the liver <note>original: "hepar"</note> another, and so on for the rest. Aristotle declares this in the same book *On Animals*, but Galen <note>Claudius Galenus, the influential 2nd-century physician and philosopher</note> explains it more fully in his book *On the Usefulness of Parts* <note>original: "De vtilitate particularum", usually cited as *De usu partium*</note>. However, these duties or works are not equal, but...

<summary>The author uses a "macrocosm-microcosm" analogy, comparing the whole human race to a single human body where various organs (heart, brain, liver) serve specific roles but work toward a common utility. This biological framework, drawing on Aristotle and Galen, sets the stage for understanding the soul's function within the human collective.</summary>

For who is there who is ignorant of the first principles? For example, it is said of anything that it either "is" or "is not"; it is impossible for the same thing to be and not to be at the same time. Who is entirely ignorant of **God**, of **being**, of the **one**, of the **true**, or of the **good**? And so it is with the rest of these concepts, which would be too burdensome to run through individually; however, these belong to the study of **Metaphysics**. 

Regarding **natural science**, it is also manifest, since those things which first occur to the intellect are subject to the senses. In **mathematics**, it is also easy to see, since human life cannot be led without numbers and figures; all humans know the hours, days, months, and years, and many other things which belong to the business of **Astronomy**. No less, unless a person is blind, does he know something of visions, which is the work of **Perspective** <note>original: "Perspectivæ"; in the medieval and Renaissance period, this term referred to the science of optics.</note>; and unless he is deaf, he knows something of harmonies, which pertains to **Music**. What shall I say, moreover, of **Rhetoric** and **Dialectic**? Since **Aristotle** says in the introduction to his *Rhetoric* that, for this reason, all people participate in some way in both...

**Babus:** Regarding the **<term>practical intellect</term>** <note>original: "intellectus operatiuus"; the part of the mind concerned with action, ethics, and social organization.</note> which deals with morals, states, and domestic affairs, it is most evident—since it is granted to everyone to distinguish good from evil and to be a part of a city and a family—that an intellect of this kind is truly and properly called "human." This is testified to by **Plato** in his *Republic* and **Aristotle** in his *Ethics*. 

As for the **<term>productive intellect</term>** <note>original: "intellectus factiuus"; the mind’s capacity for technical skill, crafts, and creating physical objects.</note>, it is plainly obvious that no man can lead his life without it; for without the mechanical arts and the things necessary for life, a human being cannot endure. 

However, one must understand that although no single human is entirely deprived of these three aforementioned types of intellect, humans do not relate to them all in the same way. For the **<term>speculative intellect</term>** <note>original: "intellectus speculatiuus"; the highest form of thought, dedicated to pure contemplation of truth for its own sake.</note> is not strictly human, but belongs to the Gods, as Aristotle teaches in the tenth book of the *Ethics* <note>Aristotle, *Nicomachean Ethics*, Book X, where he argues that the life of contemplation is "higher than the human level."</note> and Plato in the *Timaeus*, where he says that **Philosophy** is the greatest gift of the Gods. Therefore, in no way does a man share this capacity with other mortals in a common sense. For this reason, even if all men possess some small portion of it, nevertheless very few possess it—or are even capable of possessing it—exactly and perfectly. 

Because of this, it happens that the portion of the human race that gives itself over entirely to speculation holds the same position among the human species as the **heart** does among the limbs. This is true even though there is a wide range of modes within this group, such as some being mathematicians, some physicists, and some physicians...

# On Immortality

<page-num>110</page-num>

While some people are indeed **metaphysicians**, there is a wide range among all these modes of intellect, as is quite evident. The **<term>productive intellect</term>** <note>original: "intellectus factiuus"; the mind's capacity for technical skill, crafts, and physical creation.</note>, which is the lowest and most mechanical kind, is common to all humans. Indeed, even beasts share in this, as **Aristotle** teaches in his book *On the History of Animals*, since many beasts build structures <note>original: "causas construant"; referring to animals building nests or habitats.</note> and do many other things that demonstrate a productive intellect. This type of intellect is extremely necessary, so much so that the greater part of humanity is occupied with it. 

For this reason, the female sex is almost entirely devoted to this—tasks such as weaving, spinning, sewing, and so on—while the largest portion of men is dedicated to agriculture and then to various other crafts. Furthermore, someone who is devoted to one craft cannot easily master another. For this reason, **Plato** in his *Republic* and **Aristotle** in his *Politics* commanded that, just as a single limb cannot perform many different tasks well, a craftsman should not try to practice multiple crafts; for he will achieve success in neither. 

On the other hand, the **<term>practical intellect</term>** <note>original: "operatiuus intellectus"; also known as the "active" or "moral" intellect, used for ethical decision-making.</note> truly belongs to the human being. Every person, unless completely deprived of their faculties, can achieve it perfectly. It is according to this intellect that a person is called "good" or "bad" in a simple and absolute sense. Regarding the speculative or productive intellects, however, one is only called "good" or "bad" in a certain respect <note>original: "secundùm quid"; a logical term meaning "in a qualified sense." For example, one can be a "good" builder but a "bad" person.</note> and with qualification. For it is according to virtues and vices that a person is called a "good man" or a "bad man"; but...

at <meta>catchword: at</meta>

<page-num>111</page-num>

<header>Soul.</header>

A **good metaphysician** is not called a "good man," but rather a "good metaphysician"; likewise, a **good builder** is not termed "good" in an absolute sense, but is instead called a "good builder." For this reason, a person does not take offense if they are not referred to as a metaphysician, a physicist, or a craftsman. 

But if someone is called a thief, or described as intemperate, unjust, imprudent, or any such <term>vicious</term> <note>original: "vitiosum"; meaning full of vice or moral failing rather than simply "mean."</note> thing, they become extremely indignant and incensed. It is as if they recognize that being virtuous or vicious is essentially what it means to be human, and that these qualities are placed within our own power. 

On the other hand, being a philosopher or a builder is not within our innate control, nor is it strictly necessary for a human being. Thus, it follows that while all people can and ought to be <term>well-mannered</term> <note>original: "bene morigerati"; here implying moral uprightness and ethical conduct.</note>, it is not required that everyone be a philosopher, a mathematician, a builder, and so on. Indeed, the human race could not persist unless there were such a diversity of roles, just as was said previously regarding the different parts of the body. 

Returning, then, to our main point, we say that the ultimate goal of the human race in general is to participate in those three types of intellect <note>The speculative (theoretical), practical (moral), and productive (technical) intellects.</note>. It is through these that humans communicate and live together, and one person is either useful or necessary to another. This is just as all the limbs in a single person share in the <term>vital spirit</term> <note>original: "spiritu vitali"; in the philosophy of this era, the "vital spirit" was the life-force that coordinated the body's functions.</note> and perform mutual functions for one another. 

From this goal, no human can be exempt. However, regarding the **practical intellect**, which is the intellect that properly belongs to man, every individual must possess it perfectly. For this reason, so that the human race—

He compares the diversity of human roles to the different limbs of a body, all coordinated by a shared purpose.</summary>

...if the human race is to be rightly preserved, every person ought to be morally virtuous and, as much as possible, be free from vice. Vice is imputed to a person as their own failing in whatever state they may be found—whether they are needy, poor, wealthy, middle-class, or opulent. 

Regarding the other types of intellect, however, mastery is not necessary; indeed, it is neither possible nor suitable for the human race. For the world would not stand if everyone were a <term>speculative</term> <note>original: "speculatiuus"; referring to the "theoretical intellect" used for abstract philosophy and science.</note> thinker. Indeed, such a person could not even survive on their own, since it is impossible for one single class of people—such as physicians—to be self-sufficient. Nor could there be a class consisting only of builders, or any other such profession. It is impossible for one person to perfectly perform the work of another, let alone the work of everyone. This is just as we see in the limbs of the body, where each part has a specific function. 

Therefore, the universal goal of the human race is to participate <term>in a certain respect</term> <note>original: "secundum quid"; a logical term meaning "relatively" or "in a qualified sense" rather than absolutely.</note> in the speculative and <term>productive</term> <note>original: "factiuo"; the "intellect of making," used in crafts and arts.</note> intellects, but to participate perfectly in the <term>practical</term> <note>original: "practico"; the "intellect of doing," which governs moral choices and ethics.</note> intellect. For the universe would be most perfectly preserved if all people were diligent and good, but not if everyone were a philosopher, or a smith, or a house-builder. 

Furthermore, the moral virtues do not function like the arts and sciences. In the arts, one skill might hinder another, or applying oneself to one field might prevent mastery of another. However, as is said in the *Ethics* <note>Aristotle's *Nicomachean Ethics*, a standard textbook for moral philosophy in the Renaissance.</note>, the moral virtues are interconnected: whoever perfectly possesses one possesses them all. 

For this reason, all people ought to be diligent and good. But being a philosopher, a mathematician, or an architect are only partial goals, just as...

For this reason, it is said in accordance with logic that if man is mortal, every human being can possess the goal that fits humanity universally. However, the goal that belongs only to the most perfect part is neither possible nor appropriate for everyone, just as not every limb can have the perfection of the heart or the eye. Indeed, a living creature could not even exist if this were the case. 

Similarly, if every human being were a <term>speculative</term> <note>original: "speculatiuus"; one dedicated to pure theoretical study and contemplation.</note> thinker, human community would not hold together. This is why many <term>climates</term> <note>original: "climata"; referring to the distinct geographic zones of the earth.</note> and diverse regions are necessary. Therefore, happiness does not consist in a "speculative habit" achieved through logical demonstration as if it were something universally fitting for the entire human race; rather, it belongs to the first, principal part of humanity. And however much the remaining parts strive toward such happiness...

<page-num>115</page-num>

<header>The Soul</header>

<note>The text begins mid-sentence, continuing from "habere" on the previous page.</note> [To possess] happiness is characteristic of the Gods, who are in no way dependent on matter and change. The opposite is true for the human race, which occupies a middle ground among mortal things. 

Regarding the further argument that the ultimate goal <term>end</term> <note>In Aristotelian philosophy, the "end" or "finis" is the final purpose or goal of a thing.</note> must bring rest, but that this [earthly happiness] does not bring the human intellect and will to a state of rest: it is said here that **Aristotle**, at the end of the first book of the <term>Ethics</term> <note>Referring to the *Nicomachean Ethics*, Aristotle’s primary work on virtue and happiness.</note>, does not define human happiness as something that brings perfect, unmoving rest. On the contrary, he maintains that however happy a person may be, they are not so stable that many things might not disturb them—for if they were not disturbed, they would not be human. 

However, these disturbances do not remove a person from happiness, just as not every wind uproots a tree, even though it may move the leaves. Therefore, in human happiness, a stability that cannot be fully removed is sufficient, even if it is somewhat subject to trouble. 

Indeed, this applies even across every age of life. In youth, for instance, even if a person does not have the exact knowledge that is appropriate for adulthood, so long as they have the knowledge suitable for youth, they are content for that age. They do not desire more than is fitting for them, and so they will not be distressed <note>original: "angustiabitur"; to be narrowed or pressured by anxiety.</note> as was previously suggested. 

And as for the further claim that a person never knows as much as they are capable of knowing, nor knows as clearly as they might: I say that this does not take away from their happiness, provided they possess as much as is appropriate for their state of life <note>original: "statu"</note> and do not fail on their own part. For it is the mark of a moderate <term>desire</term> <note>original: "appetitus"</note> to desire only as much as one can love; likewise, it is the mark of a moderate person to be satisfied with what is appropriate for them.

<summary>The author uses Aristotle's Ethics to argue that human happiness does not require the absolute, unchanging perfection of the Gods, but rather a stability appropriate to one's human nature and stage of life.</summary>

<page-num>116</page-num>

# On Immortality

...[and he] can have [contentment]. And when it was further argued that since a human being knows they will soon lose their knowledge, and that it can be taken away in many ways, it would be more a source of misery to them than happiness: to this it is said that it is the mark of an ungenerous <term>illiberal</term> person <note>original: "illiberalis hominis"; in the philosophical tradition, an "illiberal" person lacks the noble, generous spirit associated with a free and educated mind.</note> to be unwilling to return what they received for free, given that humans are presupposed to be mortal. For even the ancients called it a "purifying" <note>original: "purgatoriam"; likely referring to the "purifying virtues" (virtutes purgatoriae) which, according to Neoplatonists, help the soul detach from the body in preparation for death.</note> state; since a person receives life on the condition that they know they will yield it back to nature, they will give thanks to God and nature, and will always be prepared to die. They will not fear death, since fear of the inevitable is pointless, and they see no evil in death.

And when it was further inferred that the human condition would be far worse than that of any beast: certainly, in my opinion, this was not said in a philosophical manner. For while the actions of beasts may be considered peaceful within their own kind, they are considered restless and incomplete when compared to the intellectual realm. Who would rather be a stone or a long-lived deer <note>Ancient writers like Hesiod and Pliny the Elder famously claimed that stags/deer could live for hundreds of years, far outlasting humans.</note> than a human being, however lowly? For a wise person, in any state or time, can maintain a quiet mind even though they might be troubled by bodily afflictions. Indeed, a wise person would choose to be in extreme necessity and in the greatest of troubles rather than be foolish, cowardly, and morally corrupt in the opposite condition. Nor is it true that when one sees the immense labors, the turning away from bodily pleasures, the dim understanding of things, and the easy loss of what has been gained, one would rather...

Just as the life of a human, however brief it may be, is to be preferred to the life of a beast, however long-lasting it is; for Aristotle, in the first book of the *Ethics* <note>original: "1. ethic."; referring to the *Nicomachean Ethics*, Aristotle’s primary work on moral philosophy</note>, says that a long life is not to be preferred to a short life unless all other things are equal. In such a case, death is not chosen for its own sake—since death is the absence of being—but rather the virtuous <term>studiosus</term> <note>original: "studiosus"; here used to describe a person or action characterized by moral diligence and zeal for what is right</note> act is chosen, even though death may follow from it. Similarly, when one refuses to commit a vice, they are not rejecting life (which is good in itself), but rather they are rejecting the vice, even if committing that vice would have allowed them to keep living. 

As for what is mentioned in the *Phaedo* <note>Plato’s dialogue depicting the final hours of Socrates, focusing on the soul's nature</note>, Plato also posits in the *Republic* and the *Crito* that just as a life burdened by an incurable illness should be rejected—indeed, removed from the living—so too must a soul filled with sin be uprooted. If a soul were to live eternally in sin, it would be the height of misery, since nothing is worse for the soul than vice itself.

# The Soul.

[...no evil] can [happen to a good man]; moreover, Aristotle demonstrates in the 7th book of the *Ethics* <note>original: "7. ethic."; referring to Aristotle's *Nicomachean Ethics*, where he discusses the self-destructive nature of vice.</note> that the life of the wicked is perverse and to be avoided above all else. There, he shows that for the wicked man, all things are out of harmony; he is faithful to no one, and he finds no rest whether waking or sleeping. He is tormented by dreadful agonies of body and soul in a most unhappy life, so much so that no wise man—however poor, physically weak, or lacking in the goods of fortune—would choose the life of a tyrant or some powerful wicked man. Instead, the wise man would prefer to remain in his own condition.

Therefore, every virtuous person is rewarded by their own virtue and happiness. For this reason, Aristotle, in the 30th section of his *Problems*, problem 10, asks why prizes are awarded in athletic contests but not for virtues and sciences. He says this happens because virtue is its own reward; for since a prize ought to be more excellent than the contest itself, and nothing can be more excellent than <term>prudence</term> <note>original: "prudentia"; in this context, it refers to practical wisdom or the ability to govern oneself rightly.</note>, it is its own reward.

But the opposite happens regarding vice. Thus, no wicked person is left unpunished, since vice itself is a punishment to the wicked person. However, rewards or punishments are <term>accidental</term> <note>In Aristotelian philosophy, "accidental" (accidentales) refers to external things like money or reputation that are not part of the essence of the action itself.</note> when they can be separated [from the act]—such as gold or damages of whatever kind. Thus, not every good deed is remunerated [externally], and not every evil deed is punished [externally], nor is this inappropriate, since these things are accidental.

One must, however, know two things here: first, that an accidental reward is far less than an essential punishment; for an accidental punishment is...

# On Immortality

...punishment of loss <note>original: "pœna damni"; a legal and theological term referring to the loss of a benefit or external good, such as money, status, or divine vision.</note>, while the punishment of guilt <note>original: "pœna culpæ"; the internal moral degradation or "stain" on the soul resulting from a wrong act.</note> is essential. Now, the punishment of guilt is far worse than the essential punishment; for the accidental punishment is the punishment of loss, but the essential one is the punishment of guilt. Indeed, the punishment of guilt is far more wretched than the punishment of loss. Therefore, it matters little if the accidental is sometimes omitted, so long as the essential remains.

Secondly, it should further be understood that when a good deed is rewarded <term>accidentally</term> <note>In this context, "accidentally" (accidentaliter) means through external means like money, fame, or physical prizes, which are not part of the act of virtue itself.</note>, the essential good seems to be diminished, and it does not remain in its full perfection. For example, if someone acts virtuously without any hope of reward, and another acts with the hope of reward, the act of the second is held to be not as virtuous as that of the first. Therefore, the one who is not rewarded accidentally is more rewarded in an essential sense than the one who is rewarded accidentally.

In the same way, the person who acts viciously and is punished accidentally seems to be punished less than the one who is not punished accidentally. This is because the punishment of guilt is greater and more terrible than the punishment of loss; and when the punishment of loss is joined to guilt, it diminishes the guilt. Consequently, the person who is not punished accidentally is punished more essentially than the one who is punished accidentally. <note>The author's provocative argument here is that an unpunished criminal is actually in a worse state because they have not "paid" for their crime externally, leaving the internal rot of the vice entirely unmitigated.</note>

This is also evidenced by what **Laertius** <note>Diogenes Laertius, the author of "Lives of Eminent Philosophers," a standard source for ancient biographical anecdotes in the Renaissance.</note> writes concerning **Aristotle**. For when **Aristotle** was asked what he had gained from **Philosophy**, he replied: "What you do through the hope of rewards and flee from through the fear of punishment, I do out of the love and nobility of virtue, and I flee from it because of the reproach of vice." # On Immortality

...[praise] and through the fear of infamy <note>original: "infamia"; referring to social disgrace or a bad reputation.</note> they work diligently and flee from vices; these people are in the second degree. Others, however, are made diligent because of the hope of some physical good or the fear of corporal punishment. Therefore, so that such men may achieve virtue, **statesmen** <note>original: "politici"</note> establish rewards like gold, or high status, or something similar; and so that they flee from vice, they decree that men be punished through their money, their honor, or their bodies—either by the mutilation of a limb or by being put to death.

Yet, some people are moved by none of these things because of the ferocity and perversity of their nature, as daily experience teaches us. Therefore, the lawgivers posited eternal rewards in another life for the virtuous, and eternal punishments <note>original: "damna"; literally 'losses' or 'damnation.'</note> for the vicious, which would terrify them the most. Indeed, the greater part of men, if they do good at all, do so more out of the fear of eternal punishment than from the hope of eternal reward, since those punishments are more easily understood by us than those eternal goods.

And since this final device <note>original: "ingenium"; a clever invention or strategic "noble lie."</note> can be useful to all men of whatever degree they may be, the **legislator** <note>original: "legislator"; the one who crafts the laws of a society.</note>—observing how prone men's ways are toward evil and intending the common good—decreed that the soul is immortal. In doing so, he did not care for the truth, but only for uprightness, so that he might lead men toward virtue. Nor should the statesman be accused of wrongdoing; for just as a physician feigns <note>original: "fingit"; creates a fiction or "white lie."</note> many things to restore health to a sick patient, so the statesman fashions **fables** <note>original: "Apologos"; stories, myths, or moral parables.</note> so that he may set the citizens on the right path.

In truth, regarding these fables, as <term>Averroes</term> <note>A 12th-century Andalusian philosopher whose commentaries on Aristotle were central to medieval and Renaissance thought.</note> says in the prologue to the third book of the *Physics*, there is strictly speaking neither truth nor falsehood.
