[nur]ses lead their pupils to those things they know will benefit children; for if a man were healthy or of sound mind, neither the physician nor the nurse would need such fictions. For this reason, if all men were of that first degree previously listed, they would become diligent even if the mortality of souls <note>original: "animorum mortalitate"; the idea that the soul dies with the body.</note> were established; but almost no one is of that disposition. Therefore, it was necessary to proceed with other devices <note>original: "ingeniis"; here meaning strategic inventions or psychological methods.</note>, and this is not inappropriate, since human nature is almost entirely immersed in matter and participates very little in the intellect. Thus, man is further removed from the **intelligences** <note>original: "intelligentiis"; referring to purely intellectual or divine beings, such as angels or the prime movers of the heavens.</note> than a sick man is from a healthy one, a boy from a man, or a fool from a wise man. It is therefore no wonder if the **statesman** <note>original: "politicus"; the person responsible for the order and morality of the city-state.</note> makes use of such things.

In the fifth main point, two matters were touched upon: one concerning things seen in tombs, and the other concerning dreams. Regarding the first of these, it is said first that many things recounted in histories are, in fact, mere fables. It is said, secondly, that in the vicinity of tombs, for the most part, the air is very thick <note>original: "crassus"; meaning dense or heavy.</note>, both from the evaporation of corpses and from the coldness of the stones, and from many other factors that bring about a denseness of the air. But as it is stated in the **Meteorology, Book 3, the chapter on the Rainbow** <term>3. meteor. cap. de Iride; referring to Aristotle’s work on natural phenomena</term>, such air easily receives the **images** <term>idola; from the Greek 'eidola,' referring to visual reflections or sensory forms that detach from objects</term> of surrounding things, just as a mirror receives figures. For this reason, things seen in air that is so disposed are thought by unrefined men to be the actual things appearing there, just as children looking into a mirror or into water believe that the things they see...

<page-num>126</page-num>
# On Immortality

...they seem to truly be there. For Aristotle reports a story of a certain man with weak eyesight who, looking at his own shadow in the night, believed it was a man following him. This explains how <term>uneducated men</term> <note>Latin: *rudes homines*. Refers to those without philosophical or scientific training.</note>, because of such an accident, come to believe that these shadows are the souls of the deceased. Both the imagination and common rumors also contribute to these beliefs. Therefore, as Aristotle reports in the second chapter of *On Sleep and Waking*, many things are thought to be seen by those gripped by fear or other intense emotions—even if they are wide awake—which nevertheless do not exist, just as happens to the sick.

Thirdly, it is said that this happens many times due to the illusions and deceits of corrupt priests, as we read in the final chapter of Daniel regarding the <term>Idol of Bel</term> <note>original: "Idolo Bel". This refers to the story in the extended Book of Daniel (Bel and the Dragon) where Daniel exposes the fraud of priests who were secretly consuming food offerings left for an idol.</note>. Indeed, many priests and guardians of temples have traded the four <term>Cardinal Virtues</term> <note>Prudence, Justice, Fortitude, and Temperance—the foundation of moral life in classical and Christian thought.</note> for ambition, greed, gluttony, and lust. All other vices follow from these; thus, so that they may enjoy their desired ends, they employ these frauds and fictions, just as we know has occurred sometimes in our own era.

Fourthly, it is said that while many histories of the Greeks and Romans relate wonders, it is most certain that omens appeared at the birth and death of men worthy of memory. For Suetonius Tranquillus, in his book *On the Twelve Caesars*, tells of great signs observed through the flight of birds, the responses of the gods, and many other things. No less does Plutarch in his book *On the Lives of Illustrious Men*. Our Virgil also sang of these things at the end of the first book of the *Georgics*.

...the splendor of their arms, and every kind of breastplate. <note>This completes the description of the heavenly army from the previous page.</note> For this reason, everyone prayed that these portents might be turned toward something good. Therefore, the previous explanations <note>The psychological or skeptical arguments mentioned earlier.</note> do not seem to fully exhaust the matter; moreover, to deny such occurrences seems to be a sign of great obstinacy and impudence. For this reason, we must speak differently:

Having conceded, therefore, that these things are not fictions, nor illusions, nor merely our imaginations, it must be said that Christians—and almost all religious traditions <term>laws</term> <note>original: "leges". In this context, "laws" often refers to religious systems or sects.</note>—as well as <term>Plato</term> <note>The famous Greek philosopher who argued for the immortality of the soul.</note>, <term>Avicenna</term> <note>Ibn Sina (c. 980–1037), a Persian polymath whose philosophical works heavily influenced Western thought.</note>, and many others, posit that these events are brought about either by God or through His ministers, whom we call <term>Angels</term> if they are good, and <term>demons</term> if they are evil. It is true that there is some difference of opinion between these thinkers, but we need not concern ourselves with that now.

These thinkers simply concede that the human soul is immortal and exists in many individual forms <note>original: "multiplicatam". This refers to the idea that each person has their own distinct, immortal soul, rather than one collective soul for all humanity.</note>, as is well enough known. However, this clearly contradicts the teachings of <term>Aristotle</term> <note>The "Stagirite" philosopher whose system dominated medieval science and logic.</note>. According to Aristotle, there is no immaterial substance <note>A being without a physical body, such as a soul or a spirit.</note> that does not move a celestial orb. For in the 12th book of his *Metaphysics*, he sets the number of <term>Intelligences</term> <note>Spiritual beings believed to be the "motors" responsible for the movement of the planets and stars.</note> according to the number of the celestial orbs. Furthermore, according to him, there is no effect here in this lower world that is not traced back to the <term>first motion</term> <note>The primary movement of the highest heaven that drives all other movements in the universe.</note>, as is clear in the 8th book of his *Physics* and the 1st book of his *Meteorology*.

Furthermore, because in my view this <note>The individual immortality of the soul.</note> cannot be demonstrated by evident natural reason, we shall not remain strictly within the limits of natural philosophy, even though we promised to do so from the beginning. Therefore, <term>Alexander of Aphrodisias</term> <note>A celebrated commentator on Aristotle (c. 200 AD) who argued that Aristotle's views actually implied the soul was mortal.</note>, as the Doctor <note>likely referring to a "Doctor of the Church" or a specific Scholastic authority.</note> reports...

<meta>This page continues the investigation into the supernatural portents described in the Book of Maccabees. Having established that these events are likely real rather than imaginary, the author now turns to the authority of Thomas Aquinas and Alexander of Aphrodisias to explain how the celestial spheres and spiritual beings might cause such signs on Earth.</meta>

<page-num>129</page-num>
<header>-> #### On the Soul #### <-</header>

**Thomas Aquinas**, in his *Disputed Question on Miracles* (articles 3 and 10 of the main text), stated that these occurrences arise from <term>separate substances</term> <note>In medieval philosophy, these are spiritual beings—like angels or celestial intelligences—that exist independently of physical matter.</note> acting through the medium of the celestial bodies, according to the powers of the stars and their conjunctions and oppositions. Truly, if such effects are granted according to the <term>Peripatetics</term> <note>The followers of Aristotle, known for their focus on the natural world and logic.</note>, it cannot be explained otherwise, since this entire lower world of ours is contiguous with the superior world above, so that all earthly power may be governed from there, as is stated at the beginning of Aristotle’s *Meteorology*. This also seems to be argued quite rationally.

Indeed, **Alexander** <note>Alexander of Aphrodisias (c. 200 AD), a highly influential Greek commentator on Aristotle.</note> holds that God and the <term>intelligences</term> <note>Divine minds or spirits believed to be responsible for the movement of the planets.</note> exercise providence over these lower realms, as Saint Thomas notes in his commentary on the 56th lesson of the second book of *On the Heavens*. Alexander himself expressly confesses this in his book *On Fate*, where he explains that providence for these lower things is provided according to the conditions of times and places—regarding kings, prophets, and other events. 

Therefore, the fact that such portents appeared at one time (as told in the <term>Book of Maccabees</term> <note>A reference to 2 Maccabees 5, which describes visions of golden-clad soldiers charging through the air over Jerusalem.</note>) signaled future wars, just as the eventual outcome proved. However, perhaps those events could have been avoided according to Alexander’s view, since in his book *On Fate* he posits that the will is free. Nor is it surprising if such signs can be fashioned by the celestial bodies, since they are animated by a most noble soul, and they generate and govern all things in the lower world.

The same can be said regarding the accounts that Titus Livius, Suetonius, Virgil, Plutarch, and Lucan <note>These are the great historians and poets of Rome and Greece, often cited in the Renaissance as authorities on the supernatural omens that accompanied the fall of republics or the deaths of Caesars.</note> record. For if signs precede the arrival of spring, summer, and the other seasons—as is clearly manifest—how much more should those <term>intelligences</term> <note>Celestial spirits believed to move the spheres of heaven.</note> be concerned with human nature? 

Indeed, this is very easy to see; for I do not recall reading the life of any man of great importance without finding that many signs preceded both his birth and his death—and even many of his significant actions. Furthermore, what the **Platonists** call a <term>genius</term> <note>A personal tutelary spirit or "daimon" that guides an individual's destiny.</note> or a "familiar spirit," the **Peripatetics** <note>The followers of Aristotle.</note> attribute to a person's nativity. This is because one person is born under one constellation and another under a different one. If we can explain these phenomena without multiplying the number of spirits and geniuses, it seems superfluous to propose them—especially since such ideas do not harmonize with reason. 

Therefore, the celestial bodies, through their own powers, produce these wonders for the benefit of mortals, and most especially for human beings, since human nature participates in the Divine. Aristotle reports, for instance, in the third book of his *History of Animals* (chapter 20), that on the island of Lemnos, a male goat had two udders near its genitals from which they milked so much that they could make <term>colostrum</term> <note>The first milk produced; here used to suggest a functional, if miraculous, dairy product.</note> from it. When the owner of the flock consulted an oracle about this, he received an answer that his wealth would greatly increase—which was indeed found to be true. If, therefore, portents are given regarding mere livestock, how much more should they be given concerning hu-

[hu]mans; but **St. Thomas** <note>original: "D. First, because these things <note>Meaning omens or supernatural signs.</note> happen in a disorderly fashion, whereas those things produced by nature—as stated in the 2nd and 8th books of the *Physics* <note>Aristotle’s foundational work on the laws of nature.</note>—happen in an orderly way. Moreover, such things are often inanimate or lack an intellect, such as when a beast speaks, or human voices are heard in the air, or something similar. For regarding the first point, it is said that, on the contrary, such things *do* happen orderly, both in terms of <unclear>time</unclear> and <unclear>place</unclear>, and through determined causes, because many **Astrologers** <note>In the author's time, astrology was a serious science involving the study of how the heavens influenced earthly events and politics.</note> know how to predict them. As for the fact that they seem indeterminate to us, this arises from our own ignorance.

Now, celestial bodies, through their virtues and the conjunctions of the stars, perform these and much greater things, because they produce both animals and stupendous effects—as is evident in the properties of stones and herbs—so why not these speech-like sounds as well? This is confirmed because the **Conciliator** <note>original: "Conciliator"; the nickname for Pietro d’Abano (c. 1250–1316), a famous physician and astrologer who sought to reconcile medicine with philosophy.</note>, in his exposition of the 26th problem of the eleventh part—where the problem is "why some speak as soon as they are born"—says that **Haly Eberangel** <note>original: "Haly Eberangel"; likely referring to the Arabic astrologer and physician Ali ibn Ridwan (c. 988–1061).</note> writes this in his book *On Nativities*:

The Ascendant was 8 degrees of **Libra**, in the term of **Mercury**, and within it were **Jupiter**, **Venus**, **Mars**, and **Mercury**. A company of **Astrologers** gathered there, each of whom gave his opinion, but I remained silent. Then the king said to me: 'What is the matter with you? Why do you not speak?'"

To whom I responded, "Give me a period of three days, because if your son survives past the third day, it will be a great miracle concerning him." And when the newborn had completed twenty-four hours of life, he began to speak and to make signs with his hand, and the king was greatly terrified. At this, I said, "It is possible that he may speak some **prophecy** and some miracle." At this, he immediately fell over and died, and it was found out to be just as he said.

It was not the first, because **Haly** <note>Haly Abenragel (d. 1037), a famous Arab astrologer whose work was central to medieval European science</note> through **Astrology** would not have known how to predict what the boy said <note>The author argues that if a spirit were speaking, the astrological charts used by Haly wouldn't have been able to foresee the event so accurately</note>. Therefore, it was from himself—that is, from an internal source—and not through knowledge that he had obtained from any other man. Therefore, it was by the power of the **Intelligences** <note>Celestial spirits that move the planetary spheres</note> and the celestial bodies, which is why such things can happen in other cases as well. 

But if it is argued that "all teaching or learning comes from pre-existing knowledge" <note>A fundamental principle of Aristotelian logic</note>, the reply to this is that this instance is not "teaching" or "learning," nor is it "knowledge" in the proper sense. A sign of this is that these **seers** <note>original: "vates"</note>, when they are no longer in their prophetic frenzy <note>original: "furorem"; a state of divine or celestial inspiration</note>, remember nothing; on the contrary, they deny having said those things, for they are moved by a celestial impulse. This is why **Plato**, in the *Meno* and in many other places, says that seers announce very many true things, yet they [know] nothing of what they speak...

Indeed, many medical treatments were discovered through dreams. However, these facts do not prove the soul is immortal in a simple sense; they only prove that the **Gods** care for lower things. Therefore, they teach and provide for human affairs through signs while awake and through many dreams, as Averroes explains there at length.

...those who are left unpunished or rewarded, while others on the contrary; this is not the subject of our present inquiry. The **Commentator** <note>Averroës (1126–1198), the Andalusian philosopher whose commentaries on Aristotle were foundational to medieval university study</note> touches on many beautiful and difficult points there, which do not serve our current purpose.

Regarding the sixth point concerning **demoniacs** <note>original: *dæmoniacis*; individuals believed to be possessed by demons or spirits</note>, the answer can be made clear through what has already been stated. For all such people are either tormented by **black bile** <note>original: *atra bilis*; one of the four "humors" of ancient medicine, thought to cause melancholy or even prophetic madness when in excess</note>, or they experience **ecstasy** <note>a trance-like state of being "outside oneself"</note>, or they are near death and far removed from human thoughts. Because of this, they become almost like inanimate and irrational things; for this reason, they are able to receive **celestial movements** <note>influences or "impressions" from the stars and planets</note>. They are driven and led by a **frenzied impulse** <note>original: *impetu lymphatico*; a state of madness or divine "frenzy" traditionally associated with water nymphs</note> rather than acting or leading themselves. 

The most evident sign of this is that, as **Plato** says and **Aristotle** agrees, they do not understand what they are saying. Instead, like beasts, they are moved by something else. This is the origin of the proverb that "children and fools prophesy," while the wise and those in control of their faculties are far removed from such things. Nor should anyone wonder at this, since **Aristotle** writes in Book 9 of the *History of Animals*, chapter 31: at the time when the guests of Media died at **Pharsalia** <note>The site of a major battle in Greece; Aristotle notes the behavior of animals during times of war</note>, the **crows** vanished from the regions of Athens and the Peloponnese. It was as if they possessed some sense by which they signaled events to one another and were moved by them. 

For if crows sensed such future events from the heavens, why shouldn't humans—who are little different from crows when they lack the use of their intellect—do the same? Therefore, let the one who objects consider the beasts from which **augurs** <note>ancient Roman diviners who interpreted the will of the gods by observing the flight and behavior of birds</note> take their signs...

<summary>The author argues that prophetic speech in "demoniacs" or fools is not divine, but a result of physical illness (black bile) or celestial influence on the irrational mind, citing Aristotle's observation of crows as evidence.</summary>
<keywords>demoniacs, black bile, melancholy, celestial influence, Aristotle, Plato, prophecy, augury, animal behavior</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the author’s response to the sixth objection regarding "demoniacs" and prophetic frenzy. ...utter their prophecies. That the **art of augury** <note>original: *augurandi ars*; the ancient practice of interpreting omens, such as the flight of birds, to predict the future</note> is not entirely empty is shown by the histories of the Greeks and Romans. **Plato**, in his book *On Laws*, established that in a well-ordered city, the art of augury should not be neglected. **Aristotle** also, in the first book of his *History of Animals* (chapter 16) and the ninth book (chapter 1), mentions several things regarding the art of augury. 

For if birds, and many things lacking reason, are able to indicate future events through **celestial impression** <note>the theory that heavenly bodies physically exert influence on "lower" earthly creatures</note>, why should not humans, who are similar to them, do the same? This is further confirmed by the fact that **Astrologers** could not otherwise judge with such certainty, as we have seen, unless celestial powers impressed themselves upon these lower things. If they sometimes seem to be wrong, it is because they are either unskilled, or they did not correctly calculate the **geniture** <note>original: *genituram*; the astrological chart or configuration of the stars at the moment of a person's birth</note>, or it happens because **free will** <note>original: *libero arbitrio*; the human capacity to choose, which philosophers often argued could override the "fated" influence of the stars</note> dominates the celestial influences.

Regarding the seventh point, however, it is said that **Aristotle** by no means felt that the soul remains after death; rather, he felt the opposite. This is clear enough in the first book of the **Ethics** <note>Aristotle’s *Nicomachean Ethics*</note>, where he says that death neither benefits nor harms the dead, since they are nothing. Instead, such things pertain only to the **reputation** <note>original: *existimationi*; the esteem or "estimation" held by the living</note> that people have of them. This is the same view that **Homer** holds in his mind: for the dead possess only such a state of being.

Regarding the passage in the second book of the **Economics** <note>original: *œconom.*; referring to the *Oeconomics*, a work attributed to Aristotle concerning the management of a household and state</note>, it is said that those women were rewarded by the Gods during their lives; for it is not said there that this happened after death. Or, if it is understood to be after death, this refers to the reputation which...

...is held regarding them, or so that he might lead other women to perform similar works. <note>This sentence fragment concludes a point from the previous page, likely regarding how certain reports of female prophets or "demoniacs" were intended to influence the behavior of other women.</note>

Regarding the eighth and final point, in which it was claimed that impure and wicked men, conscious of their own crimes, assert that souls are mortal—while holy and just men [assert they are immortal]—to this it is said that neither do all impure men universally posit mortality, nor do all temperate men universally posit immortality. For we clearly see many wicked men who believe [in immortality], yet are seduced by their passions; likewise, we know many holy and just men to have posited the mortality of souls. 

For **Plato**, in the first book of the *Republic*, says that **Simonides the Poet** was a divine and excellent man, yet he nonetheless asserted that the soul was mortal. **Homer** also, as **Aristotle** records in the second book of *On the Soul*, thought that the sense did not differ from the intellect <note>If the "intellect" is the same as the physical "senses," it follows that when the body's senses die, the mind must also perish.</note>; and who is ignorant of the great dignity of **Homer**? 

**Hippocrates** and **Galen** also, most learned and excellent men, are held to be of this same opinion. **Alexander of Aphrodisias** <note>A celebrated commentator on Aristotle (c. 200 AD) who argued that the individual human soul does not survive death.</note>, the great **Al-Farabi**, **Abubacher** <note>Abu Bakr ibn Tufayl, an Andalusian Muslim philosopher.</note>, **Avempace** <note>Ibn Bajja, another influential philosopher from Islamic Spain.</note>, and from among our own <note>original: *ex nostratibus*; referring here to the Latin/Roman tradition.</note>, **Pliny the Elder** <note>original: *Plinius Secundus*.</note>, **Seneca**, and countless others have felt this way. 

For **Seneca**, in the seventh book of his *Epistles to Lucilius* (Epistle 54, which begins "bad health had given me a long period of rest") <note>original: *longum mihi comitatum dederat mala valetudo*</note>, and clearly in his *Consolation to Marcia* <note>original: *ad Matiriam*; likely a transcription error for *Marciam*. In this work, Seneca offers Stoic reflections on death.</note>, affirms that the soul itself is mortal; and he counts many other studious and most learned men among those who agree.

<summary>The author refutes the idea that belief in the soul's mortality is a sign of a wicked character, citing a long list of respected "holy and just" thinkers—from Homer and Hippocrates to Islamic and Roman philosophers—who argued that the soul dies with the body.</summary>

...were of the same opinion. And this was because they judged that **virtue** alone was **happiness**, while **vice** was **misery**. They neglected all other goods except insofar as they serve virtue, and they cast away from themselves everything that stood in the way of virtue. 

It must also be considered that many men felt the soul was mortal, yet nevertheless wrote that it was immortal. They did this because of the inclination of men toward evil—those who have little or no intellect and, neither knowing nor loving the goods of the soul, focus only on bodily things. <note>This refers to the "noble lie" or "pious fraud"—the idea that the masses must believe in an afterlife of rewards and punishments to remain moral, even if the philosopher believes death is the end.</note> For this reason, it is necessary to heal such characters, just as a physician behaves toward the sick, and a nurse toward a child lacking reason. 

Through these points (as I think) and others, the common objections can be resolved. For it is commonly said that if the soul is mortal, man ought to surrender himself entirely to **bodily pleasures**, commit all evils for his own benefit, and that it would be in vain to worship **God**, honor divine things, pour out prayers to God, perform sacrifices, and other things of this kind. The response to this is clear enough through what has already been said. 

Since **happiness** is naturally desired and **misery** is fled from, and since (as stated) happiness consists in **virtuous action** while misery consists in **vicious action**, then worshipping God with the whole mind, honoring divine things, pouring out prayers to God, and sacrificing are the most virtuous acts. Therefore, we ought to strive with all our strength for the acquisition of these... <note>The author argues that religious and moral acts are their own reward because they constitute "virtue," which is the only true happiness, regardless of whether the soul survives death.</note>

<meta>This page continues the philosophical argument from page 138 regarding the ethical implications of the soul's mortality. It concludes a point about how true virtue is its own reward, and then begins a new section using Aristotelian biology to explain the hierarchy of living things.</meta>

<page-num>139</page-num>
<header>The Soul.</header>

...[virtuous sacrifices]; on the contrary, however, thefts, robberies, and murders are **vices** that cause a man to pass into a **beast**. Therefore, we ought to abstain from them. You should notice that the person who works diligently, and expects no reward other than **virtue** itself, seems to act far more virtuously and more nobly than he who expects some reward beyond virtue. 

Likewise, the person who flees vice because of the baseness of the vice, and not because of the dread of the punishment owed for that vice, seems more worthy of praise than he who avoids vice out of fear of punishment. This is according to the saying: "the good hate to sin for the love of virtue; the wicked hate to sin for fear of punishment." <note>original: "oderunt peccare boni virtutis amore, oderunt peccare mali formidine pœnæ." This is a famous maxim from the Roman poet Horace, often used in medieval and Renaissance ethics to distinguish between internal and external motivation.</note> Therefore, those who assert that the soul is **mortal** seem to preserve the logic of virtue more perfectly than those who assert it is **immortal**. For the hope of reward and the fear of punishment seem to introduce a certain **servility**—a slave-like mindset—which is contrary to the true principle of virtue. <note>The author argues that doing good just to get into heaven or avoid hell is "servile" (slavish), whereas doing good because it is inherently right is the only truly noble path.</note>

To complete this opinion, it must be known that, just as **Aristotle** teaches in the books *On the Generation of Animals*, nature proceeds step by step and in an orderly fashion. It does not join extreme to extreme immediately, but rather joins an extreme to a middle point. For we see that between small herbs and large trees, shrubs stand in the middle. Between **vegetables** <term>vegetabilia: plants or organisms that only have the power of growth and nutrition</term> and **animals**, there are "immobile living things," such as oysters and others of that kind. <note>This refers to the "Great Chain of Being," a historical concept where all life is ranked in a continuous, unbroken line from the simplest to the most complex.</note> And so it goes, ascending further, as the Blessed **Dionysius** <note>Likely referring to Pseudo-Dionysius the Areopagite, an influential Christian Neoplatonist.</note> states in chapter 7.

<summary>This page argues that believing the soul is mortal fosters a higher form of virtue based on the love of good itself rather than a "servile" fear of punishment. It also introduces the "Great Chain of Being," noting that nature moves in gradual steps from plants to animals.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the philosophical exploration of the human soul's nature, moving from the ethical arguments for mortality on page 139 to a Neoplatonic and Aristotelian description of man as the "middle point" of the universe.</meta>

<page-num>140</page-num>

-> # On Immortality <-

[The author] <note>original: "de diuinis nominibus insinuat" - This refers to the work *On the Divine Names* by Pseudo-Dionysius the Areopagite, a highly influential Christian Neoplatonist philosopher.</note> suggests in *The Divine Names* that **divine wisdom** joins the ends of higher things to the beginnings of lower things. Man, as has been said, is the most **perfect** of animals; therefore, since the human soul <note>original: "anima humana"</note> holds the first place among material things, it is joined with the **immaterial** <note>original: "immaterialia"</note>. It stands as a middle point between the material and the immaterial. 

Indeed, a middle point compared to its extremes is often called one of those extremes. For this reason, when compared to immaterial things, the soul can be called **material**, but in respect to material things, it is called **immaterial**. It does not merely deserve these names, but it actually participates in the properties of those extremes. 

For example, green compared to white is not only called "black," but it also **contracts the sight** <note>In ancient and medieval optics, it was believed that dark colors "gathered" or contracted the visual power of the eye, while light colors expanded it.</note> just as black does, though not as intensely. In the same way, the human soul possesses the properties of the **intelligences** <note>original: "intelligentiarum" - In this period, "intelligences" referred to purely spiritual, immaterial beings like angels or the movers of the celestial spheres.</note> and the properties of all material things. 

Because of this, when the soul performs works that harmonize with the intelligences, it is called **divine** and is said to be "transformed into God." However, when it performs the works of beasts, it is said to be "transformed into beasts." For through malice, a man is called a "serpent" or a "fox"; through cruelty, a "tiger," and so on. Indeed, there is nothing in the world with which man cannot harmonize through some specific property. 

Therefore, it is not without merit that man is called the **Microcosm** <term>Microcosm: from the Greek *mikros kosmos*, meaning "small world." This is the philosophical idea that the human being is a miniature reflection of the entire universe.</term> or "small world." Consequently, some have said that man is a **great miracle**, since he is the whole world, gathered into a single...

<summary>This page describes man as a "middle point" between the physical and spiritual realms, sharing traits with both God and beasts. Because humans can reflect any part of the universe through their actions, the author identifies man as the "Microcosm" or a "great miracle."</summary>

<keywords>Microcosm, human soul, Divine Names, Neoplatonism, hierarchy of being, transformation, intelligences, ethics</keywords>

This page explores the soul's capacity to choose its own nature—rising toward the divine or falling below the level of beasts.</meta>

<page-num>141</page-num>

-> ### The Soul <-

...and is of a changeable nature, since the power has been given to it to follow whichever property of things it prefers. Therefore, the Ancients spoke rightly in their fables <note>original: "apologisauerunt" - referring to the use of 'apologues' or moral fables, such as those of Aesop or Ovid’s Metamorphoses.</note> when they said that some men were turned into gods, some into lions, some into wolves, some into eagles, some into fish, some into plants, some into stones, and so forth; for some men followed the intellect, some the senses, and some the vegetative powers <note>The "vegetative powers" in Aristotelian biology refer to the most basic functions of life, such as growth and nutrition, shared by plants and animals.</note>, and so on. 

Therefore, whoever prefers bodily pleasures to moral or intellectual virtues makes himself more of a beast than a god. Those who love riches more [than virtue] should be called beasts, and the former should be called senseless. Thus, even if the soul were mortal, virtues are not to be spurned nor pleasures pursued, unless someone prefers to be a beast rather than a human, and senseless rather than sensible or knowing. 

One must know, however, that even though man participates in both the material and the immaterial in this way, he is properly said to "participate" in the immaterial because he falls far short of absolute immateriality <note>In medieval philosophy, "participation" implies having a share in a quality that belongs more perfectly to a higher being.</note>. On the other hand, he is not properly said to participate in beasts and plants, but rather to *contain* them within himself. For he stands below immaterial things and above material things; therefore, he cannot reach the perfection of the immaterial. Because of this, [such men] are not called "gods," but rather "holy" or "divine" <note>original: "neque dij appellati sunt, sed diui vel diuini" - A distinction between the essence of God and the reflected divinity found in virtuous humans.</note>.

Indeed, man can not only equal the beasts but even surpass them [in vice]; for some men are far more cruel than any beast. As Aristotle says in the seventh book of the *Ethics*, a bad man is ten thousand times worse <note>original: "Aristotele 7. ethic. malus homo decies millies pe-" - A reference to the *Nicomachean Ethics*, Book VII, where Aristotle argues that while a beast can do great harm, a bad man with the power of reason is infinitely more dangerous.</note>...

<summary>This page argues that because the human soul occupies a middle position in the hierarchy of being, humans have the free will to choose whether they live as divine beings or sink below the level of animals. The author uses classical fables and Aristotelian ethics to demonstrate that living for bodily pleasure or wealth degrades human nature to something worse than a beast.</summary>

## *In which the final conclusion on this matter is set forth,*
## *which seems to me a path that must undoubtedly be upheld.*

### CHAPTER XV.

These things being so, it seems to me (saving a wiser judgment <note>original: "salva saniori sententia" – a standard scholarly formula used to express humility and submission to the higher authority of the Church.</note>) that in this matter it must be said that the question of the <term>immortality of the soul</term> <note>The central debate of this text: whether the soul survives the body or dies with it.</note> is a **neutral problem**, just like the question of the **eternity of the world**. For it seems to me that no <term>natural reasons</term> <note>Arguments based on human logic and observation of nature, rather than on divine revelation or scripture.</note> can be brought forward that compel the conclusion that the soul is immortal, and even less that prove the soul to be mortal, as many Doctors who hold it to be immortal declare. Therefore, I did not wish to provide answers for the other side, since others have done so—especially **Saint Thomas** [Aquinas], clearly, abundantly, and seriously. For this reason, we shall speak as **Plato** did...

<summary>The author concludes that the soul's immortality is a "neutral problem" which cannot be proven or disproven by human reason alone. He acknowledges the work of Thomas Aquinas but suggests that, like the age of the universe, this remains a matter beyond certain philosophical demonstration.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the argument from Chapter XV, transitioning from the limits of natural reason to the necessity of divine revelation to settle the question of the soul's immortality.</meta>

<page-num>143</page-num>

<header>The Soul.</header>

1. Concerning the Laws, to provide certainty about a matter when many are in doubt belongs to God alone. Therefore, since such illustrious men <note>Referring to the great philosophers like Aristotle, Plato, and Averroes who disagreed on this topic.</note> disagree among themselves, I believe this cannot be settled except through God. Moreover, it does not seem fitting or useful for a person to lack such certainty. For if one were in doubt about this, his actions would be uncertain and without any <term>end</term> <note>The "end" (Latin: *finis*) refers to the ultimate goal or purpose of human life. In this context, if we don't know if we are immortal, we don't know what we are living for.</note>; indeed, if the end is unknown, the things that lead to that end must necessarily be unknown. 

Thus, if the soul is immortal, earthly things are to be looked down upon and eternal things are to be pursued. But if it exists as a mortal thing, the opposite path must be followed. If other beings besides man have their own determined ends, how much more so man himself? For man is the most perfect of mortals and—as Plato says in *The Republic*—he alone worships God and justice. 

Therefore, I say that before the gift or coming of grace, God settled this question in many ways through the Prophets and supernatural gifts, as can be clearly seen through the Old Testament. Most recently, however, He clarified this question through His Son, whom He appointed heir of all things and through whom He made the world <note>original: "sæcula" – literally "the ages," a common biblical term for the created universe.</note>, as the Apostle writes in the Epistle to the Hebrews. 

That He is truly the Son of God—true God and true man—the light of the Christian name, Saint Thomas [Aquinas], most clearly and without doubt declares in the first book of *Against the Gentiles*, chapter 6. These are things which John [Duns] Scotus—in everyone’s opinion, and in mine the most subtle <note>original: "subtilissimus" – Scotus was famously known as the "Subtle Doctor" (*Doctor Subtilis*) for his complex reasoning.</note>—and a man in the fir— <note>The text cuts off here at the end of the page.</note>

<header>On Immortality</header>

...[St. Jero]me <note>The text likely refers to Saint Jerome (Hieronymus), whose name ends in "-mus."</note> the most religious, reducing the arguments to the number of eight, enumerates them in the prologue to the *Book of Sentences* <note>The *Sentences* by Peter Lombard was the standard medieval textbook of theology; many commentaries were written on its prologue.</note>. Indeed, those eight points declare this so clearly that no one could deny it unless they were deprived of their mind or were simply stubborn. Since, therefore, He is the true God, He Himself alone is truly the light through which all things are seen, as in John 1; and He alone is also the truth through which all other things are true, as in John 14: "I am the way, the truth, and the life." 

Furthermore, since He has manifested both by word and by deed that the soul is immortal—by His word, indeed, when He threatens the wicked with eternal fire and promises eternal life to the good, for He says: *Come, you blessed of my Father, etc.* and follows with: *Depart, you cursed, into the eternal fire, etc.* <note>Matthew 25:34 and 25:41.</note>; and by His deed when He rose from the dead on the third day. 

Truly, by as much as light differs from the object it illuminates, and truth differs from a thing that is true, and by as much as an infinite cause is more powerful than a finite effect, so much more effectively does this demonstrate the immortality of the soul. Therefore, if any reasons seem to prove the mortality of the soul, they are false and merely apparent, since the First Light and the First Truth show the opposite. If any reasons indeed seem to prove its immortality, they are certainly true and clear, but they are not the Light and the Truth itself. Wherefore this way alone is unshaken and stable, while the others are fluctuating. 

Furthermore, every <term>art</term> <note>In this context, *ars* refers to a systematic branch of knowledge or science, like logic or ethics.</note> must proceed through principles that are proper and appropriate to that art; otherwise, it errs and proceeds unskillfully, as Aristotle says in the first book of the *Posterior Analytics* and in the *Ethics*. But that the soul is immortal is...

He concludes that true knowledge of immortality must come through the proper "art" or discipline of divine revelation rather than mere human logic.</summary>

--- Page 152 ---
<meta>This page continues the argument from page 144, where the author began distinguishing between the fallible logic of philosophers and the absolute certainty provided by divine revelation regarding the soul's immortality.</meta>

<page-num>145</page-num>

<header>The Soul</header>

...[it is an] article of faith, as is clear through the Apostles' and Athanasian <note>The Apostles' Creed and the Athanasian Creed are foundational statements of Christian belief; the latter specifically emphasizes the eternal nature of the soul and the resurrection.</note> Creeds. Therefore, it ought to be proved through the properties of faith. However, the medium upon which faith rests is revelation and Canonical scripture <note>The books of the Bible officially recognized by the Church as authoritative.</note>; only truly and properly through these must it be proved. Other reasons, however, are extraneous, and rest upon a medium that does not prove what is intended. It is no wonder, then, if philosophers disagree among themselves concerning the immortality of the soul, since they rely on arguments extraneous to the conclusion and on fallacies. But all Christians are in agreement when they proceed through proper and infallible means, since these cannot exist except according to one way. 

Furthermore, one who is infirm seeks health, but no one acts as their own physician; because, as it is said in the third book of the *Politics* <note>A reference to Aristotle’s *Politics*, Book 3, where he discusses how people are poor judges of their own cases because they are clouded by emotion.</note>, no one judges rightly in their own affairs when they are in the grip of passions. Let him, therefore, seek another [to guide him]. Now, a good [guide] ought to be skilled in the art and of good character, since neither the first without the second, nor the second without the first, is sufficient. But (as Plato says) just as a <term>distemper</term> <note>An imbalance or lack of proportion in the bodily fluids (humors).</note> in the humors is a sickness of the body, so ignorance is a sickness of the mind. One who is ignorant, therefore, of whether the soul is mortal or not, should seek someone who is knowledgeable and good. Two types of men, however, profess to know this: namely, unbelievers and Christians. Indeed, many of the unbelievers have been most learned men, but almost all were of a stained life—to say nothing of other things, [acting] at least for the sake of empty glory alone...

<summary>The author argues that the soul's immortality is a matter of faith that can only be proven by scripture and revelation, noting that philosophers disagree because they use flawed logic. He compares the search for truth to a sick person seeking a doctor, suggesting that only those who are both learned and virtuous (Christians) can provide the cure for ignorance.</summary>

<keywords>immortality of the soul, articles of faith, Apostles' Creed, Athanasian Creed, Aristotle, Plato, revelation vs reason, spiritual health, philosophy</keywords>

--- Page 153 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion from page 145 regarding the superiority of Christian revelation over pagan philosophy in proving the immortality of the soul. The author is now listing the great Christian thinkers who combined natural reason with divine insight.</meta>

<page-num>146</page-num>

-> # On Immortality <-

...they understood natural things which produce an obscure and weak knowledge. But many **Christians**, unless I am mistaken, knew no less than they did in natural matters—such as **Paul**, **Dionysius**, **Basil**, **Athanasius**, **Origen**, the two **Gregories** (of **Nazianzus** and **Nyssa**), **Augustine**, **Jerome**, **Ambrose**, and so on. <note>These are the "Church Fathers," the influential early Christian theologians whose writings established the foundations of Western and Eastern Christian thought.</note> Besides the fact that they possessed a knowledge of natural things, they also possessed a knowledge of divine things. As **Jerome** says: "The learned **Plato** did not know this, nor the eloquent **Demosthenes**." <note>original: "hoc doctus Plato nescivit, hoc eloquens Demosthenes"</note> Truly, who but a madman would trust those without faith who are so disposed, rather than **Christians** who are so well endowed? 

This gives me firm faith, because **Augustine** (who in my opinion is second to none in learning, for I do not consider him lesser than **Plato** or **Aristotle**), though at first an enemy of the Christian name, became a man of such upright life. At the end of *The City of God* <note>Augustine’s major work, written in the early 5th century, defending Christianity against claims that it caused the fall of Rome.</note>, he writes that he saw so many miracles with his own eyes, which demonstrate an undefiled, inviolable, and most firm faith. 

**Pope Gregory** <note>Gregory the Great (c. 540–604), known for his prolific writings and for strengthening the Papacy.</note> also, who is to be compared with anyone in learning and holiness, brings forward so many and such great examples in his *Dialogues* <note>A 6th-century work focusing on the lives and miracles of Italian saints, intended to prove the soul's survival after death.</note> that all ambiguity is completely removed. Therefore, it must be asserted without doubt that the soul itself is immortal. 

Truly, however, one must not proceed by the path that the "wise" of this world have trod; who, when they called themselves wise, became fools. <note>original: "qui cum sapientes se dixerunt stulti facti sunt"; a reference to the Apostle Paul's letter to the Romans 1:22, often used to critique pagan philosophy.</note> For whoever proceeds by this path, I think, will always catch at the truth uncertainly and erratically. Wherefore I believe that however much...

<summary>The author argues that Christian Church Fathers like Augustine and Jerome are intellectually superior to pagan philosophers because they combined natural reason with divine revelation. He cites the miracles recorded by Augustine and Pope Gregory as definitive proof of the soul's immortality.</summary>

<keywords>immortality of the soul, Church Fathers, Augustine, Jerome, Pope Gregory the Great, Plato, Aristotle, miracles, City of God, Dialogues, faith vs reason</keywords>

--- Page 154 ---
<meta>This page concludes the treatise on the immortality of the soul, transitioning from the critique of ancient philosophers to the final confirmation of Christian doctrine and the author's formal submission to the Church's authority.</meta>

<page-num>147</page-num>

<header>-> ### On Souls <-</header>

...that although Plato wrote so many and such great things excellently concerning the immortality of souls, I nevertheless think he lacked certainty. I conjecture this from the end of the *Apology* <note>Plato’s account of the trial and defense of Socrates (c. 399 BCE).</note>; for there he seems to leave the matter in doubt. In the *Timaeus* <note>A late dialogue by Plato that provides a theoretical map of the universe and the human soul.</note> also, when he was about to speak on this, he said it was enough for him if, in such a difficult matter, he said things that were merely probable <note>original: "verisimilia"; literally "truth-like" or "likely."</note>. Therefore, by comparing all his sayings, it seems to me that he speaks more by offering an opinion than by making a firm assertion. His endeavor was to make citizens good, rather than to make them learned.

But as **St. Thomas** <note>Thomas Aquinas (1225–1274), the preeminent Scholastic theologian who sought to reconcile Aristotelian philosophy with Christian faith.</note> says in the *Secunda Secundae*, question 1, article 3 <note>A section of the *Summa Theologiae* dealing with faith and the necessity of believing truths that exceed human reason.</note>: a moral act can coexist with a false opinion. Yet those walking in the way of the faithful remain stable and unshaken, which is demonstrated by their contempt for riches, honors, pleasures, and all worldly things; and finally by the crown of martyrdom, which they most ardently desired, and having desired it, attained it with the greatest joy.

These, then, are the things which seem to me ought to be said on this matter—always, however, submitting both in this and in other things to the Apostolic See <note>The central authority of the Catholic Church, specifically the Pope.</note>, and so forth.

The end has been reached for this treatise by me, Pietro, son of Giovanni Niccolò Pomponazzi of Mantua <note>Pietro Pomponazzi (1462–1525) was a leading Renaissance philosopher whose work on the soul caused significant controversy for suggesting that immortality could not be proven by reason alone.</note>, on the 24th day of September in the year of Christ 1516, at Bologna, in the 4th year of the Pontificate of Leo X <note>Giovanni de' Medici, who served as Pope from 1513 to 1521.</note>, to the praise of the undivided Trinity, etc.

<image-desc>A faded library ownership stamp is visible at the bottom of the page, featuring an oval border with the word "ROMA" visible in reverse, suggesting a stamp from a Roman institutional library.</image-desc>

<summary>Pietro Pomponazzi concludes his work by arguing that while Plato's views on immortality were mere "probable" opinions meant to foster civic virtue, Christian faith offers an unshakable certainty proven by the martyrs. He formally dates the completion of his treatise to September 1516 in Bologna.</summary>

<keywords>Pietro Pomponazzi, Plato, Apology, Timaeus, immortality of the soul, Thomas Aquinas, martyrdom, Bologna, Pope Leo X, 1516</keywords>
